Fluctuations of the Ising free energy on Erdős-Rényi graphs Thanks: Amin Coja-Oghlan is supported by DFG CO 646/3, DFG CO 646/5 and DFG CO 646/6. Maurice Rolvien is supported by DFG Research Group ADYN (FOR 2975) under DFG grant no. 411362735. Pavel Zakharov is supported by DFG CO 646/6. Kostas Zampetakis is supported by DFG CO 646/5.
Amin Coja-Oghlan, Dominik Kaaser, Maurice Rolvien, Pavel Zakharov, Kostas Zampetakis

Abstract
. We investigate the ferromagnetic Ising model on the Erdős-Rényi random graph 𝔾 ⁡ ( n , m ) \mathbb{G}(n,m) with bounded average degree d = 2 ​ m / n d=2m/n . Specifically, we determine the limiting distribution of log ⁡ Z 𝔾 ⁡ ( n , m ) ​ ( β , B ) \log Z_{\mathbb{G}(n,m)}(\beta,B) , where Z 𝔾 ⁡ ( n , m ) ​ ( β , B ) Z_{\mathbb{G}(n,m)}(\beta,B) is the partition function at inverse temperature β > 0 \beta>0 and external field B ≥ 0 B\geq 0 . If either B > 0 B>0 , or B = 0 B=0 , d > 1 d>1 and β > ath ⁡ ( 1 / d ) \beta>\operatorname{ath}(1/d) the limiting distribution is a Gaussian whose variance is of order Θ ⁡ ( n ) \Theta(n) and is described by a family of stochastic fixed point problems that encode the root magnetisation of two correlated Galton-Watson trees. By contrast, if B = 0 B=0 and either d ≤ 1 d\leq 1 or β < ath ⁡ ( 1 / d ) \beta<\operatorname{ath}(1/d) the limiting distribution is an infinite sum of independent random variables and has bounded variance. [MSc: 05C80, 82B44, 82B20]



1.
Introduction

1.1.
Background and motivation

The Ising model has captivated mathematical physicists for a century
[
35
,
42
]
.
This is partly because the model furnishes perhaps the most elegant example of a non-trivial phase transition
[
53
]
.
Another reason may be that, even though the definition fits in a single line, the model gives rise to profound mathematical challenges, many of which remain unresolved to this day
[
26
]
.
Additionally, the study of the Ising model has had a remarkable impact across several disciplines.
For instance, the Ising model has been seized upon to explain assortative phenomena in statistics and computer science
[
2
,
8
]
.

The original purpose of the Ising model was to demonstrate how ferromagnetism can emerge from the interactions of microscopic particles associated with the vertices of a graph
G=(V(G),E(G))
.
Each of these particles takes one of two spins
\pm 1
.
The edges of the graph prescribe the geometry of interactions.
The model comes with two scalar parameters called the inverse temperature
\beta>0
and the external field
B\geq 0
.
Depending on the graph, phase transitions may or may not occur as
\beta,B
vary.
Indeed, while Ising proved the absence of phase transitions if
G
is the one-dimensional integer ‘lattice’
[
35
]
, a deep line of subsequent work revealed phase transitions on higher dimensional lattices; see Section
2.5
.

The aim of the present contribution is to achieve a more complete understanding of the Ising model on the Erdős-Rényi random graph
\mathbb{G}=\mathbb{G}(n,m)
, a uniformly random graph on
n
vertices with
m
edges.
Specifically, we investigate the regime where the average degree
d=2m/n
, i.e., the average number of interactions per vertex, is bounded, just like in a finite-dimensional lattice.
Since the geometry of interactions and thus the Boltzmann distribution are random, the Ising model on
\mathbb{G}(n,m)
is a specimen of a disordered system, reminiscent of but conceptually simpler than a spin glass
[
48
]
.

In an important contribution Dembo and Montanari
[
23
]
proved that to the leading order the free energy of the Ising model on
\mathbb{G}(n,m)
follows the ‘replica symmetric solution’ predicted by the cavity method, a heuristic from statistical physics
[
49
,
50
]
.
The replica symmetric solution comes in terms of a fixed point problem on the space
\mathcal{P}([-1,1])
of probability measures on the interval
[-1,1]
.
To elaborate, let

\displaystyle Z_{\mathbb{G}}(\beta,B)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\sigma\in\{\pm 1\}^{V(\mathbb{G})}}\exp\left({\beta\sum_{vw\in E(\mathbb{G})}\sigma_{v}\sigma_{w}+B\sum_{v\in V(\mathbb{G})}\sigma_{v}}\right)

be the Ising partition function.
Of course, due to its dependence on the random graph
Z_{\mathbb{G}}(\beta,B)
is a random variable.
Further, consider the
Belief Propagation operator

\displaystyle\mathrm{BP}_{d,\beta,B}

\displaystyle:\mathcal{P}([-1,1])\to\mathcal{P}([-1,1]),

\displaystyle\pi\mapsto\hat{\pi}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{BP}_{d,\beta,B}(\pi),

(1.1)

defined as follows.
Let
\boldsymbol{d}\sim{\rm Po}(d)
be a Poisson variable and let
(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\pi,i})_{i\geq 0}
be a sequence of random variables with distribution
\pi
, all mutually independent and independent of
\boldsymbol{d}
.
Then
\hat{\pi}=\mathrm{BP}_{d,\beta,B}(\pi)
is the distribution of the random variable

\displaystyle\frac{\sum_{s\in\{\pm 1\}}s\mathrm{e}^{sB}\prod_{i=1}^{\boldsymbol{d}}\left({1+s\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\pi,i}\operatorname{th}\beta}\right)}{\sum_{s\in\{\pm 1\}}\mathrm{e}^{sB}\prod_{i=1}^{\boldsymbol{d}}\left({1+s\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\pi,i}\operatorname{th}\beta}\right)}

\displaystyle\in[-1,1].

(1.2)

Write
\mathrm{BP}_{d,\beta,B}^{\ell}
for the
\ell
-fold application of
\mathrm{BP}_{d,\beta,B}
and
\delta_{1}
for the atom on
1
.
Then for any
\beta,B\geq 0
the weak limit

\displaystyle\pi_{d,\beta,B}

\displaystyle=\lim_{\ell\to\infty}\mathrm{BP}^{\ell}_{d,\beta,B}(\delta_{1})

(1.3)

exists.
Finally, define the
Bethe free energy
functional
\mathcal{B}_{d,\beta,\pi}:\mathcal{P}([-1,1])\to\mathbb{R}
by letting

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{d,\beta,B}(\pi)

\displaystyle=\frac{d}{2}\left[{\log\operatorname{ch}\beta-{\mathbb{E}}\log\left({1+\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\pi,1}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\pi,2}\operatorname{th}\beta}\right)}\right]+{\mathbb{E}}\log\left[{\mathrm{e}^{B}\prod_{i=1}^{\boldsymbol{d}}\left({1+\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\pi,i}\operatorname{th}\beta}\right)+\mathrm{e}^{-B}\prod_{i=1}^{\boldsymbol{d}}\left({1-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\pi,i}\operatorname{th}\beta}\right)}\right].

(1.4)

Dembo and Montanari
[
23
, Theorem 2.4]
show that for all
d,\beta,B\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log Z_{\mathbb{G}}(\beta,B)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{B}_{d,\beta,B}(\pi_{d,\beta,B})

(1.5)

in probability.
1
1

1

Actually Dembo and Montanari prove a more general result.
Namely, they show that for any sequence of graphs that converges to a unimodular tree in the topology of local weak convergence the free energy converges to the leading order to the replica symmetric solution associated with the limiting unimodular random tree.
We will come back to this in Section
2.5
.

Due to the normalising factor
1/n
on the l.h.s., the result (
1.5
) determines the free energy only to a first-order, law-of-large-numbers degree of precision.
The contribution of the present work is to establish the precise limiting distribution of
\log Z_{\mathbb{G}}(\beta,B)
.
As we will discover, for most values of the parameters
d,\beta,B
the random variable
\log Z_{\mathbb{G}}(\beta,B)
has variance
\Theta(n)
with Gaussian fluctuations.
We will derive a precise but intricate expression for the variance.
Only in the high temperature phase with no external field (i.e.,
B=0
and
\beta>0
small) do we obtain a non-Gaussian limiting distribution with simple explicit parameters.
In this regime
\log Z_{\mathbb{G}}(\beta,0)
has bounded fluctuations.

1.2.
Results

We proceed to state the main results precisely, beginning with the somewhat simpler case
B>0
where an external field is present.

1.2.1.
The case
B>0

We prove that the (suitably shifted and scaled) free energy converges to a Gaussian whose variance derives from the fixed points of a family of operators on the space
\mathcal{P}([-1,1]^{2})
of probability measures on the square
[-1,1]^{2}
.
To elaborate, for a parameter
t\in[0,1]
define the
coupled Belief Propagation operator

\displaystyle\mathrm{BP}_{d,\beta,B,t}^{\otimes}

\displaystyle:\mathcal{P}([-1,1]^{2})\to\mathcal{P}([-1,1]^{2}),

\displaystyle\pi\mapsto\hat{\pi}=\mathrm{BP}_{d,\beta,B,t}^{\otimes}(\pi)

\displaystyle(t\in[0,1])

(1.6)

as follows.
Let
(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\pi,i}^{(0)})_{i\geq 0}
,
(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\pi,i}^{(1)})_{i\geq 0}
,
(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\pi,i}^{(2)})_{i\geq 0}
with

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\pi,i}^{(0)}

\displaystyle=(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\pi,i,1}^{(0)},\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\pi,i,2}^{(0)}),\kern 5.0pt\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\pi,i}^{(1)}=(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\pi,i,1}^{(1)},\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\pi,i,2}^{(1)}),\kern 5.0pt\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\pi,i}^{(2)}=(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\pi,i,1}^{(2)},\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\pi,i,2}^{(2)})\in[-1,1]^{2}

(1.7)

be sequences of random variables (viz., vectors) with distribution
\pi
.
In addition, let

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{d}_{t}^{(0)}

\displaystyle\sim{\rm Po}(dt),

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{d}_{t}^{(1)}

\displaystyle\sim{\rm Po}(d(1-t)),

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{d}_{t}^{(2)}

\displaystyle\sim{\rm Po}(d(1-t))

(1.8)

be Poisson variables.
All these random variables are mutually independent.
Then
\hat{\pi}=\mathrm{BP}_{d,\beta,B,t}^{\otimes}(\pi)
is defined to be the distribution of the random vector

\displaystyle\left({\frac{\sum_{s\in\{\pm 1\}}s\exp(sB)\prod_{i=1}^{\boldsymbol{d}_{t}^{(0)}}\left({1+s\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\pi,i,h}^{(0)}\operatorname{th}\beta}\right)\prod_{i=1}^{\boldsymbol{d}_{t}^{(h)}}\left({1+s\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\pi,i,h}^{(h)}\operatorname{th}\beta}\right)}{\sum_{s\in\{\pm 1\}}\exp(sB)\prod_{i=1}^{\boldsymbol{d}_{t}^{(0)}}\left({1+s\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\pi,i,h}^{(0)}\operatorname{th}\beta}\right)\prod_{i=1}^{\boldsymbol{d}_{t}^{(h)}}\left({1+s\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\pi,i,h}^{(h)}\operatorname{th}\beta}\right)}}\right)_{h=1,2}\in(-1,1)^{2}\kern 5.0pt.

(1.9)

Write
\mathrm{BP}^{\otimes\,\ell}_{d,\beta,B,t}
for the
\ell
-fold application of the operator
\mathrm{BP}^{\otimes}_{d,\beta,B,t}
and let
\delta_{(1,1)}\in\mathcal{P}([-1,1]^{2})
be the atom on
(1,1)
.
Furthermore, define a functional

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}^{\otimes}_{\beta}(\pi)

\displaystyle:\mathcal{P}([-1,1]^{2})\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0},

\displaystyle\pi

\displaystyle\mapsto{\mathbb{E}}\left[{\prod_{h=1}^{2}\log\left({1+\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\pi,1,h}^{(0)}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\pi,2,h}^{(0)}\operatorname{th}\beta}\right)}\right].

(1.10)

Finally, let us write
\mathcal{N}(M,\Sigma^{2})
for the normal distribution with mean
M
and variance
\Sigma^{2}
.
Then in the case
B>0
we obtain the following central limit theorem.

Theorem 1.1
.

For any
d,\beta,B>0
and
t\in[0,1]
the weak limit

\displaystyle\pi_{d,\beta,B,t}^{\otimes}

\displaystyle=\lim_{\ell\to\infty}\mathrm{BP}^{\otimes\,\ell}_{d,\beta,B,t}(\delta_{(1,1)})

(1.11)

exists and, in distribution,

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{\log Z_{\mathbb{G}}(\beta,B)-{\mathbb{E}}\log Z_{\mathbb{G}}(\beta,B)}{\sqrt{m}}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma(d,\beta,B)^{2}),

where

\displaystyle\Sigma(d,\beta,B)^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\mathcal{B}^{\otimes}_{\beta}(\pi^{\otimes}_{d,\beta,B,t}){\mathrm{d}}t-\mathcal{B}^{\otimes}_{\beta}(\pi^{\otimes}_{d,\beta,B,0})>0.

(1.12)

Naturally, the fact that
\Sigma(d,\beta,B)^{2}
is strictly positive is part of the statement of Theorem
1.1
.
The theorem implies that
\log Z_{\mathbb{G}}(\beta,B)
exhibits fluctuations of order
\Theta(\sqrt{m})
around its expectation.
Moreover, as we will discover in Section
2
the fixed point distributions
\pi_{d,\beta,B,t}^{\otimes}
admit a neat combinatorial interpretation, as does the functional
\mathcal{B}^{\otimes}_{\beta}
.

1.2.2.
The case
B=0

In the absence of an external field (i.e.,
B=0
) the Ising model possesses an inherent symmetry under inversion.
This symmetry causes significant complications, at least if
d>1
, i.e., if the random graph
\mathbb{G}(n,m)
possesses a giant component with high probability.
More precisely, together with (
1.5
) Lyons’ theorem
[
45
, Theorem 1.1]
implies that in the case
B=0
and
d>1
there occurs a phase transition at

\displaystyle\beta_{\mathrm{f}}(d)=\operatorname{ath}(1/d).

(1.13)

Indeed, for
d\leq 1
or
\beta<\beta_{\mathrm{f}}(d)
the distribution
\pi_{d,\beta,0}
from (
1.3
) turns out to be just the atom at zero.
In effect, the functional (
1.4
) simply evaluates to

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{d,\beta,0}(\delta_{0})

\displaystyle=\log 2+\frac{d}{2}\log\operatorname{ch}\beta.

(1.14)

The following theorem pinpoints the limiting distribution of
\log Z_{\mathbb{G}}(\beta,0)
in this ‘high temperature’ regime.

Figure 1.
Plots of
\beta\mapsto\Sigma(d,\beta)^{2}
at
d=1.9
and at external fields
B=0
,
B=0.25
,
B=0.5
and
B=0.75
.
The dashed vertical line marks the critical inverse temperature
\beta_{\mathrm{f}}(1.9)
.

Theorem 1.2
.

Assume that either
d\leq 1
, or
d>1
and
\beta<\beta_{\mathrm{f}}(d)
.
Let
(\boldsymbol{Y}_{\ell})_{\ell\geq 3}
be a sequence of independent random variables with distribution
\boldsymbol{Y}_{\ell}\sim{\rm Po}(d^{\ell}/(2\ell))
.
Then, in distribution,

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\log Z_{\mathbb{G}}(\beta,0)-\left[n\log 2+m\log\operatorname{ch}\beta-\frac{1}{2}\log\left({1-d\operatorname{th}\beta}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell\geq 3}\left({\boldsymbol{Y}_{\ell}\log\left({1+\operatorname{th}^{\ell}\beta}\right)-\frac{d^{\ell}}{2\ell}\operatorname{th}^{\ell}\beta}\right)-\frac{d}{2}\operatorname{th}\beta-\frac{d^{2}}{4}\operatorname{th}^{2}\beta.

(1.15)

Since the sum
\sum_{\ell\geq 3}\left({\boldsymbol{Y}_{\ell}\log\left({1+\operatorname{th}^{\ell}\beta}\right)-\frac{d^{\ell}}{2\ell}\operatorname{th}^{\ell}\beta}\right)
converges in
L^{2}
, Theorem
1.2
implies that the limiting distribution on the r.h.s. of (
1.15
) has variance

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell\geq 3}\frac{d^{\ell}}{2\ell}\log^{2}(1+\operatorname{th}^{\ell}\beta)<\infty.

Thus, the random variable
\log Z_{\mathbb{G}}(\beta,0)
superconcentrates, i.e., has
bounded
fluctuations.

For
d>1
and
\beta>\beta_{\mathrm{f}}(d)
, i.e., in the ‘low temperature regime’, the Ising model behaves fundamentally differently
[
23
,
45
]
.
In particular, the limiting distribution (
1.3
) is no longer just a point mass and

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{d,\beta,0}(\pi_{d,\beta,0})>\log 2+\frac{d}{2}\log\operatorname{ch}\beta.

Furthermore, the next theorem implies that for almost all
\beta>\beta_{\mathrm{f}}(d)
the free energy
\log Z_{\mathbb{G}}(\beta,0)
has a Gaussian limit with fluctuations of order
\Theta(\sqrt{m})
, as in Theorem
1.1
.

To state the theorem, we introduce

\displaystyle r_{d,\beta}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}{\mathbb{E}}\left[{\frac{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\pi_{d,\beta,0},1}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\pi_{d,\beta,0},2}+\operatorname{th}\beta}{1+\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\pi_{d,\beta,0},1}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\pi_{d,\beta,0},2}\operatorname{th}\beta}}\right]

\displaystyle(\mbox{cf.\ \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{Basak}{}{}, Eq.~(1.8)]} and \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{Dembo_2010}{}{}, Eq.~(6.11)]}}).

(1.16)

The function
\beta\mapsto r_{d,\beta}
is bounded, monotonically increasing and right-continuous for any
d>0

[
11
, Theorem 1.6]
.
Hence, the set
\mathfrak{X}_{d}
of
\beta\in(0,\infty)
where
\beta\mapsto r_{d,\beta}
fails to be continuous is (at most) countable.

Figure 2.
Plot of
\beta\mapsto{r_{d,\beta}}
for
d=1
,
d=3
and
d=5
.

Theorem 1.3
.

Let
d>1
and
\beta\in(\beta_{\mathrm{f}}(d),\infty)\setminus\mathfrak{X}_{d}
.
For all
t\in[0,1]
the weak limit

\displaystyle\pi_{d,\beta,0,t}^{\otimes}

\displaystyle=\lim_{\ell\to\infty}\mathrm{BP}^{\otimes\,\ell}_{d,\beta,0,t}(\delta_{1})

(1.17)

exists and in distribution,

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{\log Z_{\mathbb{G}}(\beta,0)-{\mathbb{E}}\log Z_{\mathbb{G}}(\beta,0)}{\sqrt{m}}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma(d,\beta)^{2}),

where

\displaystyle\Sigma(d,\beta)^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\mathcal{B}^{\otimes}_{\beta}(\pi^{\otimes}_{d,\beta,0,t}){\mathrm{d}}t-\mathcal{B}^{\otimes}_{\beta}(\pi^{\otimes}_{d,\beta,0,0})>0.

Despite their daunting appearance the variance formulas from Theorems
1.1
and
1.3
can actually be evaluated numerically with reasonable efficiency.
This is because the fixed point iterations (
1.11
) and (
1.17
) turn out to converge geometrically.
Figure
1
displays the variances
\Sigma(d,\beta)^{2}
for some instructive parameter values.

Theorem
1.3
is syntactically similar to Theorem
1.1
but for the appearance of the countable ‘exceptional’ set
\mathfrak{X}_{d}
of discontinuities of the function
\beta\mapsto r_{d,\beta}
from (
1.16
) where we do not know that convergence to a Gaussian limit occurs.
Actually for all we know the exceptional set may be empty, i.e., the function
\beta\mapsto r_{d,\beta}
may be continuous for all
d>1
; Figure
2
displays
\beta\mapsto r_{d,\beta}
for a few values of
d
.
But of course a proof (or refutation) of this hypothesis remains an open problem.

Figure 3.
The distributions
\pi_{d,\beta,0,t}^{\otimes}
for
d=1.9
,
\beta=0.65
, and
t=0.1,0.5,0.9
.
The atom at
(0,0)
has been removed to improve visibility.

Additionally, the heat map representation in Figure
3
shows the distributions
\pi_{d,\beta,0,t}
for three increasing values of
t
.
The figure shows how the probability mass gravitates towards the diagonal as
t
increases.
Roughly speaking, as
t
increases the two coordinates of the random pair

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}^{(0)}_{\pi_{d,\beta,B,t},1}=(\boldsymbol{\mu}^{(0)}_{\pi_{d,\beta,B,t},1,1},\boldsymbol{\mu}^{(0)}_{\pi_{d,\beta,B,t},1,2})

(1.18)

become more ‘aligned’.
For instance, for
t=0
the Poisson variables from (
1.8
) satisfy
\boldsymbol{d}_{t}^{(0)}=0
while
\boldsymbol{d}_{t}^{(1)},\boldsymbol{d}_{t}^{(2)}
are independent
{\rm Po}(d)
variables.
Consequently, comparing the definition (
1.9
) of the operator
\mathrm{BP}_{d,\beta,B,0}^{\otimes}
with the definition (
1.2
) of
\mathrm{BP}_{d,\beta,B}
, we see that the coordinates of the pair (
1.18
) are just two independent random variables with distribution
\pi_{d,\beta,B}
.
On the other extreme, for
t=1
we have
\boldsymbol{d}_{t}^{(1)}=\boldsymbol{d}_{t}^{(2)}=0
while
\boldsymbol{d}_{t}^{(0)}
is a
{\rm Po}(d)
variable.
Hence, the operator (
1.9
) acts identically on both coordinates, which means that the two coordinates of (
1.18
) are identical and have distribution
\pi_{d,\beta,B}
.
Naturally, for values
0<t<1
the distribution
\pi_{d,\beta,B,t}^{\otimes}
interpolates between these two extremes.
Let us make a quick note of these observations for later reference.

Remark 1.4
.

The distribution
\pi_{d,\beta,B,0}^{\otimes}
is the product measure
\pi_{d,\beta,B}\otimes\pi_{d,\beta,B}
, i.e., the two coordinates of (
1.18
) are independent.
By contrast,
\pi_{d,\beta,B,0}^{\otimes}
is the distribution of an identical pair
(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\pi_{d,\beta,B},1},\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\pi_{d,\beta,B},1})
, i.e., the two coordinates of (
1.18
) are identical.

1.3.
Notation

We denote the vertex set of the random graph
\mathbb{G}(n,m)
by
V_{n}=\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}\}
.
Throughout the paper asymptotic notation such as
o(\,\cdot\,),O(\,\cdot\,)
refers to the limit
n\to\infty
.
The random graph
\mathbb{G}(n,m)
enjoys a property
\mathfrak{P}

with high probability
(‘w.h.p. ’) if the probability that
\mathfrak{P}
occurs converges to one as
n\to\infty
.
We continue to assume tacitly that
m=m(n)
is a sequence such that
\lim_{n\to\infty}2m/n=d
for a fixed real
d>0
.
All statements concerning the random graph are understood to hold for
any
sequence
m=m(n)
with
\lim_{n\to\infty}2m/n=d
.

For a graph
G=(V,E)
and a vertex
v\in V
we denote by
\partial(G,v)
the set of all neighbours of
v
.
By extension, for an integer
\ell\geq 0
we let
\partial^{\ell}(G,v)
be the set of all vertices at (shortest path) distance precisely
\ell
from
v
.
A
rooted graph
is a graph
G=(V,E)
together with a distinguished root vertex
r\in V
.
For a vertex
v
of a graph
G
we let
\nabla^{\ell}(\mathbb{G},v)
be the subgraph of
G
induced by all vertices
u
at distance at most
\ell
from
v
, rooted at
v
.
Two rooted graphs
(G,r)
and
(G^{\prime},r^{\prime})
are
isomorphic
if there exists a graph-theoretic isomorphism
G\to G^{\prime}
that maps
r
to
r^{\prime}
.

Let
G=(V,E)
be a graph.
For a vector
\sigma\in\{\pm 1\}^{V(G)}
and a subset
U\subseteq V
we write

\displaystyle\sigma(U)

\displaystyle=\prod_{u\in U}\sigma(u).

In particular, for an edge
e=\{v,w\}\in E
we have
\sigma(e)=\sigma(v)\sigma(w)
.
The
Ising model on
G
at inverse temperature
\beta
and external field
B
is the probability distribution on
\{\pm 1\}^{V(G)}
defined by

\displaystyle\mu_{G,\beta,B}(\sigma)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{Z_{G}(\beta,B)}\exp\left({\beta\sum_{e\in E(G)}\sigma(e)+B\sum_{v\in V(G)}\sigma(v)}\right)

\displaystyle(\sigma\in\{\pm 1\}^{V(G)}),\qquad\mbox{ where}

(1.19)

\displaystyle Z_{G}(\beta,B)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\tau\in\{\pm 1\}^{V(G)}}\exp\left({\beta\sum_{e\in E(G)}\tau(e)+B\sum_{v\in V(G)}\tau(v)}\right).

(1.20)

Additionally, for a graph
G
rooted at a vertex
r\in V(G)
and an integer
\ell\geq 0
we will be led to consider the conditional distribution

\displaystyle\mu_{G,r,\beta,B}^{(\ell)}(\sigma)

\displaystyle=\mu_{G,\beta,B}\left({\sigma\mid\{\forall v\in\partial^{\ell}(G,r):\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v)=1\}}\right)

\displaystyle(\sigma\in\{\pm 1\}^{V(G)})

(1.21)

given that all vertices at distance precisely
\ell
from the root
r
carry spin
1
.

For a finite set
\Omega\neq\emptyset
we let
\mathcal{P}(\Omega)
denote the set of all probability distributions on
\Omega
.
For
\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\Omega)
we let
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\mu}\in\Omega
denote a random element of
\Omega
with distribution
\mu
; if and where the reference to
\mu
is self-evident, we just write
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
.
Moreover, for a function
X:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
we write

\displaystyle\left\langle{{X(\boldsymbol{\sigma})},{\mu}}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{\sigma\in\Omega}X(\sigma)\mu(\sigma)

for the expectation of
X(\boldsymbol{\sigma})
.
Further, if
\mathfrak{E}\subseteq\Omega
is an event with
\mu(\mathfrak{E})>0
, then
\mu(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathfrak{E})\in\mathcal{P}(\Omega)
signifies the conditional distribution given
\mathfrak{E}
.

For
\ell\geq 1
and probability measures
\mu,\nu
on
[-1,1]^{\ell}
we denote by
W_{1}(\mu,\nu)
their
L^{1}
-Wasserstein distance
[
58
]
.
Thus, with
\Gamma(\mu,\nu)
the set of all coupling of
\mu,\nu
, we have

\displaystyle W_{1}(\mu,\nu)

\displaystyle=\inf_{\gamma\in\Gamma(\mu,\nu)}\int_{[-1,1]^{\ell}\times[-1,1]^{\ell}}\left\|{x-y}\right\|_{1}{\mathrm{d}}\gamma(x,y).

We recall that the topology induced by
W_{1}(\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,)
coincides with the topology of weak convergence
[
59
, Theorem 6.9]
.

From here on we assume tacitly that
d,\beta>0
and
B\geq 0
.
Unless specified otherwise all statements are understood to hold for any such
d,\beta,B
.

2.
Overview

The basic idea behind the proofs of the central limit theorems Theorems
1.1
and
1.3
is to consider a family of correlated random graphs.
The degree of correlation is gauged by a coupling parameter
t\in[0,1]
, with
t=1
meaning that the two random graphs are identical and
t=0
corresponding to two independent random graphs.
By interpolating from
t=0
to
t=1
we will be able to get a handle on the variance of
\log Z_{\mathbb{G}}(\beta,B)
.
To obtain the central limit theorems we will ultimately combine the analysis of the variance with an off-the-shelf martingale central limit theorem.

A broadly similar proof strategy was recently used to show that the partition function of the random 2-SAT model (i.e., the number of satisfying assignments) has a central limit theorem throughout the entire regime of clause/variable densities where satisfying assignments likely exist
[
14
]
.
The fundamental difference between that proof and the present contribution is that the random 2-SAT problem enjoys a very strong spatial mixing property called ‘Gibbs uniqueness’ throughout the satisfiable regime
[
1
]
.
The Gibbs uniqueness property constitutes the linchpin of the proofs from
[
14
]
.
By contrast, the Ising model on the random graph does not have the Gibbs uniqueness property for most interesting parameter regimes
[
17
,
23
]
.
The most egregious counterexample is the case
B=0
,
d>1
and
\beta>\beta_{\mathrm{f}}(d)
, i.e., low temperature and no external field.
As Proposition
2.15
below demonstrates, in this case two inverse ‘pure states’ (in the sense of
[
47
]
) coexist w.h.p., a scenario that amounts to pretty much the opposite of Gibbs uniqueness.
Additionally, the Belief Propagation operator has a spurious (and unstable) fixed point at
\delta_{0}
.

To deal with the challenge posed by the absence of Gibbs uniqueness, we will combine insights on the Ising model from prior work
[
11
,
23
]
with the positive correlation of spins under the Ising measure; see Lemma
3.2
below.
In the case
B>0
these ingredients pretty much suffice to investigate the variance and thus to establish the central limit theorem.
By contrast, in the
B=0
,
d>1
and
\beta>\beta_{\mathrm{f}}(d)
case substantial extra work is required.
In this parameter regime we need to get a precise handle on the two inverse pure states of the model, which is precisely what the aforementioned Proposition
2.15
delivers.
Actually the proposition plugs a significant gap in the understanding of the Ising model on the Erdős-Rényi graph and should thus be of independent interest.
Apart from insights from prior work on the Ising model, the proof of Proposition
2.15
combines techniques from work on diluted spin glass models from
[
7
,
18
,
19
,
20
]
with delicate correlation and monotonicity arguments.
With the pure state decomposition in place, an analysis involving careful conditioning then provides sufficient control of the variance to invoke the martingale central limit theorem and to prove Theorem
1.3
.
Finally, the proof of Theorem
1.2
rests on a totally different set of techniques.
Here we combine a truncated second moment argument with the small subgraph conditioning method that was originally developed towards the Hamilton cycle problem on random graphs
[
57
]
.

The purpose of this section is to flesh out the proof strategy in greater detail.
Along the way we will discover the combinatorics behind the operator (
1.6
) and the functional (
1.10
).
But first we need to recall the workings of the classical Belief Propagation operator (
1.1
) that leads to the ‘replica symmetric solution’ (
1.5
), because Belief Propagation will play a vital role towards the proofs of Theorems
1.1
and
1.3
as well.
Subsequently we will survey the strategy behind the proofs of Theorems
1.1
and
1.3
, including the pure state decomposition, as well as of Theorem
1.2
.
Finally, Section
2.5
contains a detailed discussion of further related work.

2.1.
First order approximation: the replica symmetric solution

As a preparation for the proofs of Theorems
1.1
and
1.3
we need to appraise ourselves of the workings of the proof of first order formula (
1.5
) for the free energy and the role that Belief Propagation plays in this proof.
As a key step toward (
1.5
) Dembo and Montanari show that in the case
B>0
the distribution
\pi_{d,\beta,B}
from (
1.3
) is the limit of the empirical distribution of the vertex magnetisations on the random graph.
To elaborate, the
magnetisation
of a vertex
v_{i}
in
\mathbb{G}
is defined as

\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v_{i})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G},\beta,B}}}\right\rangle=\mu_{\mathbb{G},\beta,B}(\{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v_{i})=1\})-\mu_{\mathbb{G},\beta,B}(\{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v_{i})=-1\}).

Accordingly, the empirical distribution of the vertex magnetisations reads

\displaystyle\pi_{\mathbb{G},\beta,B}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v_{i})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G},\beta,B}}}\right\rangle}\in\mathcal{P}([-1,1]).

(2.1)

Of course, due to its dependence on the random graph
\mathbb{G}
,
\pi_{\mathbb{G},\beta,B}
is a
random
probability distribution.
The following proposition establishes that
\pi_{\mathbb{G},\beta,B}
converges in probability to the limit
\pi_{d,\beta,B}
from (
1.3
) if
B>0
.

Proposition 2.1

(
[
23
, Theorems 2.5 and 2.7]
)
.

Suppose
B>0
.
Then

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}[W_{1}(\pi_{\mathbb{G},\beta,B},\pi_{d,\beta,B})]

\displaystyle=o(1)

and

(2.2)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\left|{\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v_{1})\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v_{2})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G},\beta,B}}}\right\rangle-\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v_{1})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G},\beta,B}}}\right\rangle\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v_{2})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G},\beta,B}}}\right\rangle}\right|

\displaystyle=o(1).

(2.3)

Proposition
2.1
does not apply to the case
B=0
(no external field), which is due to the inversion symmetry
\mu_{\mathbb{G},\beta,0}(\sigma)=\mu_{\mathbb{G},\beta,0}(-\sigma)
of the Ising model in this case.
We will revisit this delicate issue in Section
2.3
below.

To grasp the link between the empirical magnetisations
\pi_{\mathbb{G},\beta,B}
and the distribution
\pi_{d,\beta,B}
we need to remind ourselves of two facts.
First, that the local geometry of the random graph
\mathbb{G}
is described by a Galton-Watson process.
Second, that Belief Propagation is ‘exact on trees’.

On the first point, let
\mathbb{T}=\mathbb{T}(d)
be the (possibly infinite) Galton-Watson tree with root
\varrho
where every vertex begets a
{\rm Po}(d)
number of children.
Moreover, let
\mathbb{T}^{(\ell)}
be the (finite) tree obtained from
\mathbb{T}
by deleting all vertices whose distance from
\varrho
exceeds
\ell
.
The following well-known lemma states precisely how
\mathbb{T}
captures the local geometry of
\mathbb{G}
.
Recall that
\nabla^{\ell}(\mathbb{G},v)
is the subgraph of
\mathbb{G}
induced on the vertices
u
at distance at most
\ell
from
v
, rooted at
v
.

Lemma 2.2

(e.g.
[
22
, Proposition 2.6]
)
.

For any integer
\ell\geq 0
and any rooted tree
T
we have

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{1}\left\{{\nabla^{\ell}(\mathbb{G},v_{i})\cong T}\right\}

\displaystyle={\mathbb{P}}\left[{\mathbb{T}^{(\ell)}\cong T}\right]

\displaystyle\mbox{in probability}.

Moving on to Belief Propagation, consider a tree
T
rooted at
\varrho
and recall that
\mu_{T,\varrho,\beta,B}^{(\ell)}
is the conditional distribution from (
1.21
) where we impose a
+1
‘boundary condition’ on all vertices at distance precisely
\ell
from
\varrho
.
For a vertex
v\in V(T)
let
T_{v}
be the sub-tree of
T
comprising
v
and its successors; in other words,
T_{v}
contains all vertices
w
such that the path from
w
to
\varrho
in
T
passes through
v
.
The root of
T_{v}
is vertex
v
itself.
In the case of the Ising model, Belief Propagation boils down to the following simple recurrence.

Lemma 2.3
.

For any
B\geq 0
, any tree
T
rooted at
\varrho
and any
\ell\geq 1
we have

\displaystyle\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\varrho)},{\mu_{T,\beta,B}^{(\ell)}}}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{\sum_{s\in\{\pm 1\}}s\mathrm{e}^{sB}\prod_{v\in\partial(T,\varrho)}\left({1+s\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v)},{\mu_{T_{v},\beta,B}^{(\ell-1)}}}\right\rangle\operatorname{th}\beta}\right)}{\sum_{s\in\{\pm 1\}}\mathrm{e}^{sB}\prod_{v\in\partial(T,\varrho)}\left({1+s\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v)},{\mu_{T_{v},\beta,B}^{(\ell-1)}}}\right\rangle\operatorname{th}\beta}\right)}\kern 5.0pt.

(2.4)

The upshot of Lemma
2.3
is that we can work out the root magnetisation
\langle{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\varrho)},{\mu_{T,\beta,B}^{(\ell)}}\rangle
of
T
subject to the all-ones boundary condition ‘bottom up’ from the root magnetisations of the sub-trees
T_{v}
,
v\in\partial(T,\varrho)
.
The recurrence (
2.4
) can be verified directly in a few lines (see Section
4.1
).
Alternatively, Lemma
2.3
could be derived easily from general statements about Belief Propagation
[
10
,
48
]
.

Lemma
2.3
clarifies the combinatorial meaning of the operator
\mathrm{BP}_{d,\beta,B}
from (
1.1
).
Indeed, due to the recursive nature of the Galton-Watson tree
\mathbb{T}
, the root has a
{\rm Po}(d)
number of children.
Moreover, for each child
v\in\partial(\mathbb{T},\varrho)
the tree
\mathbb{T}_{v}
is again nothing but a Galton-Watson tree with offspring distribution
{\rm Po}(d)
.
Thus, (
1.2
) matches the recurrence (
2.4
) on the random tree
\mathbb{T}
.
Consequently, we obtain the following immediate consequence of Lemma
2.3
.

Corollary 2.4
.

For any
B\geq 0
and any
\ell\geq 0
, the random variable
\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\varrho)},{\mu_{\mathbb{T},\beta,B}^{(\ell)}}\rangle}
has distribution

\displaystyle\pi_{d,\beta,B}^{(\ell)}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{BP}_{d,\beta,B}^{\ell}(\delta_{1}).

(2.5)

Hence,
\pi_{d,\beta,B}
is nothing but the limiting distribution of the root magnetisations
\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\varrho)},{\mu_{\mathbb{T},\beta,B}^{(\ell)}}\rangle}
as
\ell\to\infty
.

In summary, Eq. (
2.2
) from Proposition
2.1
shows that the empirical distribution
\pi_{\mathbb{G},\beta,B}
on the random graph converges to the same limit as the root magnetisation of the random tree
\mathbb{T}^{(\ell)}
that describes the local geometry of
\mathbb{G}
subject to the all-ones boundary condition at distance
\ell
from the root.
The fact that the all-ones configuration supplies the ‘correct’ starting point is non-trivial and one of the key facts established in
[
23
]
.
Further, a moment’s reflection shows that for
B>0
the limiting distribution
\pi_{d,\beta,B}
is not just an atom.
Rather, the distribution
\pi_{d,\beta,B}
reflects the inherent randomness of the local geometry of
\mathbb{G}
around its vertices
v_{i}
.
This fact, to which the Ising model on the Erdős-Rényi graph owes much of its mathematical depth, sets the Erdős-Rényi graph apart from other graph classes such as integer lattices or random regular graphs
[
24
]
.

The second statement (
2.3
) of Proposition
2.3
shows that w.h.p. the correlations between the spins of different vertices are benign.
Indeed, because the Erdős-Rényi random graph model is invariant under vertex permutations, the statement (
2.3
) is equivalent to

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}{\mathbb{E}}\left|{\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v_{i})\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v_{j})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G},\beta,B}}}\right\rangle-\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v_{i})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G},\beta,B}}}\right\rangle\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v_{j})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G},\beta,B}}}\right\rangle}\right|

\displaystyle=o(1).

(2.6)

In other words, for ‘most’ vertex pairs
(v_{i},v_{j})
the spins
\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v_{i}),\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v_{j})
are asymptotically independent, a property known as ‘replica symmetry’ in cavity method parlance
[
41
]
.

Given replica symmetry and the distribution of the magnetisations, the Bethe free energy (
1.4
) is generally known to provide a first order approximation to
\log Z_{\mathbb{G}}(\beta,B)

[
20
]
.
This can be verified by a coupling argument called the Aizenman-Sims-Starr scheme
[
4
]
.
The basic idea behind this technique is to reduce the calculation of the free energy to the careful computation of the effect on the partition function of a sequence of local changes to the graph, such as additions of single edges.
In combination with knowledge of the distribution of the magnetisations (
2.2
) the replica symmetry condition (
2.6
) suffices to compute the impact of such local changes accurately.

That said, the proof of (
1.5
) in
[
23
]
happens to employ a somewhat different strategy.
Instead of the Aizenman-Sims-Starr scheme, Dembo and Montanari interpolate on the inverse temperature parameter
\beta
.
However, the Aizenman-Sim-Starr scheme provides the inspiration for the variance computation that we will perform towards Theorems
1.1
and
1.3
.

2.2.
The central limit theorem in the case
B>0

To prove the central limit theorems (Theorems
1.1
and
1.3
) we develop a refined version of the Belief Propagation formalism that captures the fluctuations of
\log Z_{\mathbb{G}}(\beta,B)
.
In the case
B>0
we can directly use some of the aforementioned ingredients of the proof of (
1.5
), particularly Proposition
2.1
.
However, as we will see in Section
2.3
below, the case
B=0
turns out to be more delicate.
The proofs for the following statements can be found in Section
4
, where we prove Lemma
2.3
, Proposition
2.5
, Lemma
2.7
, Lemma
2.8
, Proposition
2.9
, Lemma
2.11
and Propositions
2.10
,
2.12
and
2.13
.
Some of the intermediate statements in this subsection actually also hold for
B=0
, in which case we will be able to reuse them in Section
2.3
towards the proof of Theorem
1.3
.

We are going to derive Theorem
1.1
from a generic martingale central limit theorem.
The main challenge will be to get a handle on the resulting variance process.
The martingale that we use is the well-known edge exposure martingale
[
6
, Chapter 7]
.
Hence, we think of the random graph
\mathbb{G}=\mathbb{G}(n,m)
as being obtained by picking one random edge at a time without replacement.
Thus, let
\boldsymbol{e}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{e}_{m}
be a uniformly random
sequence
of pairwise distinct edges and let
E(\mathbb{G}(n,m))=\{\boldsymbol{e}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{e}_{m}\}
be the edge set of the random graph.
Furthermore, let
\mathfrak{F}_{n,M}
be the
\sigma
-algebra generated by the first
M
edges
\boldsymbol{e}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{e}_{M}
.
Hence, given
\mathfrak{F}_{n,M}
we know
\boldsymbol{e}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{e}_{M}
, while
\boldsymbol{e}_{M+1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{e}_{m}
remain unexposed, viz. random (albeit not quite independent of
\boldsymbol{e}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{e}_{M}
because edges are chosen without replacement).
Let

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Z}_{n,M}

\displaystyle=m^{-1/2}{\mathbb{E}}\left[{\log Z_{\mathbb{G}}(\beta,B)\mid\mathfrak{F}_{n,M}}\right],

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{X}_{n,M}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{Z}_{n,M}-\boldsymbol{Z}_{n,M-1}.

(2.7)

Then
(\boldsymbol{Z}_{n,M})_{0\leq M\leq m}
is a Doob martingale and
\boldsymbol{X}_{n,M}
is the associated sequence of martingale differences.
The following is an easy consequence of a general martingale central limit theorem from
[
32
]
.

Proposition 2.5
.

Let
B\geq 0
.
If there exists a real
\eta=\eta(d,\beta,B)>0
such that

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}\left|{\sum_{M=1}^{m}\boldsymbol{X}_{n,M}^{2}-\eta^{2}}\right|

\displaystyle=0

(2.8)

then
\lim_{n\to\infty}\boldsymbol{Z}_{n,m}-\boldsymbol{Z}_{n,0}=\mathcal{N}(0,\eta^{2})
in distribution
.

Thus, Proposition
2.5
reduces the proof of Theorem
1.1
to the analysis of the variance process
(\boldsymbol{X}_{n,M}^{2})_{1\leq M\leq m}
.
To investigate the variance process we adapt a technique originally developed in
[
14
]
to study the random 2-SAT problem to the present setting of the Ising model on random graphs.
Namely, we will express
\boldsymbol{X}_{n,M}^{2}
in terms of a family of coupled random graphs.
Specifically, we consider three pairs of random graphs

\displaystyle(\mathbb{G}_{h,M}(n,m))_{h=1,2},

\displaystyle(\mathbb{G}_{h,M}^{-}(n,m))_{h=1,2},

\displaystyle(\mathbb{G}_{h,M}^{+}(n,m))_{h=1,2}

(2.9)

that each have a certain number of edges in common and additionally contain a number of independent random edges.
The six random graphs are generated via the following, admittedly mildly subtle coupling.
Let
0\leq M\leq m
and let us assume that
n>n_{0}(d)
exceeds a large enough number
n_{0}(d)
that depends on
d
only.

CPL0:

recall that
(\boldsymbol{e}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{e}_{m})
signifies the edge sequence of the random graph
\mathbb{G}=\mathbb{G}(n,m)
.

CPL1:

independently for
h=1,2
choose an independent sequence
(\boldsymbol{e}_{h,M,1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{e}_{h,M,m-M+1})
of pairwise distinct edges uniformly such that

\displaystyle\{\boldsymbol{e}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{e}_{M-1}\}\cap\bigcup_{1\leq h\leq 2}\{\boldsymbol{e}_{h,1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{e}_{h,M,m-M+1}\}

\displaystyle=\emptyset

(2.10)

and let

\displaystyle\mathbb{G}_{h,M}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{G}_{h,M}(n,m)=(V_{n},\{\boldsymbol{e}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{e}_{M-1},\boldsymbol{e}_{h,M,1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{e}_{h,M,m-M}\}),

\displaystyle\mathbb{G}^{-}_{h,M}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{G}_{h,M}^{-}(n,m)=(V_{n},\{\boldsymbol{e}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{e}_{M-1},\boldsymbol{e}_{h,M,1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{e}_{h,M,m-M+1}\}).

CPL2:

independently for
h=1,2
choose an edge
\boldsymbol{e}_{h,M}^{+}\not\in\{\boldsymbol{e}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{e}_{M},\boldsymbol{e}_{h,M,1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{e}_{h,M,m-M}\}
uniformly and let

\displaystyle\mathbb{G}_{h,M}^{+}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}(V_{n},\{\boldsymbol{e}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{e}_{M},\boldsymbol{e}_{h,M,1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{e}_{h,M,m-M}\})&\mbox{ if }\boldsymbol{e}_{M}\not\in\{\boldsymbol{e}_{h,M,1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{e}_{h,M,m-M}\},\\
(V_{n},\{\boldsymbol{e}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{e}_{M},\boldsymbol{e}_{h,M,1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{e}_{h,M,m-M},\boldsymbol{e}_{h,M}^{+}\})&\mbox{ otherwise}.\end{cases}

Remark 2.6
.

The random graphs
\mathbb{G}
and
(\mathbb{G}_{h,M},\mathbb{G}^{-}_{h,M},\mathbb{G}^{+}_{h,M})_{1\leq h\leq 2,\,0\leq M\leq m}
are defined on the same probability space.
The edge sequences
(\boldsymbol{e}_{h,M,1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{e}_{h,M,m-M+1})_{h=1,2}
and the edges
\boldsymbol{e}_{h,M}^{+}
are understood to be stochastically independent for different values of
M
.
But of course the sequence
\boldsymbol{e}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{e}_{m}
is independent of and thus identical for all
M
.

By construction, the two random graphs
\mathbb{G}_{1,M},\mathbb{G}_{2,M}
have
M-1
edges
\boldsymbol{e}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{e}_{M-1}
in common with each other, as well as with the Erdős-Rényi graph
\mathbb{G}
.
Additionally, each
\mathbb{G}_{h,M}
contains another
m-M
random edges that are chosen independently for
h=1,2
.
In particular, for each
h=1,2
the individual random graphs
\mathbb{G}_{h,M}
have the same distribution as the Erdős-Rényi graph
\mathbb{G}(n,m-1)
with
m-1
edges.
Analogously, the random graphs
(\mathbb{G}_{h,M}^{-})_{h=1,2}
share
M-1
common edges and each have a total of
m
edges.
Thus, individually
\mathbb{G}_{h,M}^{-}
is an Erdős-Rényi graph
\mathbb{G}(n,m)
.
Finally, the graphs
(\mathbb{G}_{h,M}^{+})_{h=1,2}
have a total of
M
shared and
m-M
independent edges, and each
\mathbb{G}_{h,M}^{+}
separately is just a
\mathbb{G}(n,m)
.

The coupling
CPL0
–
CPL2
gives us a handle on the variance process
(\boldsymbol{X}_{n,M}^{2})_{1\leq M\leq m}
.
To see this, we introduce the three random variables

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{n,M}^{+}

\displaystyle=\prod_{h=1,2}\log\frac{Z_{\mathbb{G}_{h,M}^{+}}(\beta,B)}{Z_{\mathbb{G}_{h,M}}(\beta,B)},

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{-}_{n,M}

\displaystyle=\prod_{h=1,2}\log\frac{Z_{\mathbb{G}_{h,M}^{-}}(\beta,B)}{Z_{\mathbb{G}_{h,M}}(\beta,B)},

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\pm}_{n,M}

\displaystyle=\log\frac{Z_{\mathbb{G}_{1,M}^{+}}(\beta,B)}{Z_{\mathbb{G}_{1,M}}(\beta,B)}\log\frac{Z_{\mathbb{G}_{2,M}^{-}}(\beta,B)}{Z_{\mathbb{G}_{2,M}}(\beta,B)}.

(2.11)

Lemma 2.7
.

For all
B\geq 0
and
1\leq M\leq m
we have
\boldsymbol{X}_{n,M}^{2}=m^{-1}{\mathbb{E}}\left[{\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{n,M}^{+}+\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{n,M}^{-}-2\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{n,M}^{\pm}\mid\mathfrak{F}_{n,M}}\right]
.

The merit of expressing the squared martingale differences in terms of
\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{M}^{+},\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{M}^{-},\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{M}^{\pm}
is that the expressions on the right hand sides of (
2.11
) correspond to ‘local’ changes, similar in spirit to the Aizenman-Sims-Starr scheme that we alluded to in Section
2.1
.
For instance, the ratio
{Z_{\mathbb{G}_{h,M}^{+}}(\beta,B)}/{Z_{\mathbb{G}_{h,M}}(\beta,B)}
essentially accounts for the change in partition function upon adding one more shared edge
\boldsymbol{e}_{M}
.
The ensuing changes can be expressed in terms of the Ising distributions of the ‘small’ graph
\mathbb{G}_{h,M}
.

Lemma 2.8
.

Suppose
B\geq 0
.
With probability
1-O(1/n)
we have

\displaystyle\log\frac{Z_{\mathbb{G}_{h,M}^{+}}(\beta,B)}{Z_{\mathbb{G}_{h,M}}(\beta,B)}

\displaystyle=\log\left\langle{{\exp\left({\beta\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{e}_{M})}\right)},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}_{h,M},\beta,B}}}\right\rangle,

\displaystyle\log\frac{Z_{\mathbb{G}_{h,M}^{-}}(\beta,B)}{Z_{\mathbb{G}_{h,M}}(\beta,B)}

\displaystyle=\log\left\langle{{\exp\left({\beta\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{e}_{h,m-M+1})}\right)},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}_{h,M},\beta,B}}}\right\rangle.

(2.12)

In light of Lemma
2.8
it would be fairly straightforward to use Proposition
2.1
to evaluate the expectations

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\left[{\log\frac{Z_{\mathbb{G}_{h,M}^{+}}(\beta,B)}{Z_{\mathbb{G}_{h,M}}(\beta,B)}\mid\mathfrak{F}_{n,M}}\right],

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\left[{\log\frac{Z_{\mathbb{G}_{h,M}^{-}}(\beta,B)}{Z_{\mathbb{G}_{h,M}}(\beta,B)}\mid\mathfrak{F}_{n,M}}\right]

separately (at least if
B>0
).
But as Lemma
2.7
shows what we actually need are the conditional expectations of
\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{M}^{+},\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{M}^{-},\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{M}^{\pm}
, which each comprise a
product
of terms of the form (
2.12
).
In fact, involving the correlated random graphs from (
2.9
), the factors that make up
\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{M}^{+},\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{M}^{-},\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{M}^{\pm}
are stochastically dependent.
In order to evaluate these products, we need to control the empirical distribution

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\pi}_{n,M,\beta,B}^{\otimes}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{\left({\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v_{i})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}_{1,M},\beta,B}}}\right\rangle,\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v_{i})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}_{2,M},\beta,B}}}\right\rangle}\right)}\in\mathcal{P}([-1,1]^{2})

of the
joint
magnetisations of the vertices
v_{i}
in
\mathbb{G}_{1,M}
and
\mathbb{G}_{2,M}
.
Indeed, because the random graphs
(\mathbb{G}_{h,M})_{h=1,2}
are stochastically dependent, so are the magnetisations

\displaystyle\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v_{i})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}_{h,M},\beta,B}}}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\mu_{\mathbb{G}_{h,M},\beta,B}(\{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v_{i})=1\})-\mu_{\mathbb{G}_{h,M},\beta,B}(\{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v_{i})=-1\})

\displaystyle(h=1,2).

What we are going to show is that the empirical distribution
\boldsymbol{\pi}_{n,M,\beta,B}^{\otimes}
of these correlated pairs of magnetisations converges to the measure
\pi_{d,\beta,B,M/m}
from (
1.11
).
But before we come to that we need to ascertain that the limits
\pi_{d,\beta,B,t}
from (
1.11
) actually exist.

Proposition 2.9
.

For any
B\geq 0
,
t\in[0,1]
the weak limit (
1.11
) exists and the map

\displaystyle t\in[0,1]\mapsto\pi_{d,\beta,B,t}^{\otimes}

(2.13)

is
W_{1}
-continuous.

Based on Proposition
2.9
we can now establish the convergence of the empirical distributions
\boldsymbol{\pi}_{n,M,\beta,B}^{\otimes}
in the case
B>0
.

Proposition 2.10
.

Suppose
B>0
.
Then uniformly for all
0\leq M\leq m
we have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}[W_{1}(\boldsymbol{\pi}_{n,M,\beta,B}^{\otimes},\pi_{d,\beta,B,M/m})]

\displaystyle=o(1)

and

(2.14)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\left|{\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v_{1})\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v_{2})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}_{h,M},\beta,B}}}\right\rangle-\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v_{1})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}_{h,M},\beta,B}}}\right\rangle\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v_{2})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}_{h,M},\beta,B}}}\right\rangle}\right|

\displaystyle=o(1).

(2.15)

Proposition
2.10
is to the proof of the central limit theorem what Proposition
2.1
is to the proof of the first order approximation (
1.5
).
Accordingly, the proof of Proposition
2.10
combines the Belief Propagation recurrence (
2.4
) with local weak convergence techniques, a bit like the proof of Proposition
2.1
does.
The main difference here is that we are dealing with a pair
(\mathbb{G}_{1,M},\mathbb{G}_{2,M})
of correlated random graphs, rather than the plain random graph
\mathbb{G}
.
Hence, we need to devise a Galton-Watson process that captures the local geometry around a vertex
v_{i}
jointly in the two random graphs
(\mathbb{G}_{h,M})_{h=1,2}
.

The following three-type Galton-Watson tree
\mathbb{T}^{\otimes}=\mathbb{T}^{\otimes}(d,t)
fits this bill.
The three vertex types are simply type 0, type 1 and type 2.
The root
\varrho
is type 0.
A type 0 vertex begets a
{\rm Po}(dt)
number of type 0 vertices as offspring, as well as
{\rm Po}(d(1-t))
many type 1 vertices and
{\rm Po}(d(1-t))
type 2 vertices; these three Poissons are mutually independent.
Moreover, for
h=1,2
the offspring of a type
h
vertex is just a
{\rm Po}(d)
number of type
h
vertices.

We say that two possible outcomes
T,T^{\prime}
of
\mathbb{T}^{\otimes}
are
isomorphic
if there exists a tree isomorphism
T\to T^{\prime}
that preserves the root and the types.
Further, for
h=1,2
we let
\mathbb{T}^{\otimes}[h]
be the sub-tree of
\mathbb{T}^{\otimes}
obtained by deleting all vertices of type
3-h
.
In addition, for
\ell\geq 0
we obtain
\mathbb{T}^{\otimes\,(\ell)}[h]
from
\mathbb{T}^{\otimes}[h]
by deleting all vertices whose distance from
\varrho
exceeds
\ell
.

As a next step we relate the Galton-Watson tree
\mathbb{T}^{\otimes}
to the correlated random graphs
(\mathbb{G}_{h,M})_{h=1,2}
.
Thus, let
T
be a possible outcome of
\mathbb{T}^{\otimes}
.
We call a vertex
v\in V_{n}
an
\ell
-instance
of
T
if there exist graph isomorphisms
\iota_{h}:T^{(\ell)}[h]\to\nabla^{\ell}(\mathbb{G}_{h,M},v)
with the following properties for
h=1,2
:

ISM1:

\iota_{h}(\varrho)=v
,

ISM2:

u\in T
has type 0 iff
\iota_{1}(u)=\iota_{2}(u)
.

In other words, we ask that the depth-
\ell
neighbourhood of
v
in
\mathbb{G}_{h,M}
be isomorphic to the truncated tree
T^{(\ell)}[h]
, and vertices of type 0 be mapped to identical vertices of
\mathbb{G}_{h,M}
for
h=1,2
.
Hence, the type 0 vertices of
T
represent the endpoints of shared edges of
(\mathbb{G}_{h,M})_{h=1,2}
.

Let
\boldsymbol{N}_{T}^{(\ell)}(n,M)
be the number of
\ell
-instances of
T
.
The proof of Proposition
2.10
builds upon the following local weak convergence result that verifies that
\mathbb{T}^{\otimes}
faithfully models the joint local topology of
(\mathbb{G}_{h,M})_{h=1,2}
.

Lemma 2.11
.

Suppose that
t\in[0,1]
and
M\sim tm
and let
\ell\geq 0
.
Then for any possible outcome
T
of
\mathbb{T}^{\otimes}
we have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\left|{\boldsymbol{N}_{T}^{(\ell)}(n,M)/n-{\mathbb{P}}\left[{\mathbb{T}^{\otimes\,(\ell)}\cong T^{(\ell)}}\right]}\right|

\displaystyle=o(1).

With Proposition
2.10
pinpointing the empirical distribution of the joint marginals and also providing ‘replica symmetry’, we are ready to evaluate the expressions from (
2.11
) by way of an Aizenman-Sims-Starr argument.

Proposition 2.12
.

Suppose
B>0
.
The integral
\Sigma(d,\beta,B)^{2}
from (
1.12
) exists and

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\left|{\sum_{M=1}^{m}\boldsymbol{X}_{n,M}^{2}-\Sigma(d,\beta,B)^{2}}\right|

\displaystyle=o(1).

(2.16)

At this point one last step remains.
Namely, the proof of Proposition
2.12
falls short of revealing that
\Sigma(d,\beta,B)^{2}
is strictly positive.
To verify that this is the case we exploit the fact that
\pi_{d,\beta,B}
fails to concentrate on a single point.
Together with the continuity of the map from (
2.13
) and a convexity argument we thus obtain the final piece of the jigsaw.

Proposition 2.13
.

Suppose
d,\beta,B>0
.
Then
\Sigma(d,\beta,B)>0
.

Proof of Theorem
1.1
.

The theorem is an immediate consequence of Propositions
2.5
,
2.12
and
2.13
.
∎

2.3.
The central limit theorem in the case
B=0

We proceed to survey the proof of Theorem
1.3
, the central limit theorem in the case
B=0
,
d>1
and
\beta>\beta_{\mathrm{f}}(d)
.
Significant complications in this case derive from the inversion symmetry
\mu_{\mathbb{G},\beta,0}(\sigma)=\mu_{\mathbb{G},\beta,0}(-\sigma)
.
An immediate consequence of inversion symmetry is that we cannot expect the replica symmetry condition (
2.3
) to hold; yet replica symmetry was a cornerstone of the proof of the central limit theorem for
B>0
.
Instead, we are going to show explicitly that the phase space
\{\pm 1\}^{V_{n}}
decomposes into two distinct ‘pure states’ in the sense of
[
47
]
and that
within
each of these pure states replica symmetry is restored.
This pure state decomposition (and its proof) should be of independent interest.
The proofs for the following statements can be found in Sections
5
, where we prove Propositions
2.14
,
2.15
,
2.16
,
2.17
and
2.18
.

Let us begin by doing our homework on the fixed point distribution
\pi_{d,\beta,0}
from (
1.3
) in the case
B=0
.
Let
\rho_{d}
be the smallest positive solution to the equation
\rho=\exp(d(\rho-1))
.
Thus,
\rho_{d}
is the extinction probability of a
{\rm Po}(d)
-Galton-Watson process.
Also recall
r_{d,\beta}
from (
1.16
) and remember that
\mathfrak{X}_{d}
signifies the (countable) set of
\beta\in(0,\infty)
where the function
\beta\mapsto r_{d,\beta}
is discontinuous.

Proposition 2.14
.

The weak limit
\pi_{d,\beta,0}=\lim_{\ell\to\infty}\mathrm{BP}^{\ell}_{d,\beta,0}(\delta_{\infty})
exists and enjoys the following properties.

(i)

We have
{\text{supp}\left(\pi_{d,\beta,0}\right)}\subseteq[0,\infty)
.

(ii)

For
d>1
and
\beta>\beta_{\mathrm{f}}(d)
we have
\pi_{d,\beta}(\{0\})=\rho_{d}
and
\int_{0}^{\infty}x{\mathrm{d}}\pi_{d,\beta}(x)>0
.

(iii)

The function
\beta\mapsto r_{d,\beta}
is monotonically increasing and

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}{\mathbb{E}}\left[{\sum_{e\in E(\mathbb{G})}\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(e)},{\mu_{\mathbb{G},\beta,0}}}\right\rangle}\right]

\displaystyle=d\cdot r_{d,\beta}

\displaystyle\mbox{for all $\beta\in(0,\infty)\setminus\mathfrak{X}_{d}$}.

(2.17)

Part (iii) of the proposition, which clarifies the combinatorial meaning of the function
r_{d,\beta}
, is an easy consequence of statements from
[
11
,
23
]
; see Section
5.1
for details.

We now turn our attention to the ‘pure states’ of the Ising model on
\mathbb{G}(n,m)
.
This decomposition actually undergoes a phase transition at
\beta=\beta_{\mathrm{f}}(d)
.
Indeed, in Section
2.4
we will see that for
d\leq 1
or
d>1
,
\beta<\beta_{\mathrm{f}}(d)
the distribution
\mu_{\mathbb{G},\beta,0}
concentrates on configurations
\sigma\in\{\pm 1\}^{V_{n}}
with average magnetisation
n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma(v_{i})=o(1)
, which implies that
\mu_{\mathbb{G},\beta,0}
enjoys the replica symmetry property w.h.p. By contrast, we are about to verify that
\mu_{\mathbb{G},\beta,0}
is no longer replica symmetric in the ‘low temperature’ regime
d>1
and
\beta>\beta_{\mathrm{f}}(d)
w.h.p. Yet we will demonstrate that replica symmetry is restored once we condition on either of the two events

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{+}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{S}_{+,n}=\left\{{\sigma\in\{\pm 1\}^{V_{n}}:\sum_{v\in V_{n}}\sigma(v)>0}\right\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{-}=\mathcal{S}_{-,n}

\displaystyle=\left\{{\sigma\in\{\pm 1\}^{V_{n}}:\sum_{v\in V_{n}}\sigma(v)<0}\right\}.

Moreover, once we condition on
\mathcal{S}_{+}
the empirical distribution of the magnetisations converges to the limit
\pi_{d,\beta,0}
from (
1.3
).
Of course, due to inversion symmetry this implies that the empirical distribution of the
negated
conditional magnetisations given
\mathcal{S}_{-}
converges to
\pi_{d,\beta,0}
as well.

To make these statements precise let

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\pi}_{n,m,\beta}^{+}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v_{i})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{n}^{+})}}\right\rangle}

be the empirical distribution of the conditional magnetisations

\displaystyle\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v_{i})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{+})}}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\mu_{\mathbb{G},\beta,0}(\{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v_{i})=1\}\mid\mathcal{S}_{+})-\mu_{\mathbb{G},\beta,0}(\{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v_{i})=-1\}\mid\mathcal{S}_{+}).

Also recall from (
1.21
) that
\mu_{\mathbb{G},v_{1},\beta,0}^{(\ell)}
denotes the Ising model with a
+1
boundary condition imposed on all vertices at distance
\ell
from
v_{1}
.

Proposition 2.15
.

If
d>1
and
\beta\in(\beta_{\mathrm{f}}(d),\infty)\setminus\mathfrak{X}_{d}
, then

(i)

{\mathbb{E}}\left|{\mu_{\mathbb{G},\beta,0}(\mathcal{S}_{+})-1/2}\right|=o(1)
,

(ii)

{\mathbb{E}}\left|{\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v_{1})\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v_{2})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{+})}}\right\rangle-\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v_{1})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{+})}}\right\rangle\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v_{2})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{+})}}\right\rangle}\right|=o(1)
, and

(iii)

{\mathbb{E}}[W_{1}(\boldsymbol{\pi}^{+}_{n,m,\beta},\pi_{d,\beta,0})]=o(1)
and

\limsup_{\ell\to\infty}\limsup_{n\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}\left|{\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v_{1})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{+})}}\right\rangle-\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v_{1})},{\mu^{(\ell)}_{\mathbb{G},v_{1},\beta,0}}}\right\rangle}\right|=0.

While (i) above is a relatively simple consequence of the inversion symmetry of
\mu_{\mathbb{G},\beta,0}
, the other two statements are substantial.
Indeed, (ii) establishes replica symmetry of the conditional measure
\mu_{\mathbb{G},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{+})
.
Similarly as in Eq. (
2.6
), due to invariance under vertex permutations Proposition
2.15
(ii) is equivalent to

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}{\mathbb{E}}\left|{\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v_{i})\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v_{j})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{n}^{+})}}\right\rangle-\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v_{i})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{n}^{+})}}\right\rangle\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v_{j})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{n}^{+})}}\right\rangle}\right|=o(1).

(2.18)

Hence, given
\mathcal{S}_{+}
the spins
\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v_{i}),\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v_{j})
are asymptotically independent for all but
o(n^{2})
pairs
i,j
.
Finally, (iii) demonstrates that the (random) empirical distributions
\boldsymbol{\pi}_{n,m,\beta}^{+}
converge to
\pi_{d,\beta,0}
in probability.
In light of the discussion from
[
47
]
, the three properties (i)–(iii) justify our referring to
\mathcal{S}_{+},\mathcal{S}_{-}
as
pure states
.

We emphasise that the conditioning on
\mathcal{S}_{+}
is crucial in Proposition
2.15
.
Due to inversion symmetry the unconditional magnetisations just satisfy
\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v_{i})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G},\beta,0}}}\right\rangle=0
; thus, they are uninformative.
By contrast, Proposition
2.14
(ii) and Proposition
2.15
(iii) imply that the empirical distribution
\boldsymbol{\pi}^{+}_{n,m,\beta}
of the conditional magnetisations converges to a limiting distribution with a strictly positive mean.
This fact together with the replica symmetry provided by Proposition
2.15
(ii) easily implies that for the unconditional measure
\mu_{\mathbb{G},\beta,0}
we have

\displaystyle\liminf_{n\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}\left|{\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v_{1})\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v_{2})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G},\beta,0}}}\right\rangle-\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v_{1})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G},\beta,0}}}\right\rangle\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v_{2})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G},\beta,0}}}\right\rangle}\right|

\displaystyle>0

if
d>1
and
\beta>\beta_{\mathrm{f}}(d)
.

(2.19)

In other words, the unconditional
\mu_{\mathbb{G},\beta,0}
genuinely
fails
to be replica symmetric.
The proof of Proposition
2.15

combines the insights from Proposition
2.14
and techniques for the study of diluted spin glass models
[
7
,
18
,
19
,
20
]
with monotonicity and positive correlation arguments.

Beyond the proof of Theorem
1.3
Proposition
2.15
has several interesting combinatorial implications.
For example, it is easy to deduce from the proposition that the average magnetisation given
\mathcal{S}_{+}
concentrates on the strictly positive mean of
\pi_{d,\beta,0}
if
d>1
,
\beta>\beta_{\mathrm{f}}(d)
.
Furthermore, the Proposition
2.15
implies in combination with arguments from
[
19
]
that
\mathcal{S}_{\pm}
are ‘Bethe states’ in the sense of
[
48
]
.
But since these facts are not required to prove Theorem
1.3
we are not going to elaborate in the present work.

Ploughing ahead with the proof of Theorem
1.3
, as in Section
2.2
we are going to calculate
\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{M}^{+},\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{M}^{-},\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{M}^{\pm}
from (
2.11
) by way of their ‘local’ expressions (
2.12
).
To this end we observe that, due to Proposition
2.15
(i) and inversion symmetry, (
2.12
) can be rewritten in terms of the conditional distribution
\mu_{\mathbb{G}_{h,M},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{+})
:

\displaystyle\log\frac{Z_{\mathbb{G}_{h,M}^{+}}(\beta,0)}{Z_{\mathbb{G}_{h,M}}(\beta,0)}

\displaystyle=\log\left\langle{{\exp\left({\beta\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{e}_{M})}\right)},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}_{h,M},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{+})}}\right\rangle+o(1)

\displaystyle\mbox{w.h.p.\ },

(2.20)

\displaystyle\log\frac{Z_{\mathbb{G}_{h,M}^{-}}(\beta,0)}{Z_{\mathbb{G}_{h,M}}(\beta,0)}

\displaystyle=\log\left\langle{{\exp\left({\beta\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{e}_{h,m-M+1})}\right)},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}_{h,M},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{+})}}\right\rangle+o(1)

w.h.p.

(2.21)

Since Proposition
2.15
implies that the conditional distributions
\mu_{\mathbb{G}_{h,M},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{n}^{+})
are replica symmetric w.h.p., in order to evaluate (
2.20
)–(
2.21
) we basically just need to get a handle on the empirical joint distribution of the conditional magnetisations, i.e.,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\pi}_{n,m,\beta,M}^{\oplus}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{\left({\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v_{i})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}_{1,M},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{+})}}\right\rangle,\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v_{i})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}_{2,M},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{+})}}\right\rangle}\right)}\in\mathcal{P}([-1,1]^{2})\kern 5.0pt.

(2.22)

At this point we do not need to emphasise anymore that the conditional magnetisations

\displaystyle\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v_{i})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}_{h,M},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{+})}}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\mu_{\mathbb{G}_{h,M},\beta,0}(\{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v_{i})=1\}\mid\mathcal{S}_{+})-\mu_{\mathbb{G}_{h,M},\beta,0}(\{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v_{i})=-1\}\mid\mathcal{S}_{+})

\displaystyle(h=1,2)

are correlated because the random graphs
(\mathbb{G}_{h,M})_{h=1,2}
are.
We proceed to show that
\boldsymbol{\pi}_{n,m,\beta,M}^{\oplus}
converges to the distribution
\pi_{d,\beta,0,t}^{\otimes}
from (
1.3
), the existence of which already follows from Proposition
2.9
.

Proposition 2.16
.

Suppose
d>1,\beta\in(\beta_{\mathrm{f}}(d),\infty)\setminus\mathfrak{X}_{d}
.
Then uniformly for all
0<M\leq m
we have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}[W_{1}(\boldsymbol{\pi}_{n,m,\beta,M}^{\oplus},\pi^{\otimes}_{d,\beta,M/m})]

\displaystyle=o(1),

(2.23)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\left|{\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v_{1})\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v_{2})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}_{h,M},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{n}^{+})}}\right\rangle-\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v_{1})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}_{h,M},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{n}^{+})}}\right\rangle\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v_{2})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}_{h,M},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{n}^{+})}}\right\rangle}\right|

\displaystyle=o(1).

(2.24)

The proof of Proposition
2.11
builds upon the local weak convergence to the multi-type Galton-Watson tree
\mathbb{T}^{\otimes}
from Lemma
2.11
and the insights from Propositions
2.14
and
2.15
.

In analogy to the case
B>0
, we combine the conditional replica symmetry property from Proposition
2.15
with Proposition
2.16
to calculate the squared martingale differences via an Aizenman-Sims-Starr argument.

Proposition 2.17
.

Suppose
d>1,\beta\in(\beta_{\mathrm{f}}(d),\infty)\setminus\mathfrak{X}_{d}
.
Then the integral
\Sigma(d,\beta,0)^{2}
from (
1.12
) exists and

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\left|{\sum_{M=1}^{m}\boldsymbol{X}_{n,M}^{2}-\Sigma(d,\beta,0)^{2}}\right|

\displaystyle=o(1).

(2.25)

Finally, we need to verify that
\Sigma(d,\beta,0)
is strictly positive if
d>1,\beta>\beta_{\mathrm{f}}(d)
.
As in the case
B>0
this ultimately follows from the fact that
\pi_{d,\beta,0}
fails to concentrate on a single number.

Proposition 2.18
.

Suppose
d>1,\beta>\beta_{\mathrm{f}}(d)
.
Then
\Sigma(d,\beta,0)>0
.

Proof of Theorem
1.3
.

The theorem is an immediate consequence of Proposition
2.5
, Proposition
2.17
and Proposition
2.18
.
∎

2.4.
The case
B=0
, high temperature

Theorem
1.2
asserts that in the high temperature scenario without an external field, i.e.,
B=0
and
d\leq 1
or
\beta<\beta_{\mathrm{f}}(d)
the free energy only exhibits bounded fluctuations.
To prove this fact we employ the small subgraph conditioning method
[
36
,
57
]
.
Thus, we are going to argue that the variance of the partition function
Z_{\mathbb{G}}(\beta,0)
can be attributed entirely to the presence of bounded-length cycles in
\mathbb{G}
.
Since it is well known that the number of cycles of a fixed length
\ell\geq 3
in
\mathbb{G}
converges to
\boldsymbol{c}_{\ell}\sim{\rm Po}(d^{\ell}/(2\ell))
, we will thus obtain the limiting distribution stated in Theorem
1.2
.
The proofs for the following statements can be found in Section
6
, where we prove Propositions
2.19
,
2.20
and
2.21
.

To prove Theorem
1.2
we actually apply a twist: we are not going to perform small subgraph conditioning on the partition function
Z_{\mathbb{G}}(\beta,0)
, but work with a truncated random variable instead.
Specifically, let

\displaystyle Z_{\mathbb{G},\mathrm{bal}}(\beta)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\sigma\in\{\pm 1\}^{V_{n}}}\mathbb{1}\left\{{\left|{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma(v_{i})}\right|\leq 2\sqrt{n}\log n}\right\}\exp\left({\beta\sum_{vw\in E(\mathbb{G})}\sigma_{v}\sigma_{w}}\right)

(2.26)

Thus,
Z_{\mathbb{G},\mathrm{bal}}(\beta)
only takes into account ‘almost paramagnetic’ configurations
\sigma
, i.e., configurations whose empirical magnetisation
n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma(v_{i})
is not very much bigger than
n^{-1/2}
in absolute value.

The first step of the small subgraph conditioning method is to work out the precise asymptotics of the first moment.
The following proposition provides such a precise asymptotic expression for
\beta<\beta_{\mathrm{f}}(d)
.
Indeed, the proposition shows that for
\beta<\beta_{\mathrm{f}}(d)
the first moments of
Z_{\mathbb{G}}(\beta,0)
and
Z_{\mathbb{G},\mathrm{bal}}(\beta)
are asymptotically the same.

Proposition 2.19
.

Assume that
d\leq 1
or
\beta<\beta_{\mathrm{f}}(d)
.
Then

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[Z_{\mathbb{G}}(\beta,0)]

\displaystyle\sim\mathbb{E}[Z_{\mathbb{G},\mathrm{bal}}(\beta)]\sim\frac{2^{n}\operatorname{ch}^{m}\beta}{\sqrt{1-d\operatorname{th}\beta}}\exp\left({-\frac{d}{2}\operatorname{th}\beta-\frac{d^{2}}{4}\operatorname{th}^{2}\beta}\right).

As a next step we assess the correlations between the numbers of short cycles in
\mathbb{G}
and the truncated partition function
Z_{\mathbb{G},\mathrm{bal}}(\beta)
.
Thus, let
\boldsymbol{C}_{\ell}
be the number of cycles of length
\ell
in
\mathbb{G}
.
Moreover, let

\displaystyle\vartheta_{\ell}

\displaystyle=\frac{d^{\ell}}{2\ell},

\displaystyle\chi_{\ell}

\displaystyle=\operatorname{th}^{\ell}\beta.

(2.27)

Proposition 2.20
.

Assume that
d\leq 1
or
\beta<\beta_{\mathrm{f}}(d)
.
Then for any fixed
L
and
c_{3},\ldots,c_{L}\geq 0
we have

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{{\mathbb{E}}\left[{Z_{\mathbb{G},\mathrm{bal}}(\beta)\mid\boldsymbol{C}_{3}=c_{3},\ldots,\boldsymbol{C}_{L}=c_{L}}\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}\left[{Z_{\mathbb{G},\mathrm{bal}}(\beta)}\right]}

\displaystyle=\prod_{\ell=3}^{L}(1+\chi_{\ell})^{c_{\ell}}\exp(-\vartheta_{\ell}\chi_{\ell}).

Finally, we work out the precise asymptotics of the second moment of
Z_{\mathbb{G},\mathrm{bal}}(\beta)
.

Proposition 2.21
.

Assume that
d\leq 1
or
\beta<\beta_{\mathrm{f}}(d)
.
Then

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}[Z_{\mathbb{G},\mathrm{bal}}(\beta)^{2}]

\displaystyle\sim{\mathbb{E}}[Z_{\mathbb{G},\mathrm{bal}}(\beta)]^{2}\exp\left({\sum_{\ell\geq 3}\vartheta_{\ell}\chi_{\ell}^{2}}\right).

Proposition
2.19
,
2.20
and
2.21
precisely verify the conditions of the small subgraph conditioning method as summarised in
[
36
, Theorem 1]
(see Theorem
3.11
below).
Therefore, the proof of Theorem
1.2
is now immediate.

Proof of Theorem
1.2
.

Theorem
1.2
follows from Propositions
2.19
–
2.21
and
[
36
, Theorem 1]
.
∎

2.5.
Discussion

As pointed out at the start of the section, the contribution most closely related to the present work on a technical level is the recent article by Chatterjee, Coja-Oghlan, Müller, Riddlesden, Rolvien, Zakharov and Zhu on the random 2-SAT problem
[
14
]
.
Specifically, that article shows that throughout the satisfiable phase of the random 2-SAT problem the number of satisfying assignments is asymptotically log-normal.
Similarly to the present work (see the coupling
CPL0–CPL2
), the proof is based on investigating pairs of correlated random 2-SAT instances that share a certain number of clauses and each contain a number of independent random clauses.
That proof strategy, in turn, is inspired by the proof of the central limit theorem for fully connected spin glass models by Chen, Dey and Panchenko
[
15
]
, which is based on an interpolating family of Hamiltonians.

The key fact that facilitates the proof of the 2-SAT central limit theorem is that the 2-SAT problem on the appropriate Galton-Watson tree has a unique Gibbs measure throughout the satisfiable phase
[
1
]
.
This fact immediately implies that the uniform distribution on satisfying assignments is ‘replica symmetric’ and that the empirical marginals converge to the Belief Propagation fixed point on the Galton-Watson tree.
As we pointed out earlier, the Ising model on the Erdős-Rényi graph does not generally enjoy the Gibbs uniqueness property.
Yet the work of Dembo and Montanari
[
23
]
provides a workable substitute for Gibbs uniqueness in the presence of an external field, i.e.,
B>0
.
To be precise, the results from
[
23
, Section 4]
imply that in the case
B>0
the free boundary condition and the all-
1
boundary condition induce the same Gibbs measure on the Galton-Watson tree
\mathbb{T}
.
This implies replica symmetry and the convergence of the empirical magnetisations; Proposition
2.1
summarises the precise statement.
Thus, by harnessing these results in place of Gibbs uniqueness, we can prove Theorem
1.1
concerning the case
B>0
by following a broadly similar strategy as in
[
14
]
.

By contrast, in the case
B=0
matters get more involved.
For a start, for
d>1
and
\beta>\beta_{\mathrm{f}}(d)
it is not just that the Gibbs measure on the Galton-Watson tree is no longer unique, but the free boundary condition renders an uninformative and unstable Belief Propagation fixed point distribution (the atom at zero).
Furthermore, as we pointed out in Eq. (
2.19
), the replica symmetry condition (
2.3
) does not hold in this case.
Instead, we need to derive the pure state decomposition as stated in Proposition
2.15
.
The proof of this proposition constitutes the main technical challenge of the present work.
To elaborate, the proof of Proposition
2.15
relies on a general information theoretic decomposition result for probability measures called the ‘pinning lemma’ from
[
18
]
, see Lemma
3.9
below.
The pinning lemma is a generalisation of earlier results from Montanari
[
51
]
and Raghavendra and Tan
[
56
]
.
It was seized upon in prior work
[
19
,
20
]
to study diluted models of disordered systems such as spin glass models.
The pinning lemma implies that the Ising model on
\mathbb{G}
admits a ‘simple’ decomposition into a ‘small’ number of pure states where the conditional Ising distribution enjoys replica symmetry.
Starting from the pinning lemma, ultimately we are going to argue by means of monotonicity and positive correlation arguments that the only pure state decomposition that can be reconciled with the free energy formula (
1.5
) is the one stated in Proposition
2.15
.
Once this is in place we will then show that in the proof of the central limit theorem the pure state decomposition can be used in lieu of the Gibbs uniqueness property.

The proof of Theorem
1.2
relies on small subgraph conditioning, a technique originally developed by Robinson and Wormald
[
57
]
in order to count Hamilton cycles in random regular graphs.
Janson
[
36
]
stated a generalised version of small subgraph conditioning that we shall employ (see Theorem
3.11
below).
Mossel, Neeman and Sly
[
52
]
applied small subgraph conditioning to the assortative stochastic block model, a random graph model closely related to the Ising model on Erdős-Rényi graphs.
There are two main differences between the present case of the Ising model and the stochastic block model.
First, in the (symmetric) stochastic block model the partition function only runs over balanced partitions, whereas here we need to explicitly truncate the partition function (see (
2.26
)).
Second, in the stochastic block model edges are present with certain probabilities independently, while here we consider a fixed number of edges.

Beyond the aforementioned articles that use or develop techniques related to the present ones, a substantial number of contributions deal with Ising models on random graphs.
The first to study the Ising model on the Erdős-Rényi random graph were Bovier and Gayrard
[
13
]
.
They considered the case of ‘dense’ random graphs where the average degree
d=2m/n
diverges as
n\to\infty
.
2
2

2

Strictly speaking, Bovier and Gayrard deal with the directed version of the Erdős-Rényi graph.
The same applies to the work of Kabluchko, Löwe and Schubert
[
37
,
38
,
39
]
.

In this regime an appropriate rescaling of the Hamiltonian is necessary.
The rescaled partition function reads

\displaystyle Z^{\prime}_{\mathbb{G}}(\beta,B)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\sigma\in\{\pm 1\}^{V_{n}}}\exp\left({\frac{\beta}{2d}\sum_{e\in E(\mathbb{G})}\sigma(e)+B\sum_{v\in V_{n}}\sigma(v)}\right).

(2.28)

Bovier and Gayrard prove that the commensurate free energy, i.e.,
\frac{1}{n}\log Z^{\prime}_{\mathbb{G}}(\beta,B)
, converges in probability to the very same limit as the free energy of the Curie-Weiss Ising model with identical
\beta,B
.
Since the Curie-Weiss Ising model is just the Ising model on the (deterministic) complete graph with the free energy rescaled as in (
2.28
) by the degree
n-1
, the symmetry properties of the complete graph entail that the limiting free energy is given by a simple analytic expression
[
27
,
40
]
.
A further analogy between the Curie-Weiss model and the Ising model on the dense Erdős-Rényi graph is that in both cases the empirical distribution of the magnetisations converges to an atom if
B\neq 0
or
\beta<1
and to a two-point distribution if
B=0
and
\beta>1
.
Furthermore, the result of Bovier and Gayrard implies that

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}[\log Z^{\prime}_{\mathbb{G}}(\beta,B)]\sim\log{\mathbb{E}}[Z^{\prime}_{\mathbb{G}}(\beta,B)].

(2.29)

In disordered systems jargon (
2.29
) posits that the ‘quenched’ free energy matches the ‘annealed’ free energy.

Following up on Bovier and Gayrard, Kabluchko, Löwe and Schubert
[
37
,
38
,
39
]
determined the limiting distribution of the free energy in the dense case
d=d(n)\gg n^{1/3}
but
n-d(n)\gg 1
and obtained simple explicit expressions for the parameters of this distribution.
Specifically, for
d\gg n^{1/3}
in the low temperature regime
\beta>1
they obtain a central limit theorem for
\log Z^{\prime}_{\mathbb{G}}(\beta,0)
.
Moreover, in the high temperature regime
0<\beta<1
with
d\gg n^{1/2}
they show that
Z^{\prime}_{\mathbb{G}}(\beta,0)
(without the log) converges to a Gaussian.
Additionally, Kabluchko, Löwe and Schubert worked out the limiting distribution of the magnetisation
\sum_{v\in V_{n}}\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v)
of a configuration drawn from the Boltzmann distribution.

The case of sparse graphs turned out to be far more intricate than the aforementioned dense case.
This manifests itself, for instance, in the fact that the limiting distribution
\pi_{d,\beta,B}
of the empirical distribution of the vertex magnetisations is not generally an atom or a two-point distribution
[
23
]
.
In effect, in the sparse case the ‘quenched-equals-annealed’ equality (
2.29
) is generally false.
Indeed, in the case of finite average degrees
d
(which we consider in the present paper), it is not difficult to verify that

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}[\log Z_{\mathbb{G}}(\beta,B)]

\displaystyle\leq\log{\mathbb{E}}[Z_{\mathbb{G}}(\beta,B)]-\Omega(n)

\displaystyle\mbox{ if $B>0$ or $B=0$, $d>1$ and $\beta>\beta_{\mathrm{f}}(d)$}.

(2.30)

In light of these intricacies it is all the more remarkable that Dembo and Montanari established the ‘replica symmetric solution’ not only for the sparse Erdős-Rényi graph but for a general class of graph sequences that converge to unimodular trees in the topology of local weak convergence
[
22
,
23
]
.
The proofs are based on comparing the fixed points of the Belief Propagation operator on the limiting tree when starting from the free and the all-ones boundary conditions by means of the GHS inequality
[
31
]
.
Additionally, Basak and Dembo
[
11
]
investigate the local limit of the spin configurations on such graph sequences.
They show that in the case
B=0
the local limit of the configurations results by initialising the limiting unimodular tree from the all-
1
and the all-
(-1)
boundary conditions.
Furthermore, under the assumption that the graph sequence enjoys a certain expansion property, they prove that the local limit of a random configuration with positive overall magnetisation results from the all-
1
boundary condition only.
While the sparse random graph
\mathbb{G}
does not generally satisfy said expansion condition, we will be able to use a partial result
[
11
, Lemma 3.3]
to derive Proposition
2.14
.
Furthermore, as substantial added insight that Proposition
2.15
on the pure state decomposition contributes by comparison to the local convergence results from
[
11
]
is the presence of the conditional replica symmetry condition (Proposition
2.15
(ii)).
Together with the techniques from
[
19
]
this condition could be extended to show that
\mathcal{S}_{\pm}
are ‘Bethe states’ in the sense of
[
22
,
48
]
(details omitted).
Finally, Giardinà, Giberti, van der Hofstad and Prioriello
[
30
]
established a central limit theorem for the magnetisation
\sum_{v\in V_{n}}\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v)
of a random configuration
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
on locally tree-like graph sequences.
Their proof hinges on the free energy formula from
[
23
]
, which is needed to study the cumulant generating functions of the magnetisation.

Theorems
1.2
and
1.3
leave out the critical case of the Ising model on the Erdős-Rényi graph with
\beta=\beta_{\mathrm{f}}(d)
and
B=0
.
Very recently Prodromidis and Sly
[
55
]
proved that in this case the mixing time of Glauber dynamics is polynomial in
n
.
But deriving the precise limiting distribution of the free energy in the critical case remains an interesting open problem.

Apart from the Erdős-Rényi random graph, the Ising model has also been investigated on the conceptually simpler random regular graph.
Indeed, Galanis, Stefankovic, Vigoda and Yang
[
29
]
derive the limiting distribution of general ferromagnetic spin systems, including the Ising and Potts models, on random regular graphs by means of the small subgraph conditioning method.
They employ this result towards a complexity-theoretic reduction of certain computational counting problems.
In contrast to the sparse Erdős-Rényi case, in the case of random regular graphs the quenched and annealed free energies coincide and the limit of the empirical distribution of the vertex magnetisations is either an atom or a mixture of two atoms.
Subsequent contributions
[
12
,
25
,
33
]
derive a more precise picture in the case of random regular graphs and/or graph sequences that converge locally to a
d
-regular tree for the Potts and Ising models.
Additionally, Barbier, Chan and Macris
[
9
]
studied multi-overlaps of a general class of ferromagnetic
p
