-spin models on random hypergraphs.
Their proof is based on a perturbation of the Hamiltonian that does not shift the limiting free energy to the first order, but that seems to potentially distort the limiting distribution of the free energy.

Classically the Ising model has been studied on lattices such as
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
for integers
d\geq 1
.
Indeed, Ising’s thesis
[
35
]
establishes the absence of a phase transition in the case
d=1
.
Subsequently Onsager
[
53
]
proved that a phase transition occurs in the case
d=2
.
For the rich literature on the Ising model on lattices, which involves a wealth of mathematical techniques, we refer to
[
26
]
.

In the present paper we deal with the
ferromagnetic
Ising model on random graphs.
Remarkably, the antiferromagnetic version, which results by taking
B=0
and
\beta<0
in the definition (
1.19
), appears to be far more complex.
Indeed, according to physics predictions the antiferromagnetic Ising on random graphs model behaves like a genuine spin glass model that exhibits ‘replica symmetry breaking’, at least in the low temperature phase
[
41
,
49
,
50
]
.
Hence, while Propositions
2.1
and
2.15
show that the ferromagnetic Ising model on the random graph either behaves replica symmetrically or that the phase space decomposes into two mutually inverse pure states, in the antiferromagnetic model at low temperature an unbounded number of pure states are expected.
At this point only the existence of a replica symmetry breaking phase transition has been verified rigorously
[
16
,
18
]
.

3.
Preliminaries

In this section we summarise a few results from the literature that we are going to build upon.
We begin with basics about the Ising ferromagnet.

3.1.
Ferromagnetic facts

A key property of the ferromagnetic Ising model is a ‘positive correlation’ between spins.
Roughly speaking, given that
\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v_{i})=1
for some vertex
v_{i}\in V_{n}
the conditional probability that
\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v_{j})=1
for some other vertex
v_{j}
should be at least as large as the unconditional probability of the event
\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v_{j})=1
.
The precise correlation property that we will use repeatedly follows from a general inequality that is known as the ‘four functions theorem’.

Lemma 3.1

(Ahlswede-Daykin inequality/four functions theorem
[
3
]
)
.

Let
L
be a finite distributive lattice and let
f_{1},f_{2},f_{3},f_{4}:L\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
be functions satisfying

\displaystyle f_{1}(x)f_{2}(y)\leq f_{3}(x\vee y)f_{4}(x\wedge y)\;,

\displaystyle\text{for all }x,y\in L\kern 5.0pt.

(3.1)

Then for any
X,Y\subseteq L
we have

\displaystyle\left({\sum_{x\in X}f_{1}(x)}\right)\left({\sum_{x\in Y}f_{2}(x)}\right)\leq\left({\sum_{x\in X\vee Y}f_{3}(x)}\right)\left({\sum_{x\in X\wedge Y}f_{4}(x)}\right)\kern 5.0pt,

(3.2)

where
X\vee Y=\{x\vee y:x\in X,y\in Y\}
and
X\wedge Y=\{x\wedge y:x\in X,y\in Y\}
.

We apply Lemma
3.1
to the Ising model as follows.
For a set
V
, a subset
U\subseteq V
and a configuration
\tau\in\{\pm 1\}^{U}
let

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{\tau}

\displaystyle=\left\{{\sigma\in\{\pm 1\}^{V}:\forall u\in U:\sigma(u)=\tau(u)}\right\}.

(3.3)

Thus,
\mathcal{S}_{\tau}
is the discrete cube consisting of all configurations
\sigma
that match
\tau
on
U
.

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
G=(V,E)
be a graph,
B\geq 0
,
U\subseteq V
and
v\in V
.
Moreover, suppose that
\tau,\tau^{\prime}\in\{\pm 1\}^{U}
satisfy
\tau(u)\leq\tau^{\prime}(u)
for all
u\in U
.
Then
\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v)},{\mu_{G,\beta,B}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{\tau})}}\right\rangle\leq\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v)},{\mu_{G,\beta,B}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{\tau^{\prime}})}}\right\rangle.

Hence, the magnetisation of vertex
v
is an increasing function of the vector
\tau
that defines the subcube
\mathcal{S}_{\tau}
.

Proof of Lemma
3.2
.

If
v\in U
then there is nothing to show.
Hence, we may assume that
v\in V\setminus U
.
Let
W=V\setminus U
for brevity.
We are going to apply Lemma
3.1
to the lattice
L=\{\pm 1\}^{W}
with
\wedge
,
\vee
being the coordinatewise min and max, respectively.
To this end, given a configuration
\theta\in\{\pm 1\}^{U}
we define
{\mathcal{H}}(\cdot\mid\theta):\{\pm 1\}^{W}\to\mathbb{R}
by

\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}(\sigma\mid\theta)

\displaystyle=\beta\sum_{uw\in E(G)}\sigma(u)\sigma(w)\mathbb{1}\left\{{u,w\in W}\right\}+\sigma(w)\theta(u)\mathbb{1}\left\{{u\in U,w\in W}\right\}+B\sum_{w\in W}\sigma(w)

Assuming
\tau(u)\leq\tau^{\prime}(u)
for all
u\in U
let

\displaystyle f_{1},f_{2},f_{3},f_{4}

\displaystyle:\{\pm 1\}^{W}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0},

\displaystyle f_{1}(\sigma)=f_{3}(\sigma)=\exp\left({{\mathcal{H}}(\sigma\mid\tau^{\prime})}\right),\quad f_{2}(\sigma)=f_{4}(\sigma)=\exp\left({{\mathcal{H}}(\sigma\mid\tau)}\right)

(3.4)

It is easy to verify that the above functions satisfy the hypothesis of Lemma
3.1
. Indeed, for any
\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}\in\{\pm 1\}^{W}
we have

\displaystyle\sigma(u)\sigma(w)+\sigma^{\prime}(u)\sigma^{\prime}(w)

\displaystyle\leq(\sigma(u)\vee\sigma^{\prime}(u))(\sigma(w)\vee\sigma^{\prime}(w))+(\sigma(u)\wedge\sigma^{\prime}(u))(\sigma(w)\wedge\sigma^{\prime}(w))

for all
u,w\in W
, and

\displaystyle\sigma(w)\tau^{\prime}(u)+\sigma^{\prime}(w)\tau(u)

\displaystyle\leq(\sigma(w)\vee\sigma^{\prime}(w))\tau^{\prime}(u)+(\sigma(w)\wedge\sigma^{\prime}(w))\tau(u),

\displaystyle\mbox{ for all }w\in W,\;u\in U.

Summing the above on
uw\in E(G)
and taking exponentials establishes the condition (
3.1
) from Lemma
3.1
:

\displaystyle\exp\left({{\mathcal{H}}(\sigma\mid\tau^{\prime})+{\mathcal{H}}(\sigma^{\prime}\mid\tau)}\right)\leq\exp\left({{\mathcal{H}}(\sigma\vee\sigma^{\prime}\mid\tau^{\prime})+{\mathcal{H}}(\sigma\wedge\sigma^{\prime}\mid\tau)}\right)\kern 5.0pt.

Now let
A\subseteq L
be any upwards-closed subset of
L
.
Then
A\vee L=A
and
A\wedge L=L
.
Therefore, applying (
3.2
) to the functions from (
3.4
) with
X=A
and
Y=L
yields

\displaystyle\left({\sum_{\sigma\in\{\pm 1\}^{W}}\exp\left({{\mathcal{H}}(\sigma\mid\tau^{\prime})}\right)}\right)\left({\sum_{\sigma\in A}\exp\left({{\mathcal{H}}(\sigma\mid\tau)}\right)}\right)\leq\left({\sum_{\sigma\in A}\exp\left({{\mathcal{H}}(\sigma\mid\tau^{\prime})}\right)}\right)\left({\sum_{\sigma\in\{\pm 1\}^{W}}\exp\left({{\mathcal{H}}(\sigma\mid\tau)}\right)}\right)\kern 5.0pt.

(3.5)

Letting
A^{\tau}=\left\{{\sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{\tau}:\sigma|_{W}\in A}\right\}
we find

\displaystyle\mu_{G,\beta,B}\left({A^{\tau}\mid\mathcal{S}_{\tau}}\right)=\frac{\sum_{\sigma\in A}\exp\left({{\mathcal{H}}(\sigma\mid\tau)}\right)}{\sum_{\sigma\in\{\pm 1\}^{W}}\exp\left({{\mathcal{H}}(\sigma\mid\tau)}\right)}\kern 5.0pt.

Therefore, we may rewrite (
3.5
) as

\mu_{G,\beta,0}\left({A^{\tau}\mid\mathcal{S}_{\tau}}\right)\leq\mu_{G,\beta,0}(A^{\tau^{\prime}}\mid\mathcal{S}_{\tau^{\prime}}),

i.e.,
\mu_{G,\beta,0}\left({\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{\tau^{\prime}}}\right)
stochastically dominates
\mu_{G,\beta,0}\left({\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{\tau}}\right)
.
Hence, the assertion follows from the fact that
\sigma\mapsto\sigma(v)
is an increasing function of
\sigma\in\{\pm 1\}^{W}
.
∎

Furthermore, we build upon results of Lyons
[
44
,
45
]
on the Ising model on infinite trees.
An infinite tree
T
with root
\varrho
is
locally finite
if every vertex of
T
has a finite number of neighbours.
Lyons established a threshold for the root-magnetisation to be strictly positive in terms of a parameter of
T
called the
branching number
.
To introduce this parameter, call a finite set
\Lambda\subseteq V(T)
of vertices of
T
a
cut set of
T
if every infinite path starting at
\varrho
passes through
\Lambda
.
Then the branching number of
T
is defined as

\displaystyle\br(T)=\inf\left\{{\lambda>0:\inf_{\Lambda\textrm{ cut set of }T}\sum_{v\in\Lambda}\lambda^{-\mathrm{dist}_{T}(v,\varrho)}=0}\right\}\kern 5.0pt.

(3.6)

Lemma 3.3

(
[
45
, Theorem 2.1]
)
.

Let
T
be a locally finite tree rooted at
\varrho
. Then for all
\beta>\beta_{\mathrm{f}}\left({\br(T)}\right)
we have

\displaystyle\liminf_{\ell\to\infty}\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\varrho)},{\mu_{T,\varrho,\beta,0}^{(\ell)}}}\right\rangle>0\kern 5.0pt.

We are ultimately going to apply Lemma
3.3
to the Galton-Watson tree
\mathbb{T}=\mathbb{T}(d)
.
To this end we need to get a handle on the branching number of
\mathbb{T}
.

Lemma 3.4

(
[
46
, Corollary 5.10]
)
.

If
d>1
then
{\mathbb{P}}\left[{\br(\mathbb{T})=d\mid\mathbb{T}\mbox{ is infinite}}\right]=1
.

3.2.
A martingale central limit theorem

The following generic theorem provides the starting point for the proofs of Theorems
1.1
and
1.3
.

Theorem 3.5

(
[
32
, Theorem 3.2]
)
.

Let
(\mathcal{Z}_{n,M},\mathcal{F}_{n,M})_{0\leq M\leq m_{n},n\geq 1}
be a zero-mean, square-integrable martingale array with differences
\mathcal{X}_{n,M}=\mathcal{Z}_{n,M}-\mathcal{Z}_{n,M-1}
for
1\leq M\leq m_{n}
. Assume that there exists a constant
\eta^{2}>0
such that

CLT1:

\lim_{n\to\infty}\max_{1\leq M\leq m_{n}}|\mathcal{X}_{n,M}|=0
in probability.

CLT2:

\limsup_{n\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}[\max_{1\leq M\leq m_{n}}\mathcal{X}_{n,M}^{2}]<\infty.

CLT3:

\lim_{n\to\infty}\sum_{M=1}^{m_{n}}\mathcal{X}_{n,M}^{2}=\eta^{2}
in probability.

Then
\mathcal{Z}_{n,m_{n}}
converges in distribution to a Gaussian with mean zero and variance
\eta^{2}
.

As we will see, conditions
CLT1
and
CLT2
are easily satisfied in the present context of the Ising model on random graphs.
However, establishing
CLT3
turns out to be anything but straightforward.

3.3.
The pinning lemma

The ‘pinning lemma’ is an important ingredient to the proof of Theorem
1.3
and specifically to the proof of Proposition
2.15
.
The lemma provides that probability measures on sets of the form
\Omega=\{\pm 1\}^{V}
for a finite set
V\neq\emptyset
can generally be written as ‘canonical’ convex combinations of a ‘small’ number of measures that are ‘close’ to product measures.
Of course, we need to clarify what we mean by ‘canonical’, ‘small’ and ‘close’.

For ‘close’ we refer to the
cut metric

\Delta_{\Box}(\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,)
on the space
\mathcal{P}(\{\pm 1\}^{V})
of probability distributions on
\{\pm 1\}^{V}
as introduced in
[
19
]
.
For two probability measures
\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{P}(\{\pm 1\}^{V})
let
\Gamma(\mu,\nu)
be the set of all couplings of
\mu,\nu
.
Thus,
\Gamma(\mu,\nu)
contains all probability measures
\gamma\in\mathcal{P}(\{\pm 1\}^{V}\times\{\pm 1\}^{V})
such that for all
\sigma\in\{\pm 1\}^{V}
we have

\displaystyle\mu(\sigma)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\tau\in\{\pm 1\}^{V}}\gamma(\sigma,\tau)

and

\displaystyle\nu(\sigma)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\tau\in\{\pm 1\}^{V}}\gamma(\tau,\sigma).

Then the cut distance of
\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{P}(\{\pm 1\}^{V})
is defined as

\displaystyle\Delta_{\Box}(\mu,\nu)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|V|}\min_{\gamma\in\Gamma(\mu,\nu)}\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}U\subseteq V\\
\mathcal{B}\subseteq\Omega^{V}\times\Omega^{V}\end{subarray}}\left|{\sum_{(\sigma,\tau)\in\mathcal{B}}\sum_{u\in U}\gamma(\sigma,\tau)(\sigma(u)-\tau(u))}\right|

(3.7)

Intuitively,
\Delta_{\Box}(\mu,\nu)
gauges the largest remaining ‘discrepancy’ between
\mu
and
\nu
under the best possible coupling.
The cut metric is
much
weaker that other metrics such as the total variation distance.
It can be verified that
\Delta_{\Box}(\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,)
lives up to its name and is indeed a metric on
\mathcal{P}(\{\pm 1\}^{V})

[
19
]
.
The definition (
3.7
) of the cut metric is inspired by the cut metric from the theory of graphons
[
43
]
, which in turn generalises the work of Frieze and Kannan
[
28
]
.
Moreover, both the definition (
3.7
) and the notion of graphons are closely related to the Aldous-Hoover representation
[
5
,
34
]
, on which Panchenko’s concept of an asymptotic Gibbs measure is based
[
54
]
.

For a probability measure
\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\{\pm 1\}^{V})
define

\displaystyle\bar{\mu}(\sigma)

\displaystyle=\prod_{v\in V}\mu(\{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v)=\sigma(v)\})

\displaystyle(\sigma\in\{\pm 1\}^{V}).

In words,
\bar{\mu}\in\mathcal{P}(\{\pm 1\}^{V})
is the product measure on
\{\pm 1\}^{V}
with the same marginal probabilities
\bar{\mu}(\{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v)=1\})=\mu(\{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v)=1\})
as
\mu
itself.
Following
[
21
]
, we say that
\mu
is
\varepsilon
-extremal
if
\Delta_{\Box}(\mu,\bar{\mu})<\varepsilon
.
Furthermore, we say that
\mu
is
\varepsilon
-symmetric
if

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|V|^{2}}\sum_{v,w\in V}\sum_{s,s^{\prime}\in\{\pm 1\}}\left|{\mu(\{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v)=s,\,\boldsymbol{\sigma}(w)=s^{\prime}\})-\mu(\{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v)=s\})\mu(\{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(w)=s^{\prime}\})}\right|

\displaystyle<\varepsilon.

(3.8)

In words, an
\varepsilon
-symmetric measure
\mu
has the property that for ‘most’ pairs
v,w
the spins
\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v),\boldsymbol{\sigma}(w)
are approximately independent.
Clearly, this concept is closely related to the notion of replica symmetry; cf. (
2.6
).
The following close relationship between extremality and symmetry was established in
[
19
]
.

Lemma 3.6
.

For any
\varepsilon>0
there exist
\delta>0
and
N>0
such that for all sets
V
of size
|V|>N
and all
\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\{\pm 1\}^{V})
the following is true.

(i)

if
\mu
is
\delta
-extremal, then
\mu
is
\varepsilon
-symmetric.

(ii)

if
\mu
is
\delta
-symmetric, then
\mu
is
\varepsilon
-extremal.

In addition, we are going to need the following two inequalities from
[
21
]
.

Lemma 3.7

(
[
21
, Lemmas 3.12 and 3.13]
)
.

For any
\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{P}(\{\pm 1\}^{V})
we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|V|}\sum_{v\in V}\left|{\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v)},{\mu}}\right\rangle-\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v)},{\nu}}\right\rangle}\right|

\displaystyle\leq 8\Delta_{\Box}(\mu,\nu).

(3.9)

Moreover, if
\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{P}(\{\pm 1\}^{V})
are
\varepsilon
-extremal, then

\displaystyle\Delta_{\Box}(\mu,\nu)\leq 2\varepsilon+\frac{1}{|V|}\sum_{v\in V}\left|{\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v)},{\mu}}\right\rangle-\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v)},{\nu}}\right\rangle}\right|.

(3.10)

Thus, in order to prove that two
\varepsilon
-extremal measures are close in the cut metric, we just need to bound the absolute distances of their magnetisations.
Furthermore, the following lemma provides an enhanced triangle inequality for the cut metric.

Lemma 3.8

(
[
21
, Lemma 3.14]
)
.

Let
\mu^{(1)},\nu^{(1)},\ldots,\mu^{(\ell)},\nu^{(\ell)}\in\mathcal{P}(\Omega^{V})
and let
p_{1},\ldots,p_{\ell}\geq 0
be such that
\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}p_{i}=1
.
Then

\displaystyle\Delta_{\Box}\left({\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}p_{i}\mu^{(i)},\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}p_{i}\nu^{(i)}}\right)\leq\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}p_{i}\Delta_{\Box}(\mu^{(i)},\nu^{(i)}).

The most important fact in this section, and a cornerstone of the proof of Proposition
2.15
, is the following ‘pinning lemma’.
For a set
V
, a subset
U\subseteq V
and
\tau\in\{\pm 1\}^{U}
we let
\mathcal{S}_{\tau}
be the subcube from (
3.3
).
Think of
\mathcal{S}_{\tau}
as the set of configurations
\sigma
where the ‘pin’ each coordinate
u\in U
to
\tau(u)
.

Lemma 3.9

(
[
18
, Lemma 3.5]
)
.

For any
\delta>0
there exists a bounded random variable
\boldsymbol{I}\geq 0
such that for all finite sets
V
and all probability distributions
\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\{\pm 1\}^{V})
the following is true.
Let
\boldsymbol{U}\subseteq V
be a uniformly random subset of size
|\boldsymbol{U}|=\min\{\boldsymbol{I},|V|\}
.
Then

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}\left[{\sum_{\tau\in\{\pm 1\}^{\boldsymbol{U}}}\mu(\mathcal{S}_{\tau})\mathbb{1}\left\{{\mu(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{\tau})\mbox{ is $\delta$-symmetric\,}}\right\}>1-\delta}\right]

\displaystyle>1-\delta.

Thus, if we choose a suitable random set
\boldsymbol{U}\subseteq V
of coordinates, then there is a high probability that under the decomposition
(\mathcal{S}_{\tau})_{\tau\in\{\pm 1\}^{\boldsymbol{U}}}
of the cube
\{\pm 1\}^{V}
into subcubes almost the entire mass of
\mu
is placed on subcubes where the conditional distribution
\mu(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{\tau})
is
\delta
-symmetric.
The size of the set
\boldsymbol{U}
is determined by the random variable
\boldsymbol{I}
and given its size,
\boldsymbol{U}
is just a uniformly random subset of
V
.
Crucially, the random variable
\boldsymbol{I}\geq 0
is bounded and depends on
\varepsilon
only.
Hence, the number of coordinates that get ‘pinned’ is independent of the size of
V
and of the measure
\mu
.

Since the conditional distributions
\mu(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{\tau})
are likely mostly
\delta
-symmetric, they bear a conceptual semblance of the ‘pure states’ of a physical system as set out in
[
47
]
.
The ‘pure state decomposition’ provided by Lemma
3.9
is indeed ‘small’ because the random variable
\boldsymbol{I}
is bounded independently of
V
or
\mu
.
Furthermore, it is ‘canonical’ to the extent that the ‘pure states’
\mathcal{S}_{\tau}
are simply subcubes of the discrete cube
\{\pm 1\}^{V}
.
Both these facts will play a key role in the proof of Proposition
2.15
.

Finally, we are going to have to investigate how local changes such as the addition of a few edges affect the Ising distribution.
An important tool to this end is the following notion.
Let
\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{P}(\{\pm 1\}^{V})
be probability distributions.
Following
[
21
]
we say that
\nu
is
c
-contiguous
w.r.t.
\mu
if
\nu(\sigma)\leq c\mu(\sigma)
for all
\sigma\in\{\pm 1\}^{V}
.
In addition,
\mu
and
\nu
are
mutually
c
-contiguous
if
\nu(\sigma)\leq c\mu(\sigma)
and
\mu(\sigma)\leq c\nu(\sigma)
for all
\sigma\in\{\pm 1\}^{V}
.

Lemma 3.10

(
[
21
, Lemma 3.17]
)
.

For any
c>0
,
\varepsilon>0
there exist
\delta>0
,
N>0
such that for all sets
V
of size
|V|\geq N
and all
\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{P}(\{\pm 1\}^{V})
the following is true.
If
\mu
is
\delta
-extremal and
\nu
is
c
-contiguous with respect to
\mu
, then
\nu
is
\varepsilon
-extremal and
\Delta_{\Box}(\mu,\nu)<\varepsilon
.

3.4.
Small subgraph conditioning

The proof of Theorem
1.2
is based on the small subgraph conditioning technique for which the following theorem provides the general framework.

Theorem 3.11

(
[
36
, Theorem 1]
)
.

Let
(\kappa_{i})_{i\geq 1}
and
(\xi_{i})_{i\geq 1}
be sequences such that
\kappa_{i}>0
and
\xi_{i}>-1
for all
i
.
Also let
(\mathcal{C}_{i,n})_{i,n\geq 1}
and
(\mathcal{Z}_{n})_{n\geq 1}
be random variables such that
\mathcal{C}_{i,n}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and
{\mathbb{E}}[\mathcal{Z}_{n}]\neq 0
.
Moreover, let
(\mathcal{Y}_{i})_{i\geq 1}
be independent Poisson variables with
{\mathbb{E}}[\mathcal{Y}_{i}]=\kappa_{i}
.
Assume that the following conditions hold.

SSC0:

\sum_{i\geq 1}\kappa_{i}\xi_{i}^{2}<\infty
.

SSC1:

For all
\ell>0
and
c_{1},\ldots,c_{\ell}\geq 0
we have
\lim_{n\to\infty}{\mathbb{P}}\left[{\forall 1\leq i\leq\ell:\mathcal{C}_{i,n}=c_{i}}\right]={\mathbb{P}}\left[{\forall 1\leq i\leq\ell:\mathcal{Y}_{i}=c_{i}}\right].

SSC2:

For all
\ell>0
and
c_{1},\ldots,c_{\ell}\geq 0
we have

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{{\mathbb{E}}\left[{\mathcal{Z}_{n}\mid\forall 1\leq i\leq\ell:\mathcal{C}_{i,n}=c_{i}}\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}[\mathcal{Z}_{n}]}

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}(1+\xi_{i})^{c_{i}}\exp(-\kappa_{i}\xi_{i}).

SSC3:

\limsup_{n\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}\left[{\mathcal{Z}_{n}^{2}}\right]/{\mathbb{E}}\left[{\mathcal{Z}_{n}}\right]^{2}\leq\exp\sum_{i\geq 1}\kappa_{i}\xi_{i}^{2}
.

Then the infinite product

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=1}^{\infty}(1+\xi_{i})^{\mathcal{Y}_{i}}\exp(-\kappa_{i}\xi_{i})

(3.11)

converges almost surely and in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
and
{\mathbb{E}}[\mathcal{W}]=1
,
{\mathbb{E}}[\mathcal{W}^{2}]=\exp\left({\sum_{i\geq 1}\kappa_{i}\xi_{i}^{2}}\right).

Furthermore,

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{\mathcal{Z}_{n}}{{\mathbb{E}}[\mathcal{Z}_{n}]}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{W}

\displaystyle\mbox{in distribution}.

4.
Proof of Theorem
1.1

We prove the various statements from Sections
2.1
and
2.2
towards the proof of Theorem
1.1
.
Some of these statements, specifically Lemma
2.3
, Proposition
2.5
and Lemma
2.8
will also play a role in the proof of Theorem
1.3
.

4.1.
Proof of Lemma
2.3

The lemma can be deduced from general facts about Belief Propagation
[
48
]
, but we provide a self-contained proof for the sake of completeness.
Recalling the conditional distribution
\mu_{T,\beta,B}^{(\ell)}
associated with a tree
T
rooted at
\varrho
from (
1.21
), we let

\displaystyle Z_{T,s}^{(\ell)}(\beta,B)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\tau\in\{\pm 1\}^{V(T)}}\mathbb{1}\left\{{\forall{v\in\partial^{\ell}(T,\varrho)}:\tau(v)=1,\,\tau(\varrho)=s}\right\}\exp\left({\beta\sum_{vw\in E(T)}\tau_{v}\tau_{w}+B\sum_{v\in V(T)}\tau_{v}}\right)

\displaystyle(s=\{\pm 1\})

(4.1)

be the contribution to the partition function of
\mu_{T,\beta,B}^{(\ell)}
from configurations with root spin
\tau(\varrho)=s
.
Further, let

\displaystyle Z_{T}^{(\ell)}(\beta,B)=Z_{T,-1}^{(\ell)}(\beta,B)+Z_{T,1}^{(\ell)}(\beta,B).

Then the definition (
1.21
) of
\mu_{T,\beta,B}^{(\ell)}
implies that

\displaystyle\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\varrho)},{\mu_{T,\beta,B}^{(\ell)}}}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{s\in\{\pm 1\}}\frac{sZ_{T,s}^{(\ell)}(\beta,B)}{Z_{T,-1}^{(\ell)}(\beta,B)+Z_{T,1}^{(\ell)}(\beta,B)}.

(4.2)

Rearranging the sum (
4.1
), we see that

\displaystyle Z_{T,s}^{(\ell)}(\beta,B)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{e}^{Bs}\prod_{v\in\partial(T,\varrho)}\mathrm{e}^{\beta}Z_{T_{v},s}^{(\ell-1)}(\beta,B)+\mathrm{e}^{-\beta}Z_{T_{v},-s}^{(\ell-1)}(\beta,B).

(4.3)

Combining (
4.2
)–(
4.3
), we obtain

\displaystyle\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\varrho)},{\mu_{T,\beta,B}^{(\ell)}}}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{\sum_{s\in\{\pm 1\}}s\mathrm{e}^{Bs}\prod_{v\in\partial(T,\varrho)}\mathrm{e}^{\beta}Z_{T_{v},s}^{(\ell-1)}(\beta,B)+\mathrm{e}^{-\beta}Z_{T_{v},-s}^{(\ell-1)}(\beta,B)}{\sum_{s\in\{\pm 1\}}\mathrm{e}^{Bs}\prod_{v\in\partial(T,\varrho)}\mathrm{e}^{\beta}Z_{T_{v},s}^{(\ell-1)}(\beta,B)+\mathrm{e}^{-\beta}Z_{T_{v},-s}^{(\ell-1)}(\beta,B)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sum_{s\in\{\pm 1\}}s\mathrm{e}^{Bs}\prod_{v\in\partial(T,\varrho)}\mathrm{e}^{\beta}\mu_{T_{v},\beta,B}^{(\ell-1)}(\{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v)=s\})+\mathrm{e}^{-\beta}\mu_{T_{v},\beta,B}^{(\ell-1)}(\{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v)=-s\})}{\sum_{s\in\{\pm 1\}}\mathrm{e}^{Bs}\prod_{v\in\partial(T,\varrho)}\mathrm{e}^{\beta}\mu_{T_{v},\beta,B}^{(\ell-1)}(\{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v)=s\})+\mathrm{e}^{-\beta}\mu_{T_{v},\beta,B}^{(\ell-1)}(\{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v)=-s\})}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sum_{s\in\{\pm 1\}}s\mathrm{e}^{sB}\prod_{v\in\partial(T,\varrho)}\left({1+s\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v)},{\mu_{T_{v},\beta,B}^{(\ell-1)}}}\right\rangle\operatorname{th}\beta}\right)}{\sum_{s\in\{\pm 1\}}\mathrm{e}^{sB}\prod_{v\in\partial(T,\varrho)}\left({1+s\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v)},{\mu_{T_{v},\beta,B}^{(\ell-1)}}}\right\rangle\operatorname{th}\beta}\right)}\kern 5.0pt,

as claimed.

4.2.
Proof of Proposition
2.5

We derive Proposition
2.5
from Theorem
3.5
.
Let us start with the following fact.

Fact 4.1
.

Let
\beta,B\geq 0
.
For any graph
G
and any edge
e\in E(G)
we have

\displaystyle\frac{Z_{G}(\beta,B)}{Z_{G-e}(\beta,B)}

\displaystyle=\left\langle{{\exp(\beta\boldsymbol{\sigma}(e))},{\mu_{G-e,\beta,B}}}\right\rangle\in[\mathrm{e}^{-\beta},\mathrm{e}^{\beta}].

Proof.

By the definition of the Ising model,

\displaystyle\frac{Z_{G}(\beta,B)}{Z_{G-e}(\beta,B)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\sigma\in\{\pm 1\}^{V(G)}}\exp\left({B\sum_{v\in V(G)}\sigma(v)+\beta\sum_{vw\in E(G)\setminus\left\{{e}\right\}}\sigma(v)\sigma(w)}\right)\frac{\exp(\beta\sigma(e))}{Z_{G-e}(\beta,B)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\sigma\in\{\pm 1\}^{V(G)}}\mu_{G-e,\beta,B}(\sigma)\exp(\beta\sigma(e)))=\left\langle{{\exp(\beta\boldsymbol{\sigma}(e))},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}-e,\beta,B}}}\right\rangle,

as claimed.
∎

Proof of Proposition
2.5
.

Let
m=m_{n}\sim dn/2
be the number of edges of
\mathbb{G}
.
Assuming (
2.8
), we just need to check that the random variables
\boldsymbol{Z}_{n,M}
and
\boldsymbol{X}_{n,M}
from (
2.7
) together with the
\sigma
-algebras
\mathfrak{F}_{n,M}
satisfy conditions
CLT1
and
CLT2
from Theorem
3.5
.
We remind ourselves that
\mathfrak{F}_{n,M}
is the
\sigma
-algebra generated by the first
M
edges
\boldsymbol{e}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{e}_{M}
of
\mathbb{G}
.
Hence, for any
1\leq M\leq m=m_{n}
we have

\displaystyle|\boldsymbol{X}_{n,M}|

\displaystyle=m^{-1/2}\left|{{\mathbb{E}}[\log Z_{\mathbb{G}}(\beta,B)\mid\mathfrak{F}_{n,M}]-{\mathbb{E}}[\log Z_{\mathbb{G}}(\beta,B)\mid\mathfrak{F}_{n,M-1}]}\right|

\displaystyle\leq m^{-1/2}\max_{G,\,e\in E(G),\,e^{\prime}\not\in E(G)}\left|{\log Z_{G}(\beta,B)-\log Z_{G-e+e^{\prime}}(\beta,B)}\right|\kern 5.0pt;

(4.4)

to be precise, the maximum is taken over all possible outcomes
G
of
\mathbb{G}
, all edges
e\in E(G)
and all non-edges
e^{\prime}\not\in E(G)
.
In other words,
G-e+e^{\prime}
is obtained from
G
by removing edge
e\in E(G)
and inserting
e^{\prime}
instead.
Hence, Fact
4.1
shows that

\displaystyle\frac{Z_{G}(\beta,B)}{Z_{G-e}(\beta,B)},\frac{Z_{G-e+e^{\prime}}(\beta,B)}{Z_{G-e}(\beta,B)}\in[\mathrm{e}^{-\beta},\mathrm{e}^{\beta}].

(4.5)

Finally,
CLT1
and
CLT2
follow from (
4.4
)–(
4.5
), while
CLT3
follows directly from assumption (
2.8
).
∎

4.3.
Proof of Lemma
2.7

By the definition (
2.7
) of
\boldsymbol{X}_{n,M}
we have

\displaystyle m\boldsymbol{X}_{n,M}^{2}

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\left[{\log Z_{\mathbb{G}}(\beta,B)\mid\mathfrak{F}_{n,M}}\right]^{2}+{\mathbb{E}}\left[{\log Z_{\mathbb{G}}(\beta,B)\mid\mathfrak{F}_{n,M-1}}\right]^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad-2{\mathbb{E}}\left[{\log Z_{\mathbb{G}}(\beta,B)\mid\mathfrak{F}_{n,M}}\right]{\mathbb{E}}\left[{\log Z_{\mathbb{G}}(\beta,B)\mid\mathfrak{F}_{n,M-1}}\right].

(4.6)

Moreover, writing out the first expression from (
2.11
), we obtain

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\left[{\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{n,M}^{+}\mid\mathfrak{F}_{n,M}}\right]

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\left[{\prod_{h=1}^{2}\log Z_{\mathbb{G}^{+}_{h,M}}(\beta,B)-\log Z_{\mathbb{G}_{h,M}}(\beta,B)\mid\mathfrak{F}_{n,M}}\right]

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\left[{\prod_{h=1}^{2}\log Z_{\mathbb{G}^{+}_{h,M}}(\beta,B)\mid\mathfrak{F}_{n,M}}\right]+{\mathbb{E}}\left[{\prod_{h=1}^{2}\log Z_{\mathbb{G}_{h,M}}(\beta,B)\mid\mathfrak{F}_{n,M}}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\ \qquad\qquad\qquad-2{\mathbb{E}}\left[{\log Z_{\mathbb{G}^{+}_{1,M}}(\beta,B)\log Z_{\mathbb{G}_{2,M}}(\beta,B)\mid\mathfrak{F}_{n,M}}\right].

(4.7)

The coupling
CPL0
–
CPL2
ensures that the two random graphs
(\mathbb{G}_{h,M}^{+})_{h=1,2}
share the
M
random edges
\boldsymbol{e}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{e}_{M}
that are
\mathfrak{F}_{n,M}
-measurable, while the remaining edges that constitute
(\mathbb{G}_{h,M}^{+})_{h=1,2}
are chosen independently given
\mathfrak{F}_{n,M}
.
Therefore,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\left[{\prod_{h=1}^{2}\log Z_{\mathbb{G}^{+}_{h,M}}(\beta,B)\mid\mathfrak{F}_{n,M}}\right]

\displaystyle=\prod_{h=1,2}{\mathbb{E}}\left[{\log Z_{\mathbb{G}^{+}_{h,M}}(\beta,B)\mid\mathfrak{F}_{n,M}}\right]={\mathbb{E}}\left[{\log Z_{\mathbb{G}}(\beta,B)\mid\mathfrak{F}_{n,M}}\right]^{2}.

(4.8)

Combining (
4.7
)–(
4.8
), we obtain

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\left[{\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{n,M}^{+}\mid\mathfrak{F}_{n,M}}\right]

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\left[{\log Z_{\mathbb{G}}(\beta,B)\mid\mathfrak{F}_{n,M}}\right]^{2}+{\mathbb{E}}\left[{\prod_{h=1}^{2}\log Z_{\mathbb{G}_{h,M}}(\beta,B)\mid\mathfrak{F}_{n,M}}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad-2{\mathbb{E}}\left[{\log Z_{\mathbb{G}^{+}_{1,M}}(\beta,B)\log Z_{\mathbb{G}_{2,M}}(\beta,B)\mid\mathfrak{F}_{n,M}}\right].

(4.9)

Applying similar reasoning to
\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{n,M}^{-},\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{n,M}^{\pm}
, we find

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\left[{\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{n,M}^{-}\mid\mathfrak{F}_{n,M}}\right]

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\left[{\log Z_{\mathbb{G}}(\beta,B)\mid\mathfrak{F}_{n,M-1}}\right]^{2}+{\mathbb{E}}\left[{\prod_{h=1}^{2}\log Z_{\mathbb{G}_{h,M}}(\beta,B)\mid\mathfrak{F}_{n,M}}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad-2{\mathbb{E}}\left[{\log Z_{\mathbb{G}^{-}_{1,M}}(\beta,B)\log Z_{\mathbb{G}_{2,M}}(\beta,B)\mid\mathfrak{F}_{n,M}}\right],

(4.10)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\left[{\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{n,M}^{\pm}\mid\mathfrak{F}_{n,M}}\right]

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\left[{\log Z_{\mathbb{G}}(\beta,B)\mid\mathfrak{F}_{n,M}}\right]{\mathbb{E}}\left[{\log Z_{\mathbb{G}}(\beta,B)\mid\mathfrak{F}_{n,M-1}}\right]+{\mathbb{E}}\left[{\prod_{h=1}^{2}\log Z_{\mathbb{G}_{h,M}}(\beta,B)\mid\mathfrak{F}_{n,M}}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad-{\mathbb{E}}\left[{\log Z_{\mathbb{G}^{+}_{1,M}}(\beta,B)\log Z_{\mathbb{G}_{2,M}}(\beta,B)\mid\mathfrak{F}_{n,M}}\right]-{\mathbb{E}}\left[{\log Z_{\mathbb{G}^{-}_{1,M}}(\beta,B)\log Z_{\mathbb{G}_{2,M}}(\beta,B)\mid\mathfrak{F}_{n,M}}\right].

(4.11)

Finally, combining (
4.6
) with (
4.9
)–(
4.11
), we obtain the assertion.

4.4.
Proof of Lemma
2.8

Step
CPL1
of the coupling ensures that
\mathbb{G}^{-}_{h,M}
is obtained from
\mathbb{G}_{h,M}
simply by adding edge
\boldsymbol{e}_{h,m-M+1}
.
Therefore, the second equality in (
2.12
) follows from Fact
4.1
.
To obtain the first equality, we take a closer look at
CPL2
, which ensures that
\mathbb{G}_{h,M}=\mathbb{G}_{h,M}^{+}-\boldsymbol{e}_{M}
unless
\boldsymbol{e}_{M}\in\{\boldsymbol{e}_{h,1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{e}_{h,m-M}\}
.
But the event
\boldsymbol{e}_{M}\in\{\boldsymbol{e}_{h,1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{e}_{h,m-M}\}
has probability
O(1/n)
because
M\leq m=O(n)
.
Hence, Fact
4.1
implies that the first identity in (
2.12
) holds with probability
1-O(1/n)
.

4.5.
Proof of Proposition
2.9

We begin by verifying that the sequence
(\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\varrho)},{\mu_{\mathbb{T},\beta,B}^{(\ell)}}\rangle})_{\ell}
of root marginalisations converges almost surely as
\ell\to\infty
for all
B\geq 0
.
For
B>0
this was already established in
[
23
, Section 4]
, and for
B=0
convergence is implied by
[
11
, Lemma 1.18]
.
But toward the proof of Proposition
2.9
we need to know that the convergence of the root marginalisations occurs with respect to a specific filtration on the space of all Galton-Watson trees.

To be precise, let
\mathfrak{T}
be the space of all possible outcomes of the Galton-Watson tree
\mathbb{T}
.
Moreover, let
\mathfrak{A}_{\ell}
be the
\sigma
-algebra on
\mathfrak{T}
generated by the events
\left\{{\mathbb{T}^{(\ell)}\cong T}\right\}
for all finite rooted trees
T
.
Then
(\mathfrak{A}_{\ell})_{\ell}
is a filtration and the random variable

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{R}_{\ell}(\mathbb{T})

\displaystyle=\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\varrho)},{\mu_{\mathbb{T},\beta,B}^{(\ell)}}}\right\rangle

(4.12)

is
\mathfrak{A}_{\ell}
-measurable.

Lemma 4.2
.

For all
B\geq 0
the sequence
(\boldsymbol{R}_{\ell}(\mathbb{T}))_{\ell}
is a supermartingale with respect to the filtration
(\mathfrak{A}_{\ell})_{\ell}
, and
0\leq\boldsymbol{R}_{\ell}(\mathbb{T})\leq 1
for all
\ell
.

Proof.

Lemma
3.2
implies that
\boldsymbol{R}_{\ell}(\mathbb{T})\geq\boldsymbol{R}_{\ell+1}(\mathbb{T})\geq 0
, because the Boltzmann distribution
\mu_{\mathbb{T},\beta,B}^{(\ell)}
imposes a
+1
boundary condition on all vertices at distance precisely
\ell
from
\varrho
.
∎

Hence, the martingale convergence theorem shows that
(\boldsymbol{R}_{\ell}(\mathbb{T}))_{\ell}
converges to a random variable

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{R}(\mathbb{T})=\lim_{\ell\to\infty}\boldsymbol{R}_{\ell}(\mathbb{T})\in[0,1]

\displaystyle\mbox{almost surely}.

(4.13)

This now easily implies convergence of the measures
(\pi_{d,\beta,B}^{(\ell)})_{\ell}
from (
2.5
).

Corollary 4.3
.

For all
B\geq 0
the sequence
(\pi_{d,\beta,B}^{(\ell)})_{\ell}
converges in
W_{1}
to a distribution
\pi_{d,\beta,B}
supported on
[0,1]
.

Proof.

Corollary
2.4
demonstrates that
(\pi_{d,\beta,B}^{(\ell)})_{\ell}

W_{1}
-converges to the law of
\boldsymbol{R}(\mathbb{T})
, which is supported on
[0,1]
by Lemma
4.2
.
∎

As a next step we point out the following combinatorial interpretation of the probability distribution

\displaystyle\pi_{d,\beta,B,t}^{\otimes\,(\ell)}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{BP}^{\otimes\,\ell}_{d,\beta,B,t}(\delta_{(1,1)})

obtained by
\ell
-fold iteration of the operator from (
1.6
).

Lemma 4.4
.

Let
B\geq 0
and
t\in[0,1]
.
Then
\pi_{d,\beta,B,t}^{\otimes\,(\ell)}
is the distribution of the random pair

\left({\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\varrho)},{\mu^{(\ell)}_{\mathbb{T}^{\otimes}[h],\beta,B}}}\right\rangle}\right)_{h=1,2}.

Proof.

In analogy to Corollary
2.4
, this follows from Lemma
2.3
, the definition (
1.6
)–(
1.9
) of
\mathrm{BP}^{\otimes}_{d,\beta,B,t}
and the construction of the pair
(\mathbb{T}^{\otimes}[h])_{h=1,2}
of Galton-Watson trees.
∎

Corollary 4.5
.

Let
B\geq 0
,
t\in[0,1]
.
Then the
W_{1}
-limit
\pi_{d,\beta,B,t}^{\otimes}=\lim_{\ell\to\infty}\pi_{d,\beta,B,t}^{\otimes\,(\ell)}
exists and is supported on
[0,1]^{2}
.

Proof.

Let
\mathfrak{T}^{\otimes}
be the space of all possible outcomes of the multi-type Galton-Watson tree
\mathbb{T}^{\otimes}
and let

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{R}_{\ell}^{\otimes}(\mathbb{T}^{\otimes},h)

\displaystyle=\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\varrho)},{\mu^{(\ell)}_{\mathbb{T}^{\otimes}[h],\beta,B}}}\right\rangle.

(4.14)

Since each
\mathbb{T}^{\otimes}[h]
individually has the same distribution as the plain Galton-Watson tree
\mathbb{T}
, Lemma
4.2
shows that
(\boldsymbol{R}^{\otimes}_{\ell}(\mathbb{T}^{\otimes},h))_{\ell}
converges almost surely to a random variable
\boldsymbol{R}^{\otimes}(\mathbb{T}^{\otimes},h)\in[0,1]
for
h=1,2
.
Therefore, by the triangle inequality the pairs
(\boldsymbol{R}_{\ell}^{\otimes}(\mathbb{T}^{\otimes},1),\boldsymbol{R}^{\otimes}_{\ell}(\mathbb{T}^{\otimes},2))_{\ell}
converge to
(\boldsymbol{R}^{\otimes}(\mathbb{T}^{\otimes},1),\boldsymbol{R}^{\otimes}(\mathbb{T}^{\otimes},2))\in[0,1]^{2}
on
\mathfrak{T}^{\otimes}
almost surely.
Consequently,
(\pi_{d,\beta,B}^{\otimes\,(\ell)})_{\ell}
converges to the law of
(\boldsymbol{R}^{\otimes}(\mathbb{T}^{\otimes},1),\boldsymbol{R}^{\otimes}(\mathbb{T}^{\otimes},2))
with respect to the
W_{1}
-metric.
∎

Proof of Proposition
2.9
.

The existence of the limit
\pi_{d,\beta,B,t}^{\otimes}
follows directly from Corollary
4.5
.
To prove the continuity statement, fix
\varepsilon>0
.
Let
\boldsymbol{R}_{\ell}^{\otimes}(\mathbb{T}^{\otimes},h)
be the random variable from (
4.14
), and let
\boldsymbol{R}^{\otimes}(\mathbb{T}^{\otimes},h)
be the almost sure and thus
L^{1}
-limit of the sequence
(\boldsymbol{R}_{\ell}^{\otimes}(\mathbb{T}^{\otimes},h))_{\ell}
.
Recalling the random variables
\boldsymbol{R}_{\ell}(\mathbb{T})
from (
4.12
) and their almost sure limit
\boldsymbol{R}(\mathbb{T})
, for
\ell>0
we let

\displaystyle\mathfrak{E}(\varepsilon,\ell)

\displaystyle=\left\{{T\in\mathfrak{T}:|\boldsymbol{R}_{\ell}(T)-\boldsymbol{R}(T)|>\varepsilon}\right\}\subseteq\mathfrak{T}.

Then there exists
\ell_{0}=\ell_{0}(\varepsilon)>0
such that for all
\ell>\ell_{0}
we have
{\mathbb{P}}\left[{\mathbb{T}\in\mathfrak{E}(\varepsilon,\ell)}\right]<\varepsilon
.
Since the individual trees
\mathbb{T}^{\otimes}[h]
for
h=1,2
have the same distribution as
\mathbb{T}
for all
t\in[0,1]
, we conclude that for all
\ell>\ell_{0}
,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}\left[{\left|{\boldsymbol{R}_{\ell}(\mathbb{T}^{\otimes},1)-\boldsymbol{R}(\mathbb{T}^{\otimes},1)}\right|+\left|{\boldsymbol{R}_{\ell}(\mathbb{T}^{\otimes},2)-\boldsymbol{R}(\mathbb{T}^{\otimes},2)}\right|>2\varepsilon}\right]

\displaystyle\leq{\mathbb{P}}\left[{\mathbb{T}^{\otimes}[1]\in\mathfrak{E}(\varepsilon,\ell)}\right]+{\mathbb{P}}\left[{\mathbb{T}^{\otimes}[2]\in\mathfrak{E}(\varepsilon,\ell)}\right]

\displaystyle=2{\mathbb{P}}\left[{\mathbb{T}\in\mathfrak{E}(\varepsilon,\ell)}\right]<2\varepsilon.

(4.15)

Now, pick
\delta=\delta(\varepsilon,\ell)>0
small enough and suppose that
t^{\prime},t^{\prime\prime}\in[0,1]
satisfy
|t^{\prime}-t^{\prime\prime}|<\delta
.
Then for either value
t\in\{t^{\prime},t^{\prime\prime}\}
the definition of the multi-type Galton-Watson tree
\mathbb{T}^{\otimes}
provides that a type 0 vertex spawns
{\rm Po}(d(1-t))
offspring of type 0, and
{\rm Po}(dt)
offspring of type 1 and type 2.
(The offspring distributions of type 1 and type 2 vertices are independent of
t
.)
Hence, provided that
|t^{\prime}-t^{\prime\prime}|<\delta
for a sufficiently small
\delta=\delta(\varepsilon,\ell)>0
, we can couple these Poisson variables such that

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}\left[{\mathbb{T}^{\otimes\,(\ell)}(d,t^{\prime})\neq\mathbb{T}^{\otimes\,(\ell)}(d,t^{\prime\prime})}\right]<\varepsilon.

(4.16)

Combining (
4.15
) and (
4.16
) and applying the triangle inequality, we obtain

\displaystyle W_{1}(\pi_{d,\beta,B,t^{\prime}}^{\otimes},\pi_{d,\beta,B,t^{\prime\prime}}^{\otimes})

\displaystyle\leq 4\varepsilon+{\mathbb{P}}\left[{\mathbb{T}^{\otimes\,(\ell)}(d,t^{\prime})\neq\mathbb{T}^{\otimes\,(\ell)}(d,t^{\prime\prime})}\right]+2{\mathbb{P}}\left[{\mathbb{T}\in\mathfrak{E}(\varepsilon,\ell)}\right]<7\varepsilon,

whence
t\mapsto\pi_{d,\beta,B,t}^{\otimes}
is
W_{1}
-continuous.
∎

4.6.
Proof of Lemma
2.11

The key step towards the proof is to establish the following statement.

Lemma 4.6
.

Suppose that
t\in[0,1]
,
\ell\geq 0
and
M\sim tm
.
Then for any rooted tree
T
we have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}\left[{v_{1}\mbox{ is an $\ell$-instance of }T}\right]\sim{\mathbb{P}}\left[{\mathbb{T}^{\otimes\,(\ell)}\cong T}\right].

Proof.

We proceed by induction on
\ell
.
In the case
\ell=0
there is nothing to show because the ‘tree’
\mathbb{T}_{d,t}^{\otimes\,(\ell)}
consists of the root only.
Going from
\ell-1
to
\ell>0
, let
\boldsymbol{X}_{\tau}
be the number of children of
\varrho
of type
\tau\in\{0,1,2\}
in
\mathbb{T}_{d,t}^{\otimes}
.
Moreover, let
\boldsymbol{Y}_{0}
be the number of shared edges
e\in\{\boldsymbol{e}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{e}_{M-1}\}
incident with
v_{1}
.
Additionally, let
\boldsymbol{Y}_{h}
be the number of edges
e\in\{\boldsymbol{e}_{h,1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{e}_{h,m-M}\}
incident with
v_{1}
.
The construction of
(\mathbb{G}_{h,M})_{h=1,2}
and of
\mathbb{T}^{\otimes}
ensures that

\displaystyle d_{\mathrm{TV}}((\boldsymbol{X}_{0},\boldsymbol{X}_{1},\boldsymbol{X}_{2}),(\boldsymbol{Y}_{0},\boldsymbol{Y}_{1},\boldsymbol{Y}_{2}))

\displaystyle=o(1).

(4.17)

Consider the event
\mathfrak{E}
that the depth-
\ell
neighbourhood of
v_{1}
in the union
\mathbb{G}_{1,M}\cup\mathbb{G}_{2,M}=(V_{n},E(\mathbb{G}_{1,M})\cup E(\mathbb{G}_{2,M}))
is acyclic and contains a total of at most
\log n
edges.
Then the Chernoff bound shows together with a routine breadth-first search exploration argument that

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}\left[{\mathfrak{E}}\right]

\displaystyle=1-O(1/n).

(4.18)

Let

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}_{0}

\displaystyle=|\partial(\mathbb{G}_{1,M},v_{1})\cap\partial(\mathbb{G}_{2,M},v_{1})|,

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}_{h}

\displaystyle=|\partial(\mathbb{G}_{h,M},v_{1})\setminus\partial(\mathbb{G}_{3-h,M},v_{1})|

\displaystyle(h=1,2).

Hence,
\mathcal{Y}_{0}
is the number of ‘shared’ neighbours of
v_{1}
in
\mathbb{G}_{1,M}
and
\mathbb{G}_{2,M}
, and
\mathcal{Y}_{h}
is the number of ‘separate’ neighbours of
v_{1}
in
\mathbb{G}_{h,M}
.
Given
\mathfrak{E}
we have
\mathcal{Y}_{0}\cap\mathcal{Y}_{1}=\mathcal{Y}_{0}\cap\mathcal{Y}_{2}=\mathcal{Y}_{1}\cap\mathcal{Y}_{2}=\emptyset
and
|\mathcal{Y}_{0}|=\boldsymbol{Y}_{0}
and
|\mathcal{Y}_{h}|=\boldsymbol{Y}_{h}
for
h=1,2
.

Now, given
\mathfrak{E}
and a vertex
v\in\mathcal{Y}_{0}\cup\mathcal{Y}_{1}\cup\mathcal{Y}_{2}
let
\mathcal{N}_{\ell}(v)
be the set of all vertices
w\in\nabla^{\ell}(\mathbb{G}_{1,M}\cup\mathbb{G}_{2,M},v_{1})
such that the shortest path from
w
to
v_{1}
passes through
v
.
Obtain
\mathbb{G}_{h,M}[v]
from
\mathbb{G}_{h,M}
by deleting all the vertices in
\nabla^{\ell}(\mathbb{G}_{h,M},v_{1})\setminus\mathcal{N}_{\ell}(v)
.
Since on
\mathfrak{E}
we have
|\nabla^{\ell}(\mathbb{G}_{h,M},v_{1})|=O(\log n)
, the graph
\mathbb{G}_{h,M}[v]
contains
n+O(\log n)\sim n
vertices and
m+O(\log n)\sim dn/2
edges.
Thus, the pair
(\mathbb{G}_{h,M}[v])_{h=1,2}
is distributed as a pair of random graphs
(\mathbb{G}_{h,M+O(\log n)}(n+O(\log n),m+O(\log n)))_{h=1,2}
, i.e., a pair of random graphs produced by the coupling
CPL0
–
CPL2
with asymptotically unchanged parameters.
Therefore, by induction for every possible outcome
T^{\prime}
of
\mathbb{T}_{d,t}^{\otimes}
and any vertex
v\in\mathcal{Y}_{0}
we have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}\left[{v\mbox{ is an $(\ell-1)$-instance of $T^{\prime}$ in $(\mathbb{G}_{h,M}[v])_{h=1,2}$}\mid\mathfrak{E}}\right]\sim{\mathbb{P}}\left[{\mathbb{T}^{\otimes\,(\ell-1)}_{d,t}\cong T^{\prime}}\right].

(4.19)

Similarly, by Lemma
2.2
for any vertex
v\in\mathcal{Y}_{h}
and any possible outcome
T^{\prime\prime}
of the plain Galton-Watson tree
\mathbb{T}_{d}
we have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}\left[{\nabla^{\ell-1}(\mathbb{G}_{h,M}[v],v)\cong T^{\prime\prime}\mid\mathfrak{E}}\right]\sim{\mathbb{P}}\left[{\mathbb{T}^{(\ell-1)}_{d}\cong T^{\prime\prime}}\right]

\displaystyle(h=1,2).

(4.20)

Finally, given
\mathfrak{E}
the events from (
4.19
)–(
4.20
) are asymptotically independent.
Therefore, the assertion follows from (
4.17
)–(
4.20
) and the recursive definition of the tree
\mathbb{T}_{d,t}^{\otimes}
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
2.11
.

Lemma
4.6
implies that

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\left[{\boldsymbol{N}_{T}^{(\ell)}(n,M)}\right]

\displaystyle\sim n{\mathbb{P}}\left[{\mathbb{T}_{d,t}^{\otimes}\cong T}\right].

(4.21)

Furthermore, adding or removing a single edge
e=vw
to or from
\mathbb{G}_{1,M}\cup\mathbb{G}_{2,M}
can alter the number of
\ell
-instances of
T
by only a bounded amount.
To see this, let
\Delta
be the maximum degree of
T
.
For
e
to turn a vertex
u
from being an
\ell
-instance to not being an
\ell
-instance or vice versa, one of the vertices
v,w
has to have distance at most
\ell-1
from
u
.
Say this is vertex
v
.
Then there has to be a path
u=u_{1},\ldots,u_{l}=v
with
l\leq\ell-1
such that
u_{1},\ldots,u_{l}
have degree at most
\Delta
.
Since there exist no more than
\Delta^{\ell-1}
paths of length
\ell-1
emanating from
v
where every vertex has degree at most
\Delta
, the number of affected vertices
u
is bounded.
Consequently, a straightforward application of Azuma–Hoeffding shows that w.h.p.

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{N}_{T}^{(\ell)}(n,M)={\mathbb{E}}\left[{\boldsymbol{N}_{T}^{(\ell)}(n,M)}\right]+o(n).

(4.22)

The assertion follows from (
4.21
)–(
4.22
).
∎

4.7.
Proof of Proposition
2.10

We begin with an observation about the Erdős-Rényi graph
\mathbb{G}
.
Recall from (
1.21
) that for a rooted graph
(G,r)
we denote by
\mu^{(\ell)}_{G,r,\beta,B}
the Ising distribution with a
+1
boundary condition imposed on all vertices at distance
\ell
from the root
r
.
Lemma
3.2
directly implies that for any
(G,r)
, any
\beta,B\geq 0
and any
\ell\geq 0
we have

\displaystyle\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(r)},{\mu_{G,\beta,B}}}\right\rangle\leq\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(r)},{\mu^{(\ell)}_{G,r,\beta,B}}}\right\rangle.

(4.23)

The following lemma shows that on the Erdős-Rényi graph
\mathbb{G}
rooted at
v_{1}
the bound (
4.23
) gets asymptotically tight in the limit
\ell\to\infty
, provided
B>0
.
The statement is implicit in
[
23
, Section 6]
but we provide a short proof for completeness.

Lemma 4.7
.

If
B>0
then
\limsup_{\ell\to\infty}\limsup_{n\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}\left|{\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v_{1})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G},\beta,B}}}\right\rangle-\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v_{1})},{\mu^{(\ell)}_{\mathbb{G},v_{1},\beta,B}}}\right\rangle}\right|=0.

Proof.

Recall from (
2.5
) that
\pi_{d,\beta,B}^{(\ell)}=\mathrm{BP}^{\ell}_{d,\beta,B}(\delta_{1})
.
Hence, (
1.3
) and Equation (
2.2
) from Proposition
2.1
show that

\displaystyle\limsup_{\ell\to\infty}\limsup_{n\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}[W_{1}(\pi_{\mathbb{G},\beta,B},\pi^{(\ell)}_{d,\beta,B})]

\displaystyle=\limsup_{n\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}[W_{1}(\pi_{\mathbb{G},\beta,B},\pi_{d,\beta,B})]=0.

(4.24)

Further, Lemma
2.2
(the local weak convergence result) and Corollary
2.4
show that the mean of
\pi^{(\ell)}_{d,\beta,B}
satisfies

\displaystyle\int_{-1}^{1}\xi{\mathrm{d}}\pi^{(\ell)}_{d,\beta,B}(\xi)

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\varrho)},{\mu_{\mathbb{T},\beta,B}^{(\ell)}}}\right\rangle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\mathbb{E}}\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v_{i})},{\mu^{(\ell)}_{\mathbb{G},v_{i},\beta,B}}}\right\rangle+o(1)={\mathbb{E}}\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v_{1})},{\mu^{(\ell)}_{\mathbb{G},v_{1},\beta,B}}}\right\rangle+o(1).

(4.25)

Combining (
4.24
) and (
4.25
), we obtain

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v_{1})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G},\beta,B}}}\right\rangle=\int_{-1}^{1}\xi{\mathrm{d}}\pi_{d,\beta,B}(\xi)

\displaystyle=\lim_{\ell\to\infty}\int_{-1}^{1}\xi{\mathrm{d}}\pi^{(\ell)}_{d,\beta,B}(\xi)=\lim_{\ell\to\infty}\lim_{n\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v_{1})},{\mu^{(\ell)}_{\mathbb{G},v_{1},\beta,B}}}\right\rangle.

(4.26)

Finally, since (
4.23
) shows that
\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v_{1})},{\mu^{(\ell)}_{\mathbb{G},v_{1},\beta,B}}\rangle}\geq\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v_{1})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G},\beta,B}}}\right\rangle
, the assertion follows from (
4.26
).
∎

Corollary 4.8
.

If
d,\beta,B>0
and
0\leq M\leq m
, then

\displaystyle\limsup_{\ell\to\infty}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\sum_{h=1,2}{\mathbb{E}}\left|{\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v_{1})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}_{h,M},\beta,B}}}\right\rangle-\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v_{1})},{\mu^{(\ell)}_{\mathbb{G}_{h,M},v_{1},\beta,B}}}\right\rangle}\right|

\displaystyle=0.

Proof.

Since each graph
\mathbb{G}_{h,M}
separately is distributed as the random graph
\mathbb{G}(n,m-1)
and
2(m-1)/n\sim 2m/n\sim d
, the assertion follows from Lemma
4.7
and the union bound.
∎

Proof of Proposition
2.10
.

Let

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\pi}_{n,M,\beta,B}^{\otimes\,(\ell)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{\left({\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v_{i})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}_{1,M},v_{i},\beta,B}^{(\ell)}}}\right\rangle,\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v_{i})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}_{2,M},v_{i},\beta,B}^{(\ell)}}}\right\rangle}\right)}

be the empirical distribution of the joint magnetisations
(\langle{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v_{i})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}_{h,M},v_{i},\beta,B}^{(\ell)}}\rangle)_{h=1,2}
with a
+1
boundary condition imposed at distance
\ell
from
v_{i}
.
Corollary
4.8
shows that

\displaystyle\limsup_{\ell\to\infty}\limsup_{n\to\infty}W_{1}\left({\boldsymbol{\pi}_{n,M,\beta,B}^{\otimes\,(\ell)},\boldsymbol{\pi}_{n,M,\beta,B}^{\otimes}}\right)

\displaystyle=0.

(4.27)

Furthermore, the local weak convergence from Lemma
2.11
implies together with Lemma
4.4
that for any
\ell\geq 0
,

\displaystyle W_{1}\left({\boldsymbol{\pi}_{n,M,\beta,B}^{\otimes\,(\ell)},\pi_{d,\beta,B,t}^{\otimes\,(\ell)}}\right)

\displaystyle=o(1).

(4.28)

Thus, the assertion follows from (
4.27
)–(
4.28
) and Corollary
4.5
.
∎

4.8.
Proof of Proposition
2.12

In light of Lemma
2.7
we are going to study the terms
\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{n,M}^{+},\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{n,M}^{-},\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{n,M}^{\pm}
from (
2.11
).
Let
\mathfrak{G}_{n,M}
be the
\sigma
-algebra generated by the random graphs
(\mathbb{G}_{h,M})_{h=1,2}
and set

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{n,M}=\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{+}_{n,M}+\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{-}_{n,M}-2\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\pm}_{n,M}.

Establishing the following lemma constitutes the key step towards the proof of Proposition
2.12
.

Lemma 4.9
.

Assume that
B>0
.
Uniformly for all
0\leq M\leq m
we have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\left[{\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{n,M}\mid\mathfrak{G}_{n,M}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathcal{B}_{\beta}^{\otimes}(\pi^{\otimes}_{d,\beta,B,M/m})-\mathcal{B}_{\beta}^{\otimes}(\pi^{\otimes}_{d,\beta,B,0})+o(1)

w.h.p.

The proof of Lemma
4.9
proceeds in several steps.
We begin by disecting the log-terms that make up the three expressions
\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{n,M}^{+},\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{n,M}^{-},\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{n,M}^{\pm}
from (
2.11
).
Let
\boldsymbol{v}_{M},\boldsymbol{w}_{M}
be the two vertices incident with edge
\boldsymbol{e}_{M}
from step
CPL0
of the construction of
\mathbb{G}_{h,M}
.
Then given
\mathfrak{G}_{n,M}
the pair
(\boldsymbol{v}_{M},\boldsymbol{w}_{M})
is within total variation distance
o(1)
of a uniformly random pair of vertices from
V_{n}
.
Indeed, the only condition imposed on
(\boldsymbol{v}_{M},\boldsymbol{w}_{M})
is that
\boldsymbol{v}_{M}\neq\boldsymbol{w}_{M}
and that
\{\boldsymbol{v}_{M},\boldsymbol{w}_{M}\}
is not an edge of
\mathbb{G}_{1,M}\cup\mathbb{G}_{2,M}
already, and a uniformly random pair of vertices from
V_{n}
satisfies these two conditions w.h.p. Additionally, let
\boldsymbol{v}_{h,M},\boldsymbol{w}_{h,M}
be the vertices that constitute the edge
\boldsymbol{e}_{h,M,m-M}
from
CPL2
that gets added to
\mathbb{G}_{h,M}
to obtain
\mathbb{G}_{h,M}^{-}
.

Claim 4.10
.

Let
B>0
.
Then for every
0\leq M\leq m
w.h.p. we have

\displaystyle\log\frac{Z_{\mathbb{G}_{h,M}^{+}}(\beta,B)}{Z_{\mathbb{G}_{h,M}}(\beta,B)}

\displaystyle=\log\operatorname{ch}\beta+\log\left({1+\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{v}_{M})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}_{h,M}}}}\right\rangle\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{w}_{M})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}_{h,M}}}}\right\rangle\operatorname{th}\beta}\right)+o(1),

(4.29)

\displaystyle\log\frac{Z_{\mathbb{G}_{h,M}^{-}}(\beta,B)}{Z_{\mathbb{G}_{h,M}}(\beta,B)}

\displaystyle=\log\operatorname{ch}\beta+\log\left({1+\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{v}_{h,M})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}_{h,M}}}}\right\rangle\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{w}_{h,M})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}_{h,M}}}}\right\rangle\operatorname{th}\beta}\right)+o(1).

(4.30)

Proof.

According to Lemma
2.8
with probability
1-O(1/n)
we have

\displaystyle\log\frac{Z_{\mathbb{G}_{h,M}^{+}}(\beta,B)}{Z_{\mathbb{G}_{h,M}}(\beta,B)}

\displaystyle=\log\left\langle{{\exp\left({\beta\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{e}_{M})}\right)},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}_{h,M},\beta,B}}}\right\rangle=\log\operatorname{ch}\beta+\log\left({1+\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{v}_{M})\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{w}_{M})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}_{h,M}}}}\right\rangle\operatorname{th}\beta}\right).

(4.31)

Since given
\mathfrak{G}_{n,M}
the vertex pair
(\boldsymbol{v}_{M},\boldsymbol{w}_{M})
is asymptotically uniformly random, the replica symmetry property (
2.15
) from Proposition
2.10
implies that w.h.p.

\displaystyle\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{v}_{M})\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{w}_{M})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}_{h,M}}}}\right\rangle=\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{v}_{M})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}_{h,M}}}}\right\rangle\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{w}_{M})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}_{h,M}}}}\right\rangle+o(1).

(4.32)

Combining (
4.31
) and (
4.32
) yields (
4.29
)
Similar reasoning applies to
\boldsymbol{v}_{h,M},\boldsymbol{w}_{h,M}
, whence we obtain (
4.30
).
∎

Following (
1.7
), for a probability measure
\pi\in\mathcal{P}([-1,1]^{2})
we denote by
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\pi,i}^{(k)}=(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\pi,i,h}^{(k)})_{h=1,2}\in[-1,1]^{2}
mutually independent random variables with distribution
\pi
for
k\in\{0,1,2\}
and
i\geq 1
.
For
0\leq t\leq 1
we define

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Xi}^{+}_{t}

\displaystyle=\prod_{h=1}^{2}\log\operatorname{ch}\beta+\log\left({1+\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\pi_{d,\beta,B,t}^{\otimes},1,h}^{(0)}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\pi_{d,\beta,B,t}^{\otimes},2,h}^{(0)}\operatorname{th}\beta}\right),

(4.33)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Xi}^{-}_{t}

\displaystyle=\prod_{h=1}^{2}\log\operatorname{ch}\beta+\log\left({1+\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\pi_{d,\beta,B,t}^{\otimes},2h+1,h}^{(1)}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\pi_{d,\beta,B,t}^{\otimes},2h+2,h}^{(1)}\operatorname{th}\beta}\right),

(4.34)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Xi}^{\pm}_{t}

\displaystyle=\prod_{h=1}^{2}\log\operatorname{ch}\beta+\log\left({1+\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\pi_{d,\beta,B,t}^{\otimes},2h+1,h}^{(2)}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\pi_{d,\beta,B,t}^{\otimes},2h+2,h}^{(2)}\operatorname{th}\beta}\right),

(4.35)

and set
\boldsymbol{\Xi}_{t}=\boldsymbol{\Xi}^{+}_{t}+\boldsymbol{\Xi}^{-}_{t}-2\boldsymbol{\Xi}^{\pm}_{t}.

Let
\boldsymbol{\delta}_{n,M}^{+}
be the conditional distribution of the random variable
\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{n,M}^{+}
given
\mathfrak{G}_{n,M}
.
Thus, depending on the random graphs
(\mathbb{G}_{h,M})_{h=1,2}
,
\boldsymbol{\delta}_{n,M}^{+}
is a
random
probability distribution on
\mathbb{R}
.
Define
\boldsymbol{\delta}_{n,M}^{-}
and
\boldsymbol{\delta}_{n,M}^{\pm}
analogously with respect to
\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{n,M}^{-}
and
\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{n,M}^{\pm}
.
Furthermore, let
\xi^{+}_{t}
,
\xi_{t}^{-}
and
\xi_{t}^{\pm}
be the laws of the random variables
\Xi^{+}_{t},\Xi^{-}_{t}
and
\Xi^{\pm}_{t}
, respectively.
We are going to show that
\boldsymbol{\delta}_{n,M}^{+},\boldsymbol{\delta}_{n,M}^{-},\boldsymbol{\delta}_{n,M}^{\pm}
are close to
\xi_{t}^{+},\xi_{t}^{-},\xi_{t}^{\pm}
with
t=M/m
w.h.p.

Claim 4.11
.

Let
B>0
.
For all
0\leq M\leq m
we have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\left[{W_{1}(\boldsymbol{\delta}_{n,M}^{+},\xi_{M/m}^{+})+W_{1}(\boldsymbol{\delta}_{n,M}^{-},\xi_{M/m}^{-})+W_{1}(\boldsymbol{\delta}_{n,M}^{\pm},\xi_{M/m}^{\pm})}\right]

\displaystyle=o(1).

(4.36)

To be quite explicit, the expectation in (
4.36
) refers to the choice of
(\mathbb{G}_{h,M})_{h=1,2}
.
As far as the conditional distributions
\boldsymbol{\delta}_{n,M}^{+},\boldsymbol{\delta}_{n,M}^{-},\boldsymbol{\delta}_{n,M}^{\pm}
are concerned, the graphs
(\mathbb{G}_{h,M})_{h=1,2}
are deemed fixed and the only randomness stems from the extra edges added in the course of
CPL1
–
CPL2
to obtain
\mathbb{G}_{h,M}^{+}
and
\mathbb{G}_{h,M}^{-}
, respectively.

Proof of Claim
4.11
.

Since the vertex pair
(\boldsymbol{v}_{M},\boldsymbol{w}_{M})
is asymptotically uniformly random given
\mathfrak{G}_{n,M}
,
Eq. (
2.14
) from Proposition
2.10
show that w.h.p. given
\mathfrak{G}_{n,M}
the two pairs of magnetisations

\displaystyle\left({\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{v}_{M})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}_{h,M}}}}\right\rangle}\right)_{h=1,2},

\displaystyle\left({\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{w}_{M})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}_{h,M}}}}\right\rangle}\right)_{h=1,2}

converge in distribution to two independent samples from
\pi_{d,\beta,B,M/m}^{\otimes}
.
Hence, (
4.29
) implies that

\displaystyle W_{1}(\boldsymbol{\delta}_{n,M}^{+},\xi_{M/m}^{+})

\displaystyle=o(1)

w.h.p.

Analogously, we obtain
W_{1}(\boldsymbol{\delta}_{n,M}^{-},\xi_{M/m}^{-}),W_{1}(\boldsymbol{\delta}_{n,M}^{\pm},\xi_{M/m}^{\pm})=o(1)
w.h.p. ∎

Proof of Lemma
4.9
.

Combing Claims
4.10
and
4.11
, we see that w.h.p.

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\left[{\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{n,M}\mid\mathfrak{G}_{n,M}}\right]

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\left[{\boldsymbol{\Xi}_{M/m}}\right]+o(1).

(4.37)

Furthermore, inspecting the definition (
4.33
) and reminding ourselves of (
1.10
), we calculate

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\left[{\boldsymbol{\Xi}_{M/m}^{+}}\right]

\displaystyle=\log^{2}\operatorname{ch}\beta+2{\mathbb{E}}\left[{\log\left({1+\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\pi_{d,\beta,t}^{\otimes},1,1}^{(0)}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\pi_{d,\beta,t}^{\otimes},2,1}^{(0)}\operatorname{th}\beta}\right)}\right]\log\operatorname{ch}\beta+\mathcal{B}_{\beta}^{\otimes}(\pi_{d,\beta,B,M/m}^{\otimes}).

(4.38)

Similarly, we obtain

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\left[{\boldsymbol{\Xi}_{M/m}^{-}}\right]

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\left[{\boldsymbol{\Xi}_{M/m}^{-}}\right]

\displaystyle=\log^{2}\operatorname{ch}\beta+2{\mathbb{E}}\left[{\log\left({1+\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\pi_{d,\beta,t}^{\otimes},1,1}^{(0)}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\pi_{d,\beta,t}^{\otimes},2,1}^{(0)}\operatorname{th}\beta}\right)}\right]\log\operatorname{ch}\beta+{\mathbb{E}}\left[{\log\left({1+\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\pi_{d,\beta,t}^{\otimes},1,1}^{(0)}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\pi_{d,\beta,t}^{\otimes},2,1}^{(0)}\operatorname{th}\beta}\right)}\right]^{2}

\displaystyle=\log^{2}\operatorname{ch}\beta+2{\mathbb{E}}\left[{\log\left({1+\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\pi_{d,\beta,t}^{\otimes},1,1}^{(0)}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\pi_{d,\beta,t}^{\otimes},2,1}^{(0)}\operatorname{th}\beta}\right)}\right]\log\operatorname{ch}\beta+{\mathbb{E}}\left[{\log\left({1+\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\pi_{d,\beta,0}^{\otimes},1,1}^{(0)}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\pi_{d,\beta,0}^{\otimes},2,1}^{(0)}\operatorname{th}\beta}\right)}\right]^{2}

\displaystyle=\log^{2}\operatorname{ch}\beta+2{\mathbb{E}}\left[{\log\left({1+\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\pi_{d,\beta,t}^{\otimes},1,1}^{(0)}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\pi_{d,\beta,t}^{\otimes},2,1}^{(0)}\operatorname{th}\beta}\right)}\right]\log\operatorname{ch}\beta+\mathcal{B}_{\beta}^{\otimes}(\pi_{d,\beta,B,0}^{\otimes}).

(4.39)

Combining (
4.37
)–(
4.39
) completes the proof.
∎

Corollary 4.12
.

If
B>0
then
\mathrm{Var}\sum_{0\leq M<m}{\mathbb{E}}\left[{\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{n,M}\mid\mathfrak{F}_{n,M}}\right]=o(m^{2})
.

Proof.

We expand

\displaystyle\mathrm{Var}\sum_{0\leq M<m}{\mathbb{E}}\left[{\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{n,M}\mid\mathfrak{F}_{n,M}}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{0\leq M<m}\mathrm{Var}\left({{\mathbb{E}}\left[{\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{n,M}\mid\mathfrak{F}_{n,M}}\right]}\right)

\displaystyle+2\sum_{0\leq M<M^{\prime}<m}{\mathbb{E}}\left[{{\mathbb{E}}\left[{\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{n,M}\mid\mathfrak{F}_{n,M}}\right]{\mathbb{E}}\left[{\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{n,M^{\prime}}\mid\mathfrak{F}_{n,M^{\prime}}}\right]}\right]-{\mathbb{E}}\left[{\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{n,M}}\right]{\mathbb{E}}\left[{\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{n,M^{\prime}}}\right].

(4.40)

Since the random variables
\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{n,M}
are bounded uniformly, we immediately obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{0\leq M<m}\mathrm{Var}\left({{\mathbb{E}}\left[{\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{n,M}\mid\mathfrak{F}_{n,M}}\right]}\right)

\displaystyle=O(m).

(4.41)

To bound the second summand from (
4.40
) fix
0\leq M<M^{\prime}\leq m
.
Since
\mathfrak{F}_{n,M}\subseteq\mathfrak{G}_{n,M}
, Claim
4.11
implies that

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\left[{\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{n,M}\mid\mathfrak{F}_{n,M}}\right]

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\left[{\boldsymbol{\Xi}_{n,M/m}}\right]+o(1),

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\left[{\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{n,M^{\prime}}\mid\mathfrak{F}_{n,M^{\prime}}}\right]

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\left[{\boldsymbol{\Xi}_{n,M^{\prime}/m}}\right]+o(1)

w.h.p.

(4.42)

Furthermore, taking expectations of the l.h.s. expressions from (
4.42
), we get

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\left[{\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{n,M}}\right]

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\left[{\boldsymbol{\Xi}_{n,M/m}}\right]+o(1),

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\left[{\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{n,M^{\prime}}}\right]

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\left[{\boldsymbol{\Xi}_{n,M^{\prime}/m}}\right]+o(1).

(4.43)

Combining (
4.42
)–(
4.43
), we arrive at the bound

\displaystyle\sum_{0\leq M<M^{\prime}<m}{\mathbb{E}}\left[{{\mathbb{E}}\left[{\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{n,M}\mid\mathfrak{F}_{n,M}}\right]{\mathbb{E}}\left[{\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{n,M^{\prime}}\mid\mathfrak{F}_{n,M^{\prime}}}\right]}\right]-{\mathbb{E}}\left[{\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{n,M}}\right]{\mathbb{E}}\left[{\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{n,M^{\prime}}}\right]

\displaystyle=o(m^{2}).

(4.44)

Finally, the assertion follows from (
4.40
), (
4.41
) and (
4.44
).
∎

Proof of Proposition
2.12
.

Since the random variables
\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{n,M}
are all bounded uniformly, Lemma
4.9
yields

\displaystyle\frac{1}{m}\sum_{0\leq M<m}{\mathbb{E}}\left[{\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{n,M}}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{0\leq M<m}\mathcal{B}_{\beta}^{\otimes}(\pi^{\otimes}_{d,\beta,B,M/m})-\mathcal{B}_{\beta}^{\otimes}(\pi^{\otimes}_{d,\beta,B,0})+o(1).

Due to the continuity statement from Proposition
2.9
we can replace the sum on the r.h.s. by an integral to obtain

\displaystyle\frac{1}{m}\sum_{0\leq M<m}{\mathbb{E}}\left[{\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{n,M}}\right]

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\mathcal{B}_{\beta}^{\otimes}(\pi^{\otimes}_{d,\beta,B,t}){\mathrm{d}}t-\mathcal{B}_{\beta}^{\otimes}(\pi^{\otimes}_{d,\beta,B,0})+o(1).

(4.45)

Further, (
4.45
) implies together with Corollary
4.12
and Chebyshev’s inequality that

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\left|{\int_{0}^{1}\mathcal{B}_{\beta}^{\otimes}(\pi^{\otimes}_{d,\beta,B,t}){\mathrm{d}}t-\mathcal{B}_{\beta}^{\otimes}(\pi^{\otimes}_{d,\beta,B,0})-\frac{1}{m}\sum_{0\leq M<m}\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{n,M}}\right|

\displaystyle=o(1).

(4.46)

Thus, the assertion follows from (
4.46
) and Lemma
2.7
.
∎

4.9.
Proof of Proposition
2.13

The fundamental reason why the random variable
\log Z_{\mathbb{G}}(\beta,B)
has variance
\Omega(n)
is that the limiting distribution
\pi_{d,\beta,B}
of the vertex magnetisations fails to concentrate on a single point, as verified by the following lemma.

Lemma 4.13
.

Assume that
B>0
.
Then
\pi_{d,\beta,B}
is not supported on a single point.

Proof.

We produce two distinct points that belong to the support of
\pi_{d,\beta,B}
.
With probability
\exp(-d)
the Galton-Watson tree
\mathbb{T}
consists of the root only.
In this case we have
\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\varrho)},{\mu_{\mathbb{T},\varrho}^{(\ell)}}\rangle}=\operatorname{th}B
for all
\ell\geq 1
.
Hence,

\displaystyle\operatorname{th}B\in{\text{supp}\left(\pi_{d,\beta,B}\right)}.

(4.47)

Further, with probability
d\exp(-2d)
the tree
\mathbb{T}
consists of the root
\varrho
and a single child
\theta
(and the edge
\varrho\theta
, of course).
A direct calculation shows that in this case we have

\displaystyle\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\varrho)},{\mu_{\mathbb{T},\varrho}^{(\ell)}}}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\eta(\beta,B)=\frac{\exp(2(\beta+B))-\exp(2(\beta-B))}{2+\exp(2(\beta+B))+\exp(2(\beta-B))}.

(4.48)

The function
\eta(\beta,B)
satisfies
\eta(0,B)=\operatorname{th}B
and
\frac{\partial}{\partial\beta}\eta(\beta,B)>0
.
Hence, (
4.47
) and (
4.48
) demonstrate that the two distinct points
\operatorname{th}B,\eta(\beta,B)
belong to the support of
\pi_{d,\beta,B}
.
∎

To show that the integral
\Sigma(d,\beta,B)=\int_{0}^{1}\mathcal{B}^{\otimes}_{\beta}(\pi^{\otimes}_{d,\beta,B,t})-\mathcal{B}^{\otimes}_{\beta,B,0}(\pi^{\otimes}_{d,\beta,B,0}){\mathrm{d}}t
from (
1.12
) is strictly positive, we first ascertain that the function being integrated is non-negative for all
t
.

Lemma 4.14
.

Assume that
B>0
.
Then
\mathcal{B}^{\otimes}_{\beta}(\pi^{\otimes}_{d,\beta,B,t})\geq\mathcal{B}^{\otimes}_{\beta}(\pi^{\otimes}_{d,\beta,B,0})
for all
0\leq t\leq 1
.

Proof.

Fix any
\varepsilon>0
.
Lemma
2.7
and Lemma
4.9
show that for sufficiently large
n
for all
0\leq M\leq m
we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{\beta}^{\otimes}(\pi^{\otimes}_{d,\beta,B,M/m})-\mathcal{B}_{\beta}^{\otimes}(\pi^{\otimes}_{d,\beta,B,0})\geq-\varepsilon.

(4.49)

Since Proposition
2.9
provides that the function
t\in[0,1]\mapsto\mathcal{B}_{\beta}^{\otimes}(\pi^{\otimes}_{d,\beta,B,t})
is continuous and thus uniformly continuous, the assertion follows from (
4.49
).
∎

Finally, we use a convexity argument to prove that the function value is strictly positive at
t=1
.

Lemma 4.15
.

Assume that
B>0
.
Then
\mathcal{B}^{\otimes}_{\beta}(\pi^{\otimes}_{d,\beta,B,1})>\mathcal{B}^{\otimes}_{\beta}(\pi^{\otimes}_{d,\beta,B,0})
for all
0\leq t\leq 1
.

Proof.

We already noticed in Remark
1.4
that
\pi^{\otimes}_{d,\beta,B,0}=\pi_{d,\beta,B}\otimes\pi_{d,\beta,B}
and that
\pi_{d,\beta,B,1}
is precisely the distribution of the ‘diagonal’ pair
(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\pi_{d,\beta,B},1},\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\pi_{d,\beta,B},1})\in[0,1]^{2}
.
Hence, evaluating the expression (
1.10
), we obtain

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{\beta}^{\otimes}(\pi_{d,\beta,B,0}^{\otimes})

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\left[{\log\left({1+\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\pi_{d,\beta,B},1}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\pi_{d,\beta,B},2}\operatorname{th}\beta}\right)}\right]^{2},

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{\beta}^{\otimes}(\pi_{d,\beta,B,1}^{\otimes})

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\left[{\log^{2}\left({1+\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\pi_{d,\beta,B},1}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\pi_{d,\beta,B},2}\operatorname{th}\beta}\right)}\right].

(4.50)

Therefore, Lemma
4.13
and Jensen’s inequality yield
\mathcal{B}^{\otimes}_{\beta}(\pi^{\otimes}_{d,\beta,B,1})>\mathcal{B}^{\otimes}_{\beta}(\pi^{\otimes}_{d,\beta,B,0})
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
2.13
.

The proposition follows from Lemmas
4.13
–
4.15
and the continuity of the function
t\mapsto\mathcal{B}^{\otimes}_{\beta}(\pi^{\otimes}_{d,\beta,B,t})
established by Proposition
2.9
.
∎

5.
Proof of Theorem
1.3

5.1.
Proof of Proposition
2.14

Part (i) of Proposition
2.14
follows directly from Lemma
4.2
, which covers all
B\geq 0
.

To prove Proposition
2.14
(iii) we observe the following.

Lemma 5.1
.

For all
d,\beta>0
we have
\pi_{d,\beta,0}(\{0\})\in\{\rho_{d},1\}
.

Proof.

The distribution
\pi_{d,\beta,0}
is a fixed point of the operator
\mathrm{BP}_{d,\beta,0}
from (
1.1
), i.e.,
\pi_{d,\beta,0}=\mathrm{BP}_{d,\beta,0}(\pi_{d,\beta,0})
.
Since
{\text{supp}\left(\pi_{d,\beta,0}\right)}\subseteq[0,1]
by Proposition
2.14
(i), the definition (
1.2
) of
\mathrm{BP}_{d,\beta,0}
implies that

\displaystyle\pi_{d,\beta,0}(\{0\})

\displaystyle={\mathbb{P}}\left[{\sum_{s\in\{\pm 1\}}s\prod_{i=1}^{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}\left({1+s\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\pi_{d,\beta,0},i}\operatorname{th}\beta}\right)=0}\right]

\displaystyle={\mathbb{P}}\left[{\max_{1\leq i\leq\boldsymbol{\Delta}}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\pi_{d,\beta,0}}=0}\right]=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}{\mathbb{P}}\left[{{\rm Po}(d)=j}\right]\pi_{d,\beta,0}(\{0\})=\exp(d(\pi_{d,\beta,0}(\{0\})-1)).

(5.1)

Furthermore, the fixed points of the convex function
z\mapsto\exp(d(z-1))
are precisely
z=\rho_{d}
and
z=1
.
Thus, the assertion follows from (
5.1
).
∎

Hence, to prove Proposition
2.14
(ii) we are left to rule out that
\pi_{d,\beta}=\delta_{0}
for
d>1
and
\beta>\beta_{\mathrm{f}}(d)
.

Lemma 5.2
.

If
d>1
and
\beta>\beta_{\mathrm{f}}(d)
then
\int_{0}^{1}z{\mathrm{d}}\pi_{d,\beta,0}(z)>0
.

Proof.

Recalling that
\rho_{d}
denotes the probability that the
{\rm Po}(d)
-Galton-Watson process dies out, we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}z{\mathrm{d}}\pi_{d,\beta,0}(z)

\displaystyle=\lim_{\ell\to\infty}\int_{0}^{1}z{\mathrm{d}}\pi_{d,\beta,0}^{(\ell)}(z)

[as
\lim_{\ell\to\infty}\pi_{d,\beta,0}^{(\ell)}=\pi_{d,\beta,0}
weakly]

\displaystyle=\lim_{\ell\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}\left[{\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\varrho)},{\mu_{\mathbb{T},\varrho,\beta,0}^{(\ell)}}}\right\rangle}\right]

[by Corollary
2.4
]

\displaystyle\geq(1-\rho_{d})\lim_{\ell\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}\left[{\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\varrho)},{\mu_{\mathbb{T},\varrho,\beta,0}^{(\ell)}}}\right\rangle\mid\mathbb{T}\mbox{ is infinite}}\right]>0

\displaystyle\mbox{[by Lemmas~\ref{lem_LYonORg} and~\ref{cl_BrNumOfT}]},

as desired.
∎

Thus, Proposition
2.14
(ii) follows from Lemmas
5.1
and
5.2
.

Finally, part (iii) is a direct consequence of the following lemma from
[
11
]
.

Lemma 5.3

(
[
11
, Lemma 3.3]
)
.

The function
\beta\mapsto r_{d,\beta}\in[0,1)
is monotonically increasing and right-continuous.
Moreover, at any continuity point
\beta\in(0,\infty)
of this function the identity (
2.17
) holds.

Remark 5.4
.

The expression displayed in
[
11
, Lemma 3.3]
differs syntactically from
r_{d,\beta}
as defined in (
1.16
) because
[
11
]
deals with general unimodular trees.
The identity of the two expressions has been verified explicitly in
[
23
, Eq. (6.11)]
.

5.2.
Proof of Proposition
2.15

In order to prove Proposition
2.15
we pursue the following strategy.
We know from Lemma
3.9
that the measure
\mu_{\mathbb{G},\beta,0}
admits
some
‘pure state decomposition’, with a potentially large (but bounded, in terms of
\delta
) number of ‘pure states’
\mathcal{S}_{\tau}
such that
\mu_{\mathbb{G},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{\tau})
is
\delta
-symmetric.
What we need to show is that actually these many
\mathcal{S}_{\tau}
amalgamate into only two pure states, namely
\mathcal{S}_{+}
and
\mathcal{S}_{-}
, and that the empirical distribution of the conditional marginals given
\mathcal{S}_{+}
converges to
\pi_{d,\beta,0}
.
To this end we will deduce from Proposition
2.14
(ii) that w.h.p. for ‘most’ of the pure states
\mathcal{S}_{\tau}
from Lemma
3.9
and for ‘most’
v\in V_{n}
for large
\ell
the conditional magnetisation
\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v)},{\mu_{\mathbb{G},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{\tau})}}\right\rangle
is close to
s_{v}\cdot\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v)},{\mu_{\mathbb{G},v,\beta,0}^{(\ell)}}\rangle}
for a sign
s_{v}\in\{\pm 1\}
;
recall from (
1.21
) that
\mu_{\mathbb{G},v,\beta,0}^{(\ell)}
signifies the Ising distribution with all-ones boundary condition imposed on vertices at distance
\ell
from
v
.
In particular,
\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v)},{\mu_{\mathbb{G},v,\beta,0}^{(\ell)}}\rangle}
is determined exclusively by the local geometry of
\mathbb{G}
around
v
.
Additionally, we are going to show that the sign
s_{v}=s_{\tau}
actually depends on the pure state
\mathcal{S}_{\tau}
only, and not on the vertex
v
.
In other words, the signs of the magnetisations are ‘consistent’ within the pure states
\mathcal{S}_{\tau}
.
Once these facts are established, we are going to invoke the enhanced cut metric triangle inequality from Lemma
3.8
to show that all the
\mathcal{S}_{\tau}
with positive magnetisation amalgamate into the single pure state
\mathcal{S}_{+}
whose empirical magnetisations converge to
\pi_{d,\beta,0}
.

To carry this programme out we first need to find a way to get from Proposition
2.14
(ii), which deals with
edge
magnetisations, back to vertex magnetisations.
As a first step we will derive an upper bound on the edge magnetisations that comes purely in terms of the local geometry of the random graph.
This Belief Propagation-inspired bound is fairly general and can actually be stated for arbitrary graphs
G=(V,E)
.
To elaborate, we introduce the following mild extension of the definition (
1.21
) of the Boltzmann distribution
\mu_{G,r,\beta,B}^{(\ell)}
with a
+1
‘boundary condition’ imposed at distance
\ell
from vertex
r
.
Namely, for an edge
e=vw\in E
define

\displaystyle\mathfrak{m}^{(\ell)}_{G,v\to w,\beta}

\displaystyle=\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v)},{\mu_{G-e,v,\beta,0}^{(\ell)}}}\right\rangle\in[0,1).

(5.2)

Thus, we obtain
G-e
from
G
by removing the edge
e
, then impose a
+1
‘boundary condition’ on all vertices
u
at distance precisely
\ell
from
v
in
G-e
and finally evaluate the magnetisation of
v
.
The definition (
5.2
) is similar to the idea of a ‘standard Belief Propagation message’
[
20
,
48
]
.
We obtain the following bound.

Lemma 5.5
.

Let
\ell\geq 1
be an integer, let
G=(V,E)
be a graph and let
e=vw\in E
be an edge such that the distance between
v
and
w
in
G-e
exceeds
2\ell
.
Then

\displaystyle\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(e)},{\mu_{G,\beta,0}}}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\mathfrak{m}_{G,v\to w,\beta}^{(\ell)}\mathfrak{m}_{G,w\to v,\beta}^{(\ell)}+\operatorname{th}\beta}{1+\mathfrak{m}_{G,v\to w,\beta}^{(\ell)}\mathfrak{m}_{G,w\to v,\beta}^{(\ell)}\operatorname{th}\beta}

Proof.

As a direct consequence of the definition (
1.19
)–(
1.20
) of the Ising model we obtain

\displaystyle\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(e)},{\mu_{G,\beta,0}}}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(e)\exp(\beta\boldsymbol{\sigma}(e))},{\mu_{G-e,\beta,0}}}\right\rangle}{\left\langle{{\exp(\beta\boldsymbol{\sigma}(e))},{\mu_{G-e,\beta,0}}}\right\rangle}=\frac{\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(e)},{\mu_{G-e,\beta,0}}}\right\rangle+\operatorname{th}\beta}{1+\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(e)},{\mu_{G-e,\beta,0}}}\right\rangle\operatorname{th}\beta}.

(5.3)

Thus, since the function
x\in[-1,1]\mapsto\frac{x+\operatorname{th}\beta}{1+x\operatorname{th}\beta}
is monotonically increasing, we are left to show that

\displaystyle\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(e)},{\mu_{G-e,\beta,0}}}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\leq\mathfrak{m}_{G,v\to w,\beta}^{(\ell)}\mathfrak{m}_{G,w\to v,\beta}^{(\ell)}.

(5.4)

To this end let
\mu_{G-e,e,\beta}^{(\ell)}
be the Ising distribution with a
+1
boundary condition imposed on all vertices that either have distance precisely
\ell
from
v
or from
w
; in symbols,

\displaystyle\mu_{G-e,e,\beta}^{(\ell)}(\sigma)

\displaystyle\propto\mathbb{1}\left\{{\forall u\in\partial^{\ell}(G-e,v)\cup\partial^{\ell}(G-e,w):\sigma(u)=1}\right\}\exp\left({\beta\sum_{f\in E(G-e)}\sigma(f)}\right).

Since the distance between
v,w
in
G-e
exceeds
2\ell
, Lemma
3.2
shows that

\displaystyle\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(e)},{\mu_{G-e,\beta,0}}}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\leq\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(e)},{\mu_{G-e,e,\beta}^{(\ell)}}}\right\rangle.

(5.5)

Moreover, again because the distance between
v,w
in
G-e
exceeds
2\ell
, the spins
\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v),\boldsymbol{\sigma}(w)
are stochastically independent once we impose a
+1
boundary condition on
\partial^{\ell}(G-e,v)\cup\partial^{\ell}(G-e,w)
.
Therefore,

\displaystyle\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(e)},{\mu_{G-e,e,\beta}^{(\ell)}}}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v)},{\mu_{G-e,e,\beta}^{(\ell)}}}\right\rangle\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(w)},{\mu_{G-e,e,\beta}^{(\ell)}}}\right\rangle=\mathfrak{m}_{G,v\to w,\beta}^{(\ell)}\mathfrak{m}_{G,w\to v,\beta}^{(\ell)}\kern 5.0pt.

(5.6)

Combining (
5.5
)–(
5.6
) yields (
5.4
), thereby completing the proof.
∎

As a next step we are going to prove that the upper bound from Lemma
5.5
is saturated for most edges of the random graph.
For symmetry reasons it suffices to consider the ‘last’ edge
\boldsymbol{e}_{m}=\boldsymbol{v}\boldsymbol{w}
of
\mathbb{G}=\mathbb{G}(n,m)
.

Lemma 5.6
.

Assume that
\beta\in(0,\infty)\setminus\mathfrak{X}_{d}
.
Then for any
\varepsilon>0
there exists
\ell_{0}=\ell_{0}(\varepsilon,d,\beta)>0
such that for all
\ell>\ell_{0}
,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\left|{\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{e}_{m})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G},\beta,0}}}\right\rangle-\frac{\mathfrak{m}_{\mathbb{G},\boldsymbol{v}\to\boldsymbol{w},\beta}^{(\ell)}\mathfrak{m}_{\mathbb{G},\boldsymbol{w}\to\boldsymbol{v},\beta}^{(\ell)}+\operatorname{th}\beta}{1+\mathfrak{m}_{\mathbb{G},\boldsymbol{v}\to\boldsymbol{w},\beta}^{(\ell)}\mathfrak{m}_{\mathbb{G},\boldsymbol{w}\to\boldsymbol{v},\beta}^{(\ell)}\operatorname{th}\beta}}\right|

\displaystyle<\varepsilon+o(1).

(5.7)

Proof.

The starting point for the proof is provided by (
2.17
) and the definition (
1.16
) of
r_{d,\beta}
.
Indeed, because the distribution of
\mathbb{G}
is invariant under edge permutations, (
2.17
) can be rewritten as

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\left[{\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{e}_{m})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G},\beta,0}}}\right\rangle}\right]

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\left[{\frac{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\pi_{d,\beta,0},1}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\pi_{d,\beta,0},2}+\operatorname{th}\beta}{1+\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\pi_{d,\beta,0},1}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\pi_{d,\beta,0},2}\operatorname{th}\beta}}\right]+o(1).

(5.8)

Further, since
\pi_{d,\beta,0}
is the
W_{1}
-limit of the measures
\pi_{d,\beta,0}^{(\ell)}
from (
2.5
), (
5.8
) implies that for
\ell>\ell_{0}
large enough,

\displaystyle\left|{{\mathbb{E}}\left[{\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{e}_{m})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G},\beta,0}}}\right\rangle}\right]-{\mathbb{E}}\left[{\frac{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\pi_{d,\beta,0}^{(\ell)},1}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\pi_{d,\beta,0}^{(\ell)},2}+\operatorname{th}\beta}{1+\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\pi_{d,\beta,0}^{(\ell)},1}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\pi_{d,\beta,0}^{(\ell)},2}\operatorname{th}\beta}}\right]}\right|<\varepsilon+o(1).

(5.9)

Let
\mathbb{G}^{\prime}=\mathbb{G}(n,m-1)
be the graph comprising the random edges
\boldsymbol{e}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{e}_{m-1}
.
To derive (
5.7
) from (
5.9
), we remind ourselves that given
\mathbb{G}^{\prime}
the pair of vertices
\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{w}
that constitutes
\boldsymbol{e}_{m}
is within total variation distance
o(1)
of a uniformly random pair of vertices from
V_{n}
.
Therefore, Lemma
2.2
shows that w.h.p. given
\mathbb{G}^{\prime}
the depth-
\ell
neighbourhoods
\nabla^{\ell}(\mathbb{G},\boldsymbol{v})
,
\nabla^{\ell}(\mathbb{G},\boldsymbol{w})
are within total variation distance
o(1)
of a pair
\mathbb{T}^{(\ell)},\mathbb{T}^{\prime\,(\ell)}
of independent
{\rm Po}(d)
Galton-Watson trees truncated at depth
\ell
.
Hence, recalling the definitions (
5.2
) of
\mathfrak{m}_{\mathbb{G},\boldsymbol{v}\to\boldsymbol{w},\beta}^{(\ell)},\mathfrak{m}_{\mathbb{G},\boldsymbol{w}\to\boldsymbol{v},\beta}^{(\ell)}
and applying Corollary
2.4
, we obtain

\displaystyle W_{1}\left({\left({\mathfrak{m}_{\mathbb{G},\boldsymbol{v}\to\boldsymbol{w},\beta}^{(\ell)},\mathfrak{m}_{\mathbb{G},\boldsymbol{w}\to\boldsymbol{v},\beta}^{(\ell)}}\right),\left({\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\pi_{d,\beta,0}^{(\ell)},1},\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\pi_{d,\beta,0}^{(\ell)},2}}\right)}\right)

\displaystyle=o(1).

(5.10)

Combining (
5.9
) and (
5.10
), we find

\displaystyle\left|{{\mathbb{E}}\left[{\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{e}_{m})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G},\beta,0}}}\right\rangle}\right]-{\mathbb{E}}\left[{\frac{\mathfrak{m}_{\mathbb{G},\boldsymbol{v}\to\boldsymbol{w},\beta}^{(\ell)}\mathfrak{m}_{\mathbb{G},\boldsymbol{w}\to\boldsymbol{v},\beta}^{(\ell)}+\operatorname{th}\beta}{1+\mathfrak{m}_{\mathbb{G},\boldsymbol{v}\to\boldsymbol{w},\beta}^{(\ell)}\mathfrak{m}_{\mathbb{G},\boldsymbol{w}\to\boldsymbol{v},\beta}^{(\ell)}\operatorname{th}\beta}}\right]}\right|<\varepsilon+o(1).

(5.11)

Moreover, since
\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{w}
have distance
\Omega(\log n)
in
\mathbb{G}(n,m-1)
w.h.p., Lemma
5.5
implies the upper bound

\displaystyle\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{e}_{m})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G},\beta,0}}}\right\rangle\leq\frac{\mathfrak{m}_{\mathbb{G},\boldsymbol{v}\to\boldsymbol{w},\beta}^{(\ell)}\mathfrak{m}_{\mathbb{G},\boldsymbol{w}\to\boldsymbol{v},\beta}^{(\ell)}+\operatorname{th}\beta}{1+\mathfrak{m}_{\mathbb{G},\boldsymbol{v}\to\boldsymbol{w},\beta}^{(\ell)}\mathfrak{m}_{\mathbb{G},\boldsymbol{w}\to\boldsymbol{v},\beta}^{(\ell)}\operatorname{th}\beta}

w.h.p.

(5.12)

Combining (
5.11
) and (
5.12
) completes the proof.
∎

We are going to use Lemma
5.6
in order to investigate the conditional magnetisations of the ‘pure states’
\mathcal{S}_{\tau}
provided by Lemma
3.9
;
recall from (
3.3
) that for
U\subseteq V_{n}
and
\tau\in\{\pm 1\}^{U}
we let
\mathcal{S}_{\tau}=\left\{{\sigma\in\{\pm 1\}^{V_{n}}:\forall u\in U:\sigma(u)=\tau(u)}\right\}
.
We continue to denote by
\boldsymbol{e}_{m}=\boldsymbol{v}\boldsymbol{w}
the final edge of
\mathbb{G}=\mathbb{G}(n,m)
and we let
\mathbb{G}^{\prime}=\mathbb{G}(n,m-1)=\mathbb{G}-\boldsymbol{e}_{m}
.
Furthermore, given
\delta>0
let
\boldsymbol{U}_{\delta}\subseteq V_{n}
be the random set from Lemma
3.9
applied to
\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}
.
As a final preparation we need the following basic random graphs fact.

Lemma 5.7
.

Assume that
d>1
and
\beta\in(\beta_{\mathrm{f}}(d),\infty)\setminus\mathfrak{X}_{d}
.
Then for any fixed
\delta,\ell>0
the event
\mathfrak{D}_{\delta,\ell}
that

(i)

the distance between
\boldsymbol{v}
and
\boldsymbol{w}
in
\mathbb{G}^{\prime}
exceeds
2\ell+2
,

(ii)

the distance between
\boldsymbol{v}
and the set
\boldsymbol{U}_{\delta}
in
\mathbb{G}^{\prime}
exceeds
\ell+1
, and

(iii)

the distance between
\boldsymbol{w}
and the set
\boldsymbol{U}_{\delta}
in
\mathbb{G}^{\prime}
exceeds
\ell+1
.

has probability
{\mathbb{P}}\left[{\mathfrak{D}_{\delta,\ell}}\right]=1-o(1).

Proof.

We exploit the fact that given
\mathbb{G}^{\prime}
the pair
(\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{w})
is within total variation distance
o(1)
of a uniformly random pair
(\boldsymbol{v}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{w}^{\prime})
of vertices from
V_{n}
that is chosen independently of
\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{U}_{\delta}
.
Hence, it suffices to prove that (i)–(iii) above hold for the pair
(\boldsymbol{v}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{w}^{\prime})
w.h.p. A routine first moment calculation shows that for any fixed vertex
u\in V_{n}
and any
1\leq l\leq\ell
the expected number of paths of length
l
from
\boldsymbol{v}
to
u
is bounded from above by
(1+o(1))d^{l}/n=O(1/n)
.
Therefore, the probability that
\boldsymbol{v}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{w}^{\prime}
are within distance
\ell
is
O(1/n)
.
