Similarly, since
|\boldsymbol{U}_{\delta}|=O(1)
(because the size of
\boldsymbol{U}_{\delta}
depends on
\delta
but not on
n
), the event that either
\boldsymbol{v}^{\prime}
or
\boldsymbol{w}^{\prime}
is within distance
\ell
of some vertex
u\in\boldsymbol{U}_{\delta}
has probability
O(1/n)
.
∎

Beginning with the investigation of the ‘pure states’ proper, we are now going to show that with probability close to one for ‘most’
\mathcal{S}_{\tau}
the magnetisation
\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{e}_{m})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{\tau})}}\right\rangle
of the edge
\boldsymbol{e}_{m}
approximately equals the product
\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{v})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{\tau})}}\right\rangle\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{w})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{\tau})}}\right\rangle
of the vertex magnetisations.
In the proofs of the following lemmas we will occasionally condition on
\mathbb{G}^{\prime}
and
\boldsymbol{U}_{\delta}
.
We emphasise that given
\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{U}_{\delta}
the only remaining randomness stems from the choice of
\boldsymbol{e}_{m}=\boldsymbol{v}\boldsymbol{w}
.

Lemma 5.8
.

Assume that
d>1
and
\beta\in(\beta_{\mathrm{f}}(d),\infty)\setminus\mathfrak{X}_{d}
.
For any
\varepsilon>0
there is
\delta_{0}=\delta_{0}(\varepsilon)>0
such that for any
0<\delta<\delta_{0}(\varepsilon)
the following is true.
Let

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=\left\{{\tau\in\{\pm 1\}^{\boldsymbol{U}_{\delta}}:\left|{\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{e}_{m})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{\tau})}}\right\rangle-\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{v})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{\tau})}}\right\rangle\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{w})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{\tau})}}\right\rangle}\right|<\varepsilon}\right\}

and

\displaystyle\mathfrak{S}_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=\left\{{\sum_{\tau\in\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}}\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}(\mathcal{S}_{\tau})>1-\varepsilon}\right\}.

Then
{\mathbb{P}}\left[{\mathfrak{S}_{\varepsilon}}\right]\geq 1-\varepsilon-o(1)
.

Proof.

Assuming
n
is sufficiently large, Lemma
3.9
shows that with probability at least
1-\delta
the subcube decomposition
(\mathcal{S}_{\tau})_{\tau\in\{\pm 1\}^{\boldsymbol{U}_{\delta}}}
satisfies

\displaystyle\sum_{\tau\in\{\pm 1\}^{\boldsymbol{U}_{\delta}}}\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}(\mathcal{S}_{\tau})\mathbb{1}\left\{{\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{\tau})\mbox{ is $\delta$-symmetric\,}}\right\}>1-\delta.

(5.13)

Given
\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{U}_{\delta}
the vertex pair
(\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{w})
is within total variation distance
o(1)
of a uniformly random pair of vertices.
Therefore, the definition (
3.8
) of
\delta
-symmetry ensures that for any
\tau\in\{\pm 1\}^{\boldsymbol{U}_{\delta}}
such that
\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{\tau})
is
\delta
-symmetric we have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\left[{\left|{\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{v}\boldsymbol{w})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{\tau})}}\right\rangle-\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{v})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{\tau})}}\right\rangle\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{w})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{\tau})}}\right\rangle}\right|\mid\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{U}_{\delta}}\right]

\displaystyle<2\delta+o(1).

(5.14)

Thus, the assertion follows from (
5.13
), (
5.14
) and Markov’s inequality.
∎

Next we combine Lemmas
5.5
–
5.8
with Lemma
3.2
to show that likely for ‘most’
\tau
the product

\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{v})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{\tau})}}\right\rangle\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{w})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{\tau})}}\right\rangle

of the conditional magnetisations is close to the product

\displaystyle\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{v})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}^{(\ell)}}}\right\rangle\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{w})},{\mu^{(\ell)}_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}}}\right\rangle

of the magnetisations with the all-
1
boundary condition imposed at distance
\ell
.
Crucially, the latter depends only on the topology of
\mathbb{G}^{\prime}
around
\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{w}
but not on
\tau
.

Lemma 5.9
.

Assume that
d>1
and
\beta\in(\beta_{\mathrm{f}}(d),\infty)\setminus\mathfrak{X}_{d}
.
For any
\varepsilon>0
there exist
\delta_{0}=\delta_{0}(d,\beta,\varepsilon)>0
,
\ell_{0}=\ell_{0}(d,\beta,\varepsilon)
such that for
0<\delta<\delta_{0}(\varepsilon)
and
\ell>\ell_{0}
the following is true.
Let

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\left\{{\tau\in\{\pm 1\}^{\boldsymbol{U}_{\delta}}:\left|{\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{v})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{\tau})}}\right\rangle\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{w})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{\tau})}}\right\rangle-\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{v})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}^{(\ell)}}}\right\rangle\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{w})},{\mu^{(\ell)}_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}}}\right\rangle}\right|<\varepsilon}\right\}

and

\displaystyle\mathfrak{S}_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\left\{{\sum_{\tau\in\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}}\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}(\mathcal{S}_{\tau})>1-\varepsilon}\right\}.

Then
{\mathbb{P}}\left[{\mathfrak{S}_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}}\right]\geq 1-\varepsilon-o(1).

Proof.

Given
\varepsilon>0
choose
\xi=\xi(d,\beta,\varepsilon)>0
and
\delta_{0}=\delta_{0}(d,\beta,\varepsilon,\xi)>0
small enough and
\ell_{0}=\ell_{0}(d,\beta,\varepsilon,\xi)>0
large enough and assume that
0<\delta<\delta_{0}
and
\ell>\ell_{0}
.
Let
\mathbb{1}_{\boldsymbol{U}_{\delta}}\in\{\pm 1\}^{\boldsymbol{U}_{\delta}}
be the all-ones vector.
Then Lemma
3.2
shows that

\displaystyle\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{v})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{\tau})}}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\leq\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{v})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{1}_{\boldsymbol{U}_{\delta}}})}}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\mbox{for all }\tau\in\{\pm 1\}^{\boldsymbol{U}_{\delta}}.

(5.15)

Moreover, given the event
\mathfrak{D}_{\delta,\ell}
from Lemma
5.7
, we have that the
distance between
\boldsymbol{v}
and
\boldsymbol{U}_{\delta}
is at least
\ell+1
, and therefore, Lemma
3.2
implies that

\displaystyle\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{v})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{1}_{\boldsymbol{U}_{\delta}}})}}\right\rangle\leq\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{v})},{\mu^{(\ell)}_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{v},\beta,0}}}\right\rangle.

(5.16)

Combining (
5.15
)–(
5.16
), due to the inversion symmetry of the Ising model without an external field we conclude that given
\mathfrak{D}_{\delta,\ell}
for all
\tau\in\{\pm 1\}^{\boldsymbol{U}_{\delta}}
,

\displaystyle\left|{\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{v})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{\tau})}}\right\rangle}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{v})},{\mu^{(\ell)}_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{v},\beta,0}}}\right\rangle=\mathfrak{m}_{\mathbb{G},\boldsymbol{v}\to\boldsymbol{w},\beta,0}^{(\ell)},

and analogously

(5.17)

\displaystyle\left|{\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{w})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{\tau})}}\right\rangle}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{w})},{\mu^{(\ell)}_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{w},\beta,0}}}\right\rangle=\mathfrak{m}_{\mathbb{G},\boldsymbol{w}\to\boldsymbol{v},\beta,0}^{(\ell)}.

(5.18)

Further, consider the event

\displaystyle\mathfrak{E}_{\xi}

\displaystyle=\left\{{0\leq\frac{\mathfrak{m}_{\mathbb{G},\boldsymbol{v}\to\boldsymbol{w},\beta,0}^{(\ell)}\mathfrak{m}_{\mathbb{G},\boldsymbol{w}\to\boldsymbol{v},\beta,0}^{(\ell)}+\operatorname{th}\beta}{1+\mathfrak{m}_{\mathbb{G},\boldsymbol{v}\to\boldsymbol{w},\beta,0}^{(\ell)}\mathfrak{m}_{\mathbb{G},\boldsymbol{w}\to\boldsymbol{v},\beta,0}^{(\ell)}\operatorname{th}\beta}-\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{v}\boldsymbol{w})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G},\beta,0}}}\right\rangle<\xi}\right\}.

Lemmas
5.5
,
5.6
and
5.7
imply that

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}\left[{\mathfrak{E}_{\xi}}\right]>1-\xi+o(1),

(5.19)

provided that
\ell
is sufficiently large.
Hence, with
\mathcal{T}_{\xi}
the set from Lemma
5.8
, consider

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{\xi}^{*}

\displaystyle=\left\{{\tau\in\mathcal{T}_{\xi}:\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{v}\boldsymbol{w})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{\tau})}}\right\rangle\geq\frac{\mathfrak{m}_{\mathbb{G},\boldsymbol{v}\to\boldsymbol{w},\beta,0}^{(\ell)}\mathfrak{m}_{\mathbb{G},\boldsymbol{w}\to\boldsymbol{v},\beta,0}^{(\ell)}+\operatorname{th}\beta}{1+\mathfrak{m}_{\mathbb{G},\boldsymbol{v}\to\boldsymbol{w},\beta,0}^{(\ell)}\mathfrak{m}_{\mathbb{G},\boldsymbol{w}\to\boldsymbol{v},\beta,0}^{(\ell)}\operatorname{th}\beta}-\xi^{1/2}}\right\}.

(5.20)

If the event
\mathfrak{D}_{\delta,\ell}\cap\mathfrak{E}_{\xi}\cap\mathfrak{S}_{\xi}
occurs, then by the law of total probability we have

\displaystyle\sum_{\tau\not\in\mathcal{T}^{*}_{\xi}}\mu_{\mathbb{G},\beta,0}(\mathcal{S}_{\tau})

\displaystyle<\xi^{1/4}.

(5.21)

As a direct consequence of the definition (
1.19
)–(
1.20
) of the Ising model we find

\displaystyle\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{v}\boldsymbol{w})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{\tau})}}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{e}_{m})\exp(\beta\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{e}_{m}))},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{\tau})}}\right\rangle}{\left\langle{{\exp(\beta\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{e}_{m}))},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{\tau})}}\right\rangle}=\frac{\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{e}_{m})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{\tau})}}\right\rangle+\operatorname{th}\beta}{1+\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{e}_{m})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{\tau})}}\right\rangle\operatorname{th}\beta}.

(5.22)

Furthermore, for any
\tau\in\mathcal{T}_{\xi}^{*}\subseteq\mathcal{T}_{\xi}
we have

\displaystyle\left|{\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{v}\boldsymbol{w})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{\tau})}}\right\rangle-\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{v})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{\tau})}}\right\rangle\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{w})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{\tau})}}\right\rangle}\right|<\xi.

(5.23)

Combining (
5.22
) and (
5.23
) with the definition (
5.20
) of
\mathcal{T}_{\xi}^{*}
we see that for
\tau\in\mathcal{T}_{\xi}^{*}
,

\displaystyle\frac{\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{v})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{\tau})}}\right\rangle\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{w})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{\tau})}}\right\rangle+\operatorname{th}\beta}{1+\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{v})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{\tau})}}\right\rangle\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{w})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{\tau})}}\right\rangle\operatorname{th}\beta}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\mathfrak{m}_{\mathbb{G},\boldsymbol{v}\to\boldsymbol{w},\beta,0}^{(\ell)}\mathfrak{m}_{\mathbb{G},\boldsymbol{w}\to\boldsymbol{v},\beta,0}^{(\ell)}+\operatorname{th}\beta}{1+\mathfrak{m}_{\mathbb{G},\boldsymbol{v}\to\boldsymbol{w},\beta,0}^{(\ell)}\mathfrak{m}_{\mathbb{G},\boldsymbol{w}\to\boldsymbol{v},\beta,0}^{(\ell)}\operatorname{th}\beta}-\xi^{1/3},

(5.24)

provided that
\xi>0
is chosen sufficiently small.

Since the function
z\in[-1,1]\mapsto(z+\operatorname{th}\beta)/(1+z\operatorname{th}\beta)
is differentiable and strictly increasing, (
5.24
) implies that on the event
\mathfrak{D}_{\delta,\ell}\cap\mathfrak{E}_{\xi}\cap\mathfrak{S}_{\xi}
for
\tau\in\mathcal{T}_{\xi}^{*}
we have

\displaystyle\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{v})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{\tau})}}\right\rangle\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{w})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{\tau})}}\right\rangle

\displaystyle>\mathfrak{m}_{\mathbb{G},\boldsymbol{v}\to\boldsymbol{w},\beta,0}^{(\ell)}\mathfrak{m}_{\mathbb{G},\boldsymbol{w}\to\boldsymbol{v},\beta,0}^{(\ell)}-\xi^{1/4}

\displaystyle=\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{v})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}^{(\ell)}}}\right\rangle\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{w})},{\mu^{(\ell)}_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}}}\right\rangle-\xi^{1/4},

(5.25)

provided
\xi>0
is chosen small enough.
Thus, combining (
5.17
)–(
5.18
) and (
5.25
), we conclude that on the event
\mathfrak{D}_{\delta,\ell}\cap\mathfrak{E}_{\xi}\cap\mathfrak{S}_{\xi}
,

\displaystyle\left|{\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{v})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{\tau})}}\right\rangle\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{w})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{\tau})}}\right\rangle-\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{v})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}^{(\ell)}}}\right\rangle\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{w})},{\mu^{(\ell)}_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}}}\right\rangle}\right|<\varepsilon

\displaystyle\mbox{for all }\tau\in\mathcal{T}_{\xi}^{*}.

(5.26)

Finally, (
5.26
) shows that
\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\supseteq\mathcal{T}_{\xi}^{*}
and (
5.21
) shows that
\mathfrak{S}_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\supseteq\mathfrak{D}_{\delta,\ell}\cap\mathfrak{E}_{\xi}\cap\mathfrak{S}_{\xi}
.
Therefore, (
5.19
), Lemma
5.7
and Lemma
5.8
show that

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}\left[{\mathfrak{S}_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}}\right]\geq{\mathbb{P}}\left[{\mathfrak{D}_{\delta,\ell}\cap\mathfrak{E}_{\xi}\cap\mathfrak{S}_{\xi}}\right]>1-3\xi+o(1)>1-\varepsilon+o(1),

as desired.
∎

As a preparation for the next step we point out that the empirical distribution of the magnetisations with an all-ones boundary condition at distance
\ell
is close to the distribution
\pi_{d,\beta,0}^{(\ell)}
obtained after
\ell
iterations of the BP operator from (
2.5
).

Fact 5.10
.

For
\ell\geq 0
the distribution

\displaystyle\pi_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}^{(\ell)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v_{i})},{\mu^{(\ell)}_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},v_{i},\beta,0}}}\right\rangle}

(5.27)

satisfies
{\mathbb{E}}[W_{1}(\pi_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}^{(\ell)},\pi_{d,\beta,0}^{(\ell)})]=o(1)
.

Proof.

This is an immediate consequence of Lemma
2.2
and Corollary
2.4
.
∎

While Lemma
5.9
provides information about the products

\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{v})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{\tau})}}\right\rangle\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{w})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{\tau})}}\right\rangle
, what we are really after is the single magnetisations
\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{v})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{\tau})}}\right\rangle
.
The following lemma combines Lemma
5.9
and Fact
5.10
to at least extract the absolute values
|\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{v})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{\tau})}}\right\rangle|
.

Lemma 5.11
.

Assume that
d>1
and
\beta\in(\beta_{\mathrm{f}}(d),\infty)\setminus\mathfrak{X}_{d}
.
For any
\varepsilon>0
there exist
\delta_{0}=\delta_{0}(d,\beta,\varepsilon)>0
,
\ell_{0}=\ell_{0}(d,\beta,\varepsilon)
such that for
0<\delta<\delta_{0}(\varepsilon)
and
\ell>\ell_{0}
the following is true.
Let

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle=\left\{{\tau\in\{\pm 1\}^{\boldsymbol{U}_{\delta}}:\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{v})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}^{(\ell)}}}\right\rangle-\varepsilon\leq\left|{\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{v})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{\tau})}}\right\rangle}\right|\leq\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{v})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}^{(\ell)}}}\right\rangle}\right\}

and

\displaystyle\mathfrak{S}_{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle=\left\{{\sum_{\tau\in\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}}\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}(\mathcal{S}_{\tau})>1-\varepsilon}\right\}.

Then
{\mathbb{P}}\left[{\mathfrak{S}_{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}}\right]\geq 1-\varepsilon-o(1).

Proof.

Given
\varepsilon>0
choose
\xi=\xi(d,\beta,\varepsilon)>0
and
\delta=\delta_{0}(d,\beta,\varepsilon,\xi)>0
sufficiently small and
\ell_{0}=\ell_{0}(d,\beta,\varepsilon,\xi)>0
sufficiently large so that in the notation from Lemma
5.9
we have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}\left[{{\mathbb{P}}\left[{\mathfrak{S}_{\xi}^{\prime}\mid\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{U}_{\delta}}\right]>1-\xi^{1/2}}\right]>1-\xi^{1/2}-o(1).

(5.28)

Additionally, let
\mathfrak{W}_{\xi}
be the event that the distribution
\pi_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}^{(\ell)}
from (
5.27
) satisfies
W_{1}(\pi_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}^{(\ell)},\pi_{d,\beta,0})<\xi
.
Since (
1.3
) ensures that

\displaystyle\lim_{\ell\to\infty}W_{1}(\pi_{d,\beta,0}^{(\ell)},\pi_{d,\beta,0})=0,

Fact
5.10
shows that for large enough
\ell
we have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}\left[{\mathfrak{W}_{\xi}}\right]=1-o(1).

(5.29)

Since
d>1
and
\beta>\beta_{\mathrm{f}}(d)
, Proposition
2.14
(ii) shows that there exists a number
\eta=\eta(d,\beta)>0
that depends on
d,\beta
only such that
\pi_{d,\beta,0}([2\eta,\infty])>2\eta
.
Let

\displaystyle\mathfrak{B}_{\eta}

\displaystyle=\left\{{\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{w})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}^{(\ell)}}}\right\rangle>\eta}\right\}.

Because given
\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{U}_{\delta}
the vertex
\boldsymbol{w}
is within total variation distance
o(1)
of a uniformly random element of
V_{n}
, we conclude that on the event
\mathfrak{W}_{\xi}
,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}\left[{\mathfrak{B}_{\eta}\mid\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{U}_{\delta}}\right]

\displaystyle>\eta,

(5.30)

provided
\xi>0
is chosen small enough.

Now consider the event

\displaystyle\mathfrak{V}_{\xi}=\left\{{{\mathbb{P}}[\mathfrak{S}_{\xi}^{\prime}\mid\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{U}_{\delta}]>1-\xi^{1/2}}\right\}\cap\mathfrak{W}_{\xi}.

Then (
5.28
) and (
5.29
) imply that

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}\left[{\mathfrak{V}_{\xi}}\right]>1-\xi^{1/2}-o(1).

(5.31)

Furthermore, on the event
\mathfrak{V}_{\xi}
we have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}[\mathfrak{S}_{\xi}^{\prime}\cap\mathfrak{B}_{\eta}\mid\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{U}_{\delta}]>1-\xi^{1/2}/\eta-o(1)>1-\xi^{1/3}-o(1)

(5.32)

if
\xi>0
is chosen sufficiently small (which is possible because
\eta=\eta(d,\beta)
depends on
d,\beta
only).

Finally, if
\mathfrak{S}_{\xi}^{\prime}\cap\mathfrak{B}_{\eta}
occurs, then for any
\tau\in\mathcal{T}_{\xi}^{\prime}
we have

\displaystyle\left|{\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{v})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{\tau})}}\right\rangle}\right|\geq\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{v})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}^{(\ell)}}}\right\rangle-\frac{\xi}{\eta}>\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{v})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}^{(\ell)}}}\right\rangle-\varepsilon,

provided that we chose
\xi=\xi(d,\beta,\varepsilon)>0
suitably small.
Moreover, (
5.17
) readily yields the upper bound

\displaystyle\left|{\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{v})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{\tau})}}\right\rangle}\right|\leq\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{v})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}^{(\ell)}}\rangle}.

Hence, if
\mathfrak{S}_{\xi}^{\prime}\cap\mathfrak{B}_{\eta}
occurs, then
\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}\supseteq\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}
.
Therefore, the assertion follows from (
5.31
) and (
5.32
).
∎

The next step is to establish consistency of the signs of the magnetisations.
In other words, while Lemma
5.11
determines
\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{v})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{\tau})}}\right\rangle
essentially up to a sign
s_{\boldsymbol{v},\tau}
, we are now going to show that
s_{\boldsymbol{v},\tau}
actually only depends on
\tau
.
In other words, the ‘pure states’
\mathcal{S}_{\tau}
split into ‘positive’ ones where
s_{\boldsymbol{v},\tau}=1
and negative ones where
s_{\boldsymbol{v},\tau}=-1
with high probability over the choice of
\boldsymbol{v}
.
In effect, in the cut metric most pure states are close to either a ‘positive’ product measure with magnetisations at least zero, or a negative one with magnetisations less than or equal to zero.

Lemma 5.12
.

Assume that
d>1
and
\beta\in(\beta_{\mathrm{f}}(d),\infty)\setminus\mathfrak{X}_{d}
.
For any
\varepsilon>0
there exist
\delta_{0}=\delta_{0}(d,\beta,\varepsilon)>0
,
\ell_{0}=\ell_{0}(d,\beta,\varepsilon)
such that for
0<\delta<\delta_{0}(\varepsilon)
and
\ell>\ell_{0}
the following is true.
Consider the product measures

\displaystyle\bar{\mu}_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}^{+}(\sigma)

\displaystyle=\prod_{v\in V_{n}}\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}^{(\ell)}(\{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v)=\sigma(v)\}),

\displaystyle\bar{\mu}_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}^{-}(\sigma)

\displaystyle=\prod_{v\in V_{n}}\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}^{(\ell)}(\{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v)=-\sigma(v)\})

and let

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime\prime}

\displaystyle=\left\{{\tau\in\{\pm 1\}^{\boldsymbol{U}_{\delta}}:\min\left\{{\Delta_{\Box}(\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{\tau}),\bar{\mu}^{+}_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}),\Delta_{\Box}(\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{\tau}),\bar{\mu}^{-}_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0})}\right\}<\varepsilon}\right\}

and

\displaystyle\mathfrak{S}_{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime\prime}

\displaystyle=\left\{{\sum_{\tau\in\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime\prime}}\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}(\mathcal{S}_{\tau})>1-\varepsilon}\right\}.

Then
{\mathbb{P}}\left[{\mathfrak{S}_{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime\prime}}\right]\geq 1-\varepsilon-o(1).

Proof.

Given
\varepsilon>0
choose
\zeta=\zeta(d,\beta,\varepsilon),\xi=\xi(d,\beta,\varepsilon,\zeta),\delta_{0}(d,\beta,\varepsilon,\zeta,\xi)>0
sufficiently small and
\ell_{0}=\ell_{0}(d,\beta,\varepsilon,\zeta,\xi)>0
sufficiently large and assume that
0<\delta<\delta_{0}
and
\ell>\ell_{0}
.
With
\pi_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}^{(\ell)}
from (
5.27
) let

\displaystyle\mathfrak{W}_{\xi}

\displaystyle=\left\{{W_{1}(\pi_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}^{(\ell)},\pi_{d,\beta,0})<\xi}\right\}.

Then Lemmas
5.8
,
5.9
,
5.10
and
5.11
demonstrate that the event

\displaystyle\mathfrak{V}_{\xi}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{W}_{\xi}\cap\left\{{{\mathbb{P}}[\mathfrak{S}_{\xi}^{\prime}\cap\mathfrak{S}_{\xi}^{\prime\prime}\mid\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{U}_{\delta}]>1-3\sqrt{\xi}}\right\}

has probability

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}[\mathfrak{V}_{\xi}]>1-4\sqrt{\xi}+o(1).

(5.33)

Since
\boldsymbol{v}
is within total variation distance
o(1)
of a uniformly random vertex from
V_{n}
we conclude that on
\mathfrak{V}_{\xi}
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}

\displaystyle\sum_{v\in V_{n}}\sum_{\tau\in\{\pm 1\}^{\boldsymbol{U}_{\delta}}}\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}(\mathcal{S}_{\tau})\left|{\left|{\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v)},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{\tau})}}\right\rangle}\right|-\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v)},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}^{(\ell)}}}\right\rangle}\right|\leq{\mathbb{E}}\left[{\sum_{\tau\not\in\mathcal{T}_{\xi}^{\prime\prime}}\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}(\mathcal{S}_{\tau})\mid\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{U}_{\delta}}\right]+\xi+o(1)

\displaystyle\leq 1-{\mathbb{P}}\left[{\mathfrak{S}^{\prime\prime}_{\xi}\mid\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{U}_{\delta}}\right]+2\xi+o(1)<4\sqrt{\xi}+o(1).

(5.34)

Similarly, since
(\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{w})
is within total variation distance
o(1)
of a uniformly random vertex pair, we see that on
\mathfrak{V}_{\xi}
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n^{2}}

\displaystyle\sum_{\tau\in\{\pm 1\}^{\boldsymbol{U}_{\delta}}}\sum_{v,w\in V_{n}}\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}(\mathcal{S}_{\tau})\left|{\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v)},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{\tau})}}\right\rangle\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(w)},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{\tau})}}\right\rangle-\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v)},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}^{(\ell)}}}\right\rangle\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(w)},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}^{(\ell)}}}\right\rangle}\right|

\displaystyle\leq 1-{\mathbb{P}}\left[{\mathfrak{S}_{\xi}^{\prime}\mid\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{U}_{\delta}}\right]+2\xi+o(1)<4\sqrt{\xi}+o(1).

(5.35)

Combining (
5.34
)–(
5.35
), we see that on
\mathfrak{V}_{\xi}
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{\tau\in\{\pm 1\}^{\boldsymbol{U}_{\delta}}}\sum_{v,w\in V_{n}}\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}(\mathcal{S}_{\tau})\left|{\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v)\boldsymbol{\sigma}(w)},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{\tau})}}\right\rangle-\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v)},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{\tau})}}\right\rangle\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(w)},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{\tau})}}\right\rangle}\right|<\xi^{1/3}+o(1),

provided
\xi
is chosen small enough.
Hence, letting

\displaystyle\Theta

\displaystyle=\left\{{\tau\in\{\pm 1\}^{\boldsymbol{U}_{\delta}}:\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{\tau})\mbox{ is $\xi^{1/4}$-symmetric}}\right\},

\displaystyle\Theta^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\left\{{\tau\in\{\pm 1\}^{\boldsymbol{U}_{\delta}}:\sum_{v,w\in V_{n}}\left|{\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v)},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{\tau})}}\right\rangle\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(w)},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{\tau})}}\right\rangle-\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v)},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}^{(\ell)}}}\right\rangle\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(w)},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}^{(\ell)}}}\right\rangle}\right|<\xi^{1/4}n^{2}}\right\},

\displaystyle\Theta^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle=\left\{{\tau\in\{\pm 1\}^{\boldsymbol{U}_{\delta}}:\sum_{v\in V_{n}}\left|{\left|{\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v)},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{\tau})}}\right\rangle}\right|-\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v)},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}^{(\ell)}}}\right\rangle}\right|<\xi^{1/4}n}\right\},

we learn from (
5.34
)–(
5.35
) that on the event
\mathfrak{V}_{\xi}
for small enough
\xi>0
and large enough
n
we have

\displaystyle\sum_{\tau\in\Theta\cap\Theta^{\prime}\cap\Theta^{\prime\prime}}\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}(\mathcal{S}_{\tau})>1-\xi^{1/4}.

(5.36)

Now, for
\tau\in\Theta^{\prime}\cap\Theta^{\prime\prime}
consider

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{\tau}^{+}

\displaystyle=\left\{{v\in V_{n}:\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v)},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{\tau})}}\right\rangle\geq\zeta}\right\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{\tau}^{-}

\displaystyle=\left\{{v\in V_{n}:\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v)},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{\tau})}}\right\rangle\leq-\zeta}\right\}.

Let
\Theta^{\#}=\{\tau\in\Theta^{\prime}\cap\Theta^{\prime\prime}:\min\{|\mathcal{V}_{\tau}^{+}|,|\mathcal{V}_{\tau}^{-}|\}\geq\zeta n\}
.
Thus,
\Theta^{\#}
contains those
\tau\in\Theta^{\prime}\cap\Theta^{\prime\prime}
where given
\mathcal{S}_{\tau}
there are ‘many’ vertices
v
with a substantially positive magnetisation, as well as many
v
with a substantially negative magnetisation.
We are going to argue that this is not possible, i.e.,
\Theta^{\#}=\emptyset
.
Indeed, for
\tau\in\Theta^{\#}
we have

\displaystyle\sum_{v,w\in V_{n}}

\displaystyle\left|{\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v)},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{\tau})}}\right\rangle\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(w)},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{\tau})}}\right\rangle-\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v)},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}^{(\ell)}}}\right\rangle\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(w)},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}^{(\ell)}}}\right\rangle}\right|

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{v\in\mathcal{V}_{\tau}^{+},\,w\in\mathcal{V}_{\tau}^{-}}\zeta^{2}-\left|{\left|{\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v)},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{\tau})}}\right\rangle\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(w)},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{\tau})}}\right\rangle}\right|-\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v)},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}^{(\ell)}}}\right\rangle\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(w)},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}^{(\ell)}}}\right\rangle}\right|

\displaystyle\geq\zeta^{4}n^{2}-\xi^{1/5}n^{2}>\xi^{1/4}n^{2},

(5.37)

provided that
\xi=\xi(\zeta)>0
is chosen small enough.
Hence, comparing (
5.37
) with the definition of
\Theta^{\prime}\supseteq\Theta^{\#}
, we conclude that
\Theta^{\#}=\emptyset
.

Next we are going to argue that on
\mathfrak{V}_{\xi}
the set
\Theta^{0}=\left\{{\tau\in\Theta^{\prime}\cap\Theta^{\prime\prime}:\max\{|\mathcal{V}_{\tau}^{+}|,|\mathcal{V}_{\tau}^{-}|\}\leq\zeta n}\right\}
of
\tau
such that given
\mathcal{S}_{\tau}
there are very few substantially positive
or
negative magnetisations is empty as well.
Indeed, by Proposition
2.14
for
d>1
and
\beta>\beta_{\mathrm{f}}(d)
there exists
\eta=\eta(d,\beta)>0
such that
\pi_{d,\beta,0}([\eta,1])>\eta
.
Because the sequence
(\pi_{d,\beta,0}^{(l)})_{l}
converges to
\pi_{d,\beta,0}
in
W_{1}
, we see that
\pi_{d,\beta,0}^{(\ell)}([\eta/2,1])>\eta/2
for large enough
\ell
.
Hence, on the event
\mathfrak{W}_{\xi}\supseteq\mathfrak{V}_{\xi}
we have

\displaystyle\sum_{v\in V_{n}}\mathbb{1}\left\{{\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v)},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}^{(\ell)}}}\right\rangle\geq\eta/3}\right\}\geq\eta n/3.

(5.38)

But for
\tau\in\Theta^{0}
we find

\displaystyle\sum_{v\in V_{n}}\left|{\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v)},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{\tau})}}\right\rangle}\right|\leq 3\zeta n.

(5.39)

Thus, combining (
5.38
)–(
5.39
) and assuming that
\zeta,\xi
are chosen suitably small, we obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{v\in V_{n}}\left|{\left|{\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v)},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{\tau})}}\right\rangle}\right|-\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v)},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}^{(\ell)}}}\right\rangle}\right|\geq(\eta/3-3\zeta)n>\xi^{1/4}n,

in contradiction to the fact that
\tau\in\Theta^{\prime\prime}
.
Hence,
\Theta^{0}=\emptyset
.

Since
\Theta^{0}\cup\Theta^{\#}=\emptyset
on
\mathfrak{V}_{\xi}
, we conclude that for any
\tau\in\Theta\cap\Theta^{\prime}\cap\Theta^{\prime\prime}
there exists
s_{\tau}\in\{\pm 1\}
such that

\displaystyle\sum_{v\in V_{n}}\left|{\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v)},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{\tau})}}\right\rangle-s_{\tau}\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v)},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}^{(\ell)}}}\right\rangle}\right|<2\zeta n.

(5.40)

Let

\displaystyle\Theta^{+}

\displaystyle=\left\{{\tau\in\Theta\cap\Theta^{\prime}\cap\Theta^{\prime\prime}:s_{\tau}=1}\right\},

\displaystyle\Theta^{-}

\displaystyle=\Theta\cap\Theta^{\prime}\cap\Theta^{\prime\prime}\setminus\Theta^{+}.

For each
\tau\in\Theta^{+}\subseteq\Theta
the conditional distribution
\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{\tau})
is
\xi^{1/8}
-symmetric.
Therefore, (
5.40
) implies together with Lemmas
3.6
and
3.7
that
\Delta_{\Box}(\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{\tau}),\bar{\mu}_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}^{+})<\varepsilon
.
Analogously, we have
\Delta_{\Box}(\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{\tau}),\bar{\mu}_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}^{-})<\varepsilon
for
\tau\in\Theta^{-}
.
Therefore, the assertion follows from (
5.33
) and (
5.36
).
∎

Proof of Proposition
2.15
.

Let
\varepsilon>0
and pick
\gamma=\gamma(d,\beta,\varepsilon)
,
\zeta=\zeta(d,\beta,\gamma)>0
and
\xi=\xi(d,\beta,\zeta)>0
small enough and
\ell=\ell(d,\xi)>0
sufficiently large.
Then by Fact
5.10
and Lemma
5.12
the events
\mathfrak{S}_{\xi}^{\prime\prime\prime}
and
\mathfrak{W}_{\xi,\ell}=\{W_{1}(\pi_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}^{(\ell)},\pi_{d,\beta,0})<\xi\}
occur with probability

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}\left[{\mathfrak{S}_{\xi}^{\prime\prime\prime}\cap\mathfrak{W}_{\xi}}\right]>1-2\xi+o(1).

(5.41)

Suppose that
\mathfrak{S}_{\xi}^{\prime\prime\prime}\cap\mathfrak{W}_{\xi}
occurs and let

\displaystyle\Theta^{+}

\displaystyle=\left\{{\tau\in\{\pm 1\}^{\boldsymbol{U}_{\delta}}:\Delta_{\Box}(\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{\tau}),\bar{\mu}^{+}_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0})<\xi}\right\},

\displaystyle\Theta^{-}

\displaystyle=\left\{{\tau\in\{\pm 1\}^{\boldsymbol{U}_{\delta}}:\Delta_{\Box}(\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{\tau}),\bar{\mu}^{-}_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0})<\xi}\right\}.

In addition, for
\sigma\in\{\pm 1\}^{V_{n}}
let
X(\sigma)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{v\in V_{n}}\sigma(v)
and define
\lambda_{d,\beta}=\int_{0}^{1}z{\mathrm{d}}\pi_{d,\beta,0}(z)
.
Then Lemma
3.7
and the triangle inequality yield

\displaystyle\left|{\left\langle{{X(\boldsymbol{\sigma})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{\tau})}}\right\rangle-\lambda_{d,\beta}}\right|

\displaystyle=\left|{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{v\in V_{n}}\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v)},{\mu_{\mathbb{G},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{\tau})}}\right\rangle-\lambda_{d,\beta}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq 8\Delta_{\Box}(\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{\tau}),\bar{\mu}_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0})+W_{1}(\pi_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}^{(\ell)},\pi_{d,\beta,0})<9\xi.

(5.42)

Furthermore, provided
\xi
is sufficiently small, Lemmas
3.6
and
3.7
show that the measure
\mu_{\mathbb{G},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{\tau})
is
\zeta
-symmetric.
Hence, (
5.42
) yields

\displaystyle\left\langle{{X(\boldsymbol{\sigma})^{2}},{\mu_{\mathbb{G},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{\tau})}}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{v,w\in V_{n}}\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v)\boldsymbol{\sigma}(w)},{\mu_{\mathbb{G},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{\tau})}}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\leq 2\zeta+\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{v,w\in V_{n}}\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v)},{\mu_{\mathbb{G},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{\tau})}}\right\rangle\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(w)},{\mu_{\mathbb{G},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{\tau})}}\right\rangle<2\zeta+9\xi+\lambda_{d,\beta}^{2}.

(5.43)

Combining (
5.42
) and (
5.43
) with Chebyshev’s inequality, we conclude that

\displaystyle\mu_{\mathbb{G},\beta,0}\left({\left\{{\left|{X(\boldsymbol{\sigma})-\lambda_{d,\beta}}\right|>\zeta^{1/4}}\right\}\mid\mathcal{S}_{\tau}}\right)<\zeta^{1/4}

\displaystyle\mbox{for all }\tau\in\Theta^{+}\mbox{ and analogously}

(5.44)

\displaystyle\mu_{\mathbb{G},\beta,0}\left({\left\{{\left|{X(\boldsymbol{\sigma})+\lambda_{d,\beta}}\right|>\zeta^{1/4}}\right\}\mid\mathcal{S}_{\tau}}\right)<\zeta^{1/4}

\displaystyle\mbox{for all }\tau\in\Theta^{-}.

(5.45)

Since
\lambda_{d,\beta}>0
by Proposition
2.14
, (
5.44
)–(
5.45
) imply that on the event
\mathfrak{S}^{\prime\prime\prime}_{\xi}\cap\mathfrak{W}_{\xi}
,

\displaystyle\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}(\{\pm 1\}^{V_{n}}\setminus(\mathcal{S}_{+}\cup\mathcal{S}_{-}))<1-\zeta^{1/4}-\xi<1-2\zeta^{1/4},

(5.46)

provided that
\zeta,\xi
are chosen suitably small.
In combination with the inherent symmetry of the Ising model with
B=0
, (
5.46
) shows that on the event
\mathfrak{S}^{\prime\prime\prime}_{\xi}\cap\mathfrak{W}_{\xi}
,

\displaystyle\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}(\mathcal{S}_{+})=\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}(\mathcal{S}_{-})>\frac{1}{2}-2\zeta^{1/4}.

(5.47)

Further, let

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}^{+}

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{\tau\in\Theta^{+}}\mathcal{S}_{\tau},

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}^{-}

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{\tau\in\Theta^{-}}\mathcal{S}_{\tau}.

Then (
5.44
) and (
5.45
) imply that on the event
\mathfrak{S}^{\prime\prime\prime}_{\xi}\cap\mathfrak{W}_{\xi}
,

\displaystyle\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}(\mathcal{S}_{+}\triangle\mathcal{S}^{+}),\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}(\mathcal{S}_{-}\triangle\mathcal{S}^{-})

\displaystyle<8\zeta^{1/4}.

(5.48)

Consequently, by the definition of the cut metric we have

\displaystyle\Delta_{\Box}(\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{+}),\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}^{+}))

\displaystyle<\gamma,

(5.49)

provided that
\zeta=\zeta(\gamma)>0
is chosen small enough.
In addition, the conditional distributions
\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{\tau})
for
\tau\in\Theta^{+}
satisfy

\displaystyle\Delta_{\Box}(\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}^{+}),\bar{\mu}_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}^{+})

\displaystyle<\xi.

(5.50)

Combining (
5.49
)–(
5.50
) and applying the triangle inequality, we find
\Delta_{\Box}(\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{+}),\bar{\mu}_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}^{+})<2\gamma
.
Therefore, Lemmas
3.6
and
3.7
show that on the event
\mathfrak{S}^{\prime\prime\prime}_{\xi}\cap\mathfrak{W}_{\xi}
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{v,w\in V_{n}}\left|{\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v)\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v)},{\mu_{\mathbb{G},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{+})}}\right\rangle-\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v)},{\mu_{\mathbb{G},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{+})}}\right\rangle\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(w)},{\mu_{\mathbb{G},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{+})}}\right\rangle}\right|

\displaystyle<\varepsilon.

(5.51)

Moreover, (
5.50
) and Lemma
3.7
show that on
\mathfrak{S}^{\prime\prime\prime}_{\xi}\cap\mathfrak{W}_{\xi}
,

\displaystyle W_{1}(\boldsymbol{\pi}^{+}_{n,m,\beta},\pi_{d,\beta,0})<\varepsilon.

(5.52)

Similarly, since
\Delta_{\Box}(\mu_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{+}),\bar{\mu}_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime},\beta,0}^{+})<2\gamma
,
Lemma
3.7
shows that on
\mathfrak{S}^{\prime\prime\prime}_{\xi}\cap\mathfrak{W}_{\xi}
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{v\in V_{n}}\left|{\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v)},{\mu_{\mathbb{G},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{+})}}\right\rangle-\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v)},{\mu^{(\ell)}_{\mathbb{G},v,\beta,0}}}\right\rangle}\right|<\varepsilon.

(5.53)

Finally, the proposition follows from (
5.41
), (
5.47
), (
5.51
), (
5.52
) and (
5.53
) upon taking
\varepsilon\to 0
slowly enough as
n\to\infty
.
∎

5.3.
Proof of Proposition
2.16

The steps of the proof are broadly similar to those that we pursued towards the proof of Proposition
2.10
.
It is just that we have to take into account the conditioning on
\mathcal{S}_{+}
in the definition (
2.22
) of
\boldsymbol{\pi}_{n,m,\beta,M}^{\oplus}
.

Lemma 5.13
.

If
d,\beta>0
and
0\leq M\leq m
, then

\displaystyle\limsup_{\ell\to\infty}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\sum_{h=1,2}{\mathbb{E}}\left|{\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v_{1})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}_{h,M},\beta,B}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{+})}}\right\rangle-\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v_{1})},{\mu^{(\ell)}_{\mathbb{G}_{h,M},v_{1},\beta,B}}}\right\rangle}\right|

\displaystyle=0.

Proof.

Since each graph
\mathbb{G}_{h,M}
separately is distributed as the random graph
\mathbb{G}(n,m-1)
, the assertion follows from Proposition
2.15
(iii) and the union bound.
∎

Proof of Proposition
2.16
.

Letting

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\pi}_{n,M,\beta,0}^{\oplus\,(\ell)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{\left({\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v_{i})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}_{1,M},v_{i},\beta,0}^{(\ell)}}}\right\rangle,\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(v_{i})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}_{2,M},v_{i},\beta,0}^{(\ell)}}}\right\rangle}\right)},

we obtain from Lemma
5.13
that

\displaystyle\limsup_{\ell\to\infty}\limsup_{n\to\infty}W_{1}\left({\boldsymbol{\pi}_{n,M,\beta}^{\oplus\,(\ell)},\boldsymbol{\pi}_{n,M,\beta}^{\oplus}}\right)

\displaystyle=0.

(5.54)

Furthermore, the local weak convergence from Lemma
2.11
implies together with Lemma
4.4
that for any
\ell\geq 0
,

\displaystyle W_{1}\left({\boldsymbol{\pi}_{n,M,\beta}^{\oplus\,(\ell)},\pi_{d,\beta,0,t}^{\otimes\,(\ell)}}\right)

\displaystyle=o(1).

(5.55)

Finally, the assertion follows from (
5.54
), (
5.55
) and Corollary
4.5
.
∎

5.4.
Proof of Proposition
2.17

As in Section
4.8
we let
\mathfrak{G}_{n,M}
be the
\sigma
-algebra generated by
(\mathbb{G}_{h,M})_{h=1,2}
and we set
\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{n,M}=\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{+}_{n,M}+\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{-}_{n,M}-2\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\pm}_{n,M}
.
The principal difference between the present proof and the proof of Proposition
2.12
is that here we need to take the conditioning on
\mathcal{S}_{+}
and the inversion symmetry of the Ising model with
B=0
into account.

Lemma 5.14
.

Uniformly for all
0\leq M\leq m
we have

{\mathbb{E}}\left[{\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{n,M}\mid\mathfrak{G}_{n,M}}\right]=\mathcal{B}_{\beta}^{\otimes}(\pi^{\otimes}_{d,\beta,0,M/m})-\mathcal{B}_{\beta}^{\otimes}(\pi^{\otimes}_{d,\beta,0,0})+o(1)
w.h.p.

We pursue a similar strategy as towards the proof of Lemma
4.9
.
Let
\boldsymbol{v}_{M},\boldsymbol{w}_{M}
be the vertices of the final ‘shared’ edge
\boldsymbol{e}_{M}
and let
\boldsymbol{v}_{h,M},\boldsymbol{w}_{h,M}
be the vertices of
\boldsymbol{e}_{h,M,m-M}
.

Claim 5.15
.

Uniformly for all
0\leq M\leq m
w.h.p. we have

\displaystyle\log\frac{Z_{\mathbb{G}_{h,M}^{+}}(\beta,0)}{Z_{\mathbb{G}_{h,M}}(\beta,0)}

\displaystyle=\log\operatorname{ch}\beta+\log\left({1+\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{v}_{M})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}_{h,M}}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{+})}}\right\rangle\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{w}_{M})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}_{h,M}}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{+})}}\right\rangle\operatorname{th}\beta}\right)+o(1),

(5.56)

\displaystyle\log\frac{Z_{\mathbb{G}_{h,M}^{-}}(\beta,0)}{Z_{\mathbb{G}_{h,M}}(\beta,0)}

\displaystyle=\log\operatorname{ch}\beta+\log\left({1+\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{v}_{h,M})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}_{h,M}}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{+})}}\right\rangle\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{w}_{h,M})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}_{h,M}}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{+})}}\right\rangle\operatorname{th}\beta}\right)+o(1).

(5.57)

Proof.

Lemma
2.8
demonstrates that with probability
1-O(1/n)
,

\displaystyle\log\frac{Z_{\mathbb{G}_{h,M}^{+}}(\beta,B)}{Z_{\mathbb{G}_{h,M}}(\beta,B)}

\displaystyle=\log\left\langle{{\exp\left({\beta\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{e}_{M})}\right)},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}_{h,M},\beta,B}}}\right\rangle=\log\operatorname{ch}\beta+\log\left({1+\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{v}_{M})\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{w}_{M})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}_{h,M}}}}\right\rangle\operatorname{th}\beta}\right).

(5.58)

By construction, each individual graph
\mathbb{G}_{h,M}
is distributed as a random graph
\mathbb{G}(n,m-1)
.
Therefore, by Proposition
2.15
(i) and the union bound, w.h.p. we have
\mu_{\mathbb{G}_{h,M},\beta,0}(\{\pm 1\}^{V_{n}}\setminus(\mathcal{S}_{+}\cup\mathcal{S}_{-}))=o(1)
.
Therefore, the inversion symmetry of the Ising model in the case
B=0
implies that w.h.p.

\displaystyle\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{v}_{M})\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{w}_{M})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}_{h,M},\beta,0}}}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{v}_{M})\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{w}_{M})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}_{h,M},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{+})}}\right\rangle+o(1).

Further, since
\boldsymbol{v}_{M},\boldsymbol{w}_{M}
are asymptotically uniform and independent, Proposition
2.15
(ii) ensures that w.h.p.

\displaystyle\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{v}_{1,M})\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{v}_{2,M})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}_{h,M},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{+})}}\right\rangle=\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{v}_{1,M})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}_{h,M},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{+})}}\right\rangle\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{v}_{2,M})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}_{h,M},\beta,0}(\,\cdot\,\mid\mathcal{S}_{+})}}\right\rangle+o(1).

(5.59)

Combining (
5.58
) and (
5.59
), we obtain (
5.56
).
A similar argument yields (
5.57
).
∎

Let
\boldsymbol{\Xi}^{+}_{t},\boldsymbol{\Xi}^{-}_{t},\boldsymbol{\Xi}_{t}^{\pm}
be the expressions from (
4.33
)–(
4.35
) with
B=0
and set
\boldsymbol{\Xi}_{t}=\boldsymbol{\Xi}^{+}_{t}+\boldsymbol{\Xi}^{-}_{t}-2\boldsymbol{\Xi}^{\pm}_{t}
.
Moreover, let
\boldsymbol{\delta}_{n,M}^{+}
be the distribution of
\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{n,M}^{+}
given
\mathfrak{G}_{n,M}
and define
\boldsymbol{\delta}_{n,M}^{-}
,
\boldsymbol{\delta}_{n,M}^{\pm}
analogously w.r.t.
\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{n,M}^{-}
,
\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{n,M}^{\pm}
.
Let
\xi^{+}_{t}
,
\xi_{t}^{-}
,
\xi_{t}^{\pm}
be the distributions of
\Xi^{+}_{t},\Xi^{-}_{t},\Xi^{\pm}_{t}
.

Claim 5.16
.

For all
0\leq M\leq m
we have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\left[{W_{1}(\boldsymbol{\delta}_{n,M}^{+},\xi_{M/m}^{+})+W_{1}(\boldsymbol{\delta}_{n,M}^{-},\xi_{M/m}^{-})+W_{1}(\boldsymbol{\delta}_{n,M}^{\pm},\xi_{M/m}^{\pm})}\right]

\displaystyle=o(1).

(5.60)

Proof.

Since the vertex pair
(\boldsymbol{v}_{M},\boldsymbol{w}_{M})
is asymptotically uniform, Proposition
2.16
shows that w.h.p. given
\mathfrak{G}_{n,M}
the pairs

\displaystyle\left({\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{v}_{M})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}_{h,M}}}}\right\rangle}\right)_{h=1,2},

\displaystyle\left({\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{w}_{M})},{\mu_{\mathbb{G}_{h,M}}}}\right\rangle}\right)_{h=1,2}

converge in
W_{1}
to
(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\pi_{d,\beta,B,M/m}^{\otimes},1,h}^{(0)})_{h=1,2}
and
(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\pi_{d,\beta,B,M/m}^{\otimes},2,h}^{(0)})_{h=1,2}
.
Hence, (
5.56
) yields
W_{1}(\boldsymbol{\delta}_{n,M}^{+},\xi_{M/m}^{+})=o(1)
w.h.p. The bounds on
W_{1}(\boldsymbol{\delta}_{n,M}^{-},\xi_{M/m}^{-})
and
W_{1}(\boldsymbol{\delta}_{n,M}^{\pm},\xi_{M/m}^{\pm})
follow analogously.
∎

Proof of Lemma
5.14
.

Claims
5.15
and
5.16
imply that w.h.p.

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\left[{\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{n,M}\mid\mathfrak{G}_{n,M}}\right]

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\left[{\boldsymbol{\Xi}_{M/m}}\right]+o(1).

(5.61)

Furthermore, retracing the steps that we performed towards Eqs. (
4.38
) and (
4.39
), we find

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\left[{\boldsymbol{\Xi}_{M/m}^{+}}\right]

\displaystyle=\log^{2}\operatorname{ch}\beta+2{\mathbb{E}}\left[{\log\left({1+\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\pi_{d,\beta,t}^{\otimes},1,1}^{(0)}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\pi_{d,\beta,t}^{\otimes},2,1}^{(0)}\operatorname{th}\beta}\right)}\right]\log\operatorname{ch}\beta+\mathcal{B}_{\beta}^{\otimes}(\pi_{d,\beta,B,M/m}^{\otimes}),

(5.62)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\left[{\boldsymbol{\Xi}_{M/m}^{-}}\right]

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\left[{\boldsymbol{\Xi}_{M/m}^{\pm}}\right]=\log^{2}\operatorname{ch}\beta+2{\mathbb{E}}\left[{\log\left({1+\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\pi_{d,\beta,t}^{\otimes},1,1}^{(0)}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\pi_{d,\beta,t}^{\otimes},2,1}^{(0)}\operatorname{th}\beta}\right)}\right]\log\operatorname{ch}\beta+\mathcal{B}_{\beta}^{\otimes}(\pi_{d,\beta,B,0}^{\otimes}).

(5.63)

Finally, the assertion follows from (
5.61
)–(
5.63
).
∎

Corollary 5.17
.

We have
\mathrm{Var}\sum_{0\leq M<m}{\mathbb{E}}\left[{\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{n,M}\mid\mathfrak{F}_{n,M}}\right]=o(m^{2})
.

Proof.

This is identical to the proof of Corollary
4.12
, except that we invoke Lemma
5.14
rather than Lemma
4.9
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
2.17
.

Once again the argument is identical to the proof of Proposition
2.12
, except that we invoke Lemma
5.14
and Corollary
5.17
instead of Lemma
4.9
and Corollary
4.12
.
∎

5.5.
Proof of Proposition
2.18

As in the proof of Proposition
2.13
, we start from the observation that the limiting distribution
\pi_{d,\beta,0}
fails to concentrate on a single point.

Lemma 5.18
.

Assume that
d>1
,
\beta>\beta_{\mathrm{f}}(d)
.
Then
\pi_{d,\beta,0}
is not supported on a single point.

Proof.

This is a direct consequence of Proposition
2.14
(ii).
∎

Lemma 5.19
.

If
d>1
and
\beta>\beta_{\mathrm{f}}(d)
, then
\mathcal{B}^{\otimes}_{\beta}(\pi^{\otimes}_{d,\beta,B,t})\geq\mathcal{B}^{\otimes}_{\beta}(\pi^{\otimes}_{d,\beta,B,0})
for all
0\leq t\leq 1
.

Proof.

Let
\varepsilon>0
.
Lemmas
2.7
and
5.14
imply that for sufficiently large
n
for all
0\leq M\leq m
we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{\beta}^{\otimes}(\pi^{\otimes}_{d,\beta,B,M/m})-\mathcal{B}_{\beta}^{\otimes}(\pi^{\otimes}_{d,\beta,B,0})\geq-\varepsilon.

(5.64)

Finally, the continuity of
t\in[0,1]\mapsto\mathcal{B}_{\beta}^{\otimes}(\pi^{\otimes}_{d,\beta,B,t})
,
which follows from Proposition
2.9
, implies together with (
5.64
) that
\mathcal{B}^{\otimes}_{\beta}(\pi^{\otimes}_{d,\beta,B,t})-\mathcal{B}^{\otimes}_{\beta}(\pi^{\otimes}_{d,\beta,B,0})\geq 0
for all
t\in[0,1]
.
∎

Lemma 5.20
.

If
d>1
and
\beta>\beta_{\mathrm{f}}(d)
, then

\mathcal{B}^{\otimes}_{\beta}(\pi^{\otimes}_{d,\beta,B,1})>\mathcal{B}^{\otimes}_{\beta}(\pi^{\otimes}_{d,\beta,B,0})
.

Proof.

Since (
4.50
) holds for
B=0
, the assertion follows from Lemma
5.18
and Jensen’s inequality.
∎

Proof of Proposition
2.13
.

Since
t\mapsto\mathcal{B}^{\otimes}_{\beta}(\pi^{\otimes}_{d,\beta,B,t})
is continuous
by Proposition
2.9
, the assertion follows from Lemmas
5.18
–
5.20
.
∎

6.
Proof of Theorem
1.2

Throughout this section we assume that
d\operatorname{th}\beta<1
.

6.1.
Proof of Proposition
2.19

Let
\omega=\omega(n)=\sqrt{n}\log n
and let
\mathfrak{B}=\mathfrak{B}_{n}=\left\{{\alpha\in\mathbb{Z}:|\alpha-n/2|\leq\omega}\right\}
.

Lemma 6.1
.

We have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\left[{Z_{\mathbb{G}}(\beta,0)}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\alpha=0}^{n}\sum_{M=0}^{m}\binom{n}{\alpha}\exp(\beta(2M-m))\binom{\binom{\alpha}{2}+\binom{n-\alpha}{2}}{M}\binom{\alpha(n-\alpha)}{m-M}\binom{\binom{n}{2}}{m}^{-1},

(6.1)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\left[{Z_{\mathbb{G},\mathrm{bal}}(\beta,0)}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathfrak{B}}\sum_{M=0}^{m}\binom{n}{\alpha}\exp(\beta(2M-m))\binom{\binom{\alpha}{2}+\binom{n-\alpha}{2}}{M}\binom{\alpha(n-\alpha)}{m-M}\binom{\binom{n}{2}}{m}^{-1}.

(6.2)

Proof.

We begin with the formula for the mean of
Z_{\mathbb{G}}(\beta,0)
.
By the linearity of expectation,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}[Z_{\mathbb{G}}(\beta,0)]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\sigma\in\{\pm 1\}^{V_{n}}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[{\exp\left({\beta\sum_{e\in E(\mathbb{G})}\sigma(e)}\right)}\right].

(6.3)

Since the distribution of the random graph is invariant under permutations of the vertex set, the contribution of a configuration
\sigma
to (
6.3
) depends only on the number of vertices
v\in V_{n}
with
\sigma(v)=1
.
Indeed, for a given
0\leq\alpha\leq n
there are a total of
\binom{n}{\alpha}
configurations
\sigma
such that
\sigma(v)=1
for precisely
\alpha
vertices
v\in V_{n}
.
Let
\sigma^{(\alpha)}
be the configuration with
\sigma(v_{1})=\cdots=\sigma(v_{\alpha})=1
and
\sigma(v_{\alpha+1})=\cdots=\sigma(v_{n})=-1
.
Then we can rewrite (
6.3
) as

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}[Z_{\mathbb{G}}(\beta,0)]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\alpha=0}^{n}\binom{n}{\alpha}{\mathbb{E}}\left[{\exp\left({\beta\sum_{e\in E(\mathbb{G})}\sigma^{(\alpha)}(e)}\right)}\right].

(6.4)

Further, the term
\sum_{e\in E(\mathbb{G})}\sigma^{(\alpha)}(e)
simply equals the number of edges
e=vw
with
\sigma^{(\alpha)}(v)=\sigma^{(\alpha)}(w)
minus the number of edges with
\sigma^{(\alpha)}(v)\neq\sigma^{(\alpha)}(w)
.
Now, given
\alpha
there are precisely
\binom{\alpha}{2}+\binom{(n-\alpha)}{2}
potential edges
e=vw
with
\sigma^{(\alpha)}(v)=\sigma^{(\alpha)}(w)
, and
a(n-\alpha)
potential edges with
\sigma^{(\alpha)}(v)\neq\sigma^{(\alpha)}(w)
.
Hence, the probability that the random graph contains precisely
M
edges
e=vw
with
\sigma^{(\alpha)}(v)=\sigma^{(\alpha)}(w)
equals

\displaystyle\binom{\binom{\alpha}{2}+\binom{n-\alpha}{2}}{M}\binom{\alpha(n-\alpha)}{m-M}\binom{\binom{n}{2}}{m}^{-1}.

(6.5)

Combining (
6.4
) and (
6.5
), we obtain the expression for (
6.1
).
The derivation of (
6.2
) is similar, except that we only sum on configurations
\sigma
with
|\sum_{v\in V_{n}}\sigma(v)|\leq 2\sqrt{n}\log n
.
∎

Let
h(z)=-z\log z-(1-z)\log(1-z)
.

Lemma 6.2
.

We have
{\mathbb{E}}\left[{Z_{\mathbb{G}}(\beta,0)}\right]\sim{\mathbb{E}}\left[{Z_{\mathbb{G},\mathrm{bal}}(\beta,0)}\right].

Proof.

Applying Stirling’s formula, we obtain for
\alpha\in\mathfrak{B}

\displaystyle\binom{\binom{\alpha}{2}+\binom{n-\alpha}{2}}{M}\binom{\alpha(n-\alpha)}{m-M}\binom{\binom{n}{2}}{m}^{-1}

\displaystyle\sim\left({\frac{\alpha(n-\alpha)}{\binom{n}{2}}}\right)^{m}\binom{m}{M}\left({\frac{{\alpha\choose 2}+{n-\alpha\choose 2}}{\alpha(n-\alpha)}}\right)^{M}\exp\left({-{2M^{2}\over n^{2}}-{2(m-M)^{2}\over n^{2}}+{m^{2}\over n^{2}}}\right).

(6.6)

Alternatively for
\alpha\notin\mathfrak{B}
we have the following upper bound.

\displaystyle\binom{\binom{\alpha}{2}+\binom{n-\alpha}{2}}{M}\binom{\alpha(n-\alpha)}{m-M}\binom{\binom{n}{2}}{m}^{-1}

\displaystyle=O(1)\cdot\left({\frac{\alpha(n-\alpha)}{\binom{n}{2}}}\right)^{m}\binom{m}{M}\left({\frac{{\alpha\choose 2}+{n-\alpha\choose 2}}{\alpha(n-\alpha)}}\right)^{M}\exp\left({-{2M^{2}\over n^{2}}-{2(m-M)^{2}\over n^{2}}+{m^{2}\over n^{2}}}\right).

(6.7)

Since the final exponential term is bounded (i.e,
\Theta(1)
) in both (
6.6
) and (
6.7
), Lemma
6.1
and (
6.6
)–(
6.7
) yield

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}[Z_{\mathbb{G}}(\beta,0)]

\displaystyle=c_{1}\cdot\sum_{\alpha\in\mathfrak{B}}\binom{n}{\alpha}\left({\frac{\alpha(n-\alpha)}{\exp(\beta)\binom{n}{2}}}\right)^{m}\sum_{M=0}^{m}\binom{m}{M}\exp(2\beta M))\left({\frac{{\alpha\choose 2}+{n-\alpha\choose 2}}{\alpha(n-\alpha)}}\right)^{M}

\displaystyle+O(1)\cdot\sum_{\alpha\notin\mathfrak{B}}\binom{n}{\alpha}\left({\frac{\alpha(n-\alpha)}{\exp(\beta)\binom{n}{2}}}\right)^{m}\sum_{M=0}^{m}\binom{m}{M}\exp(2\beta M))\left({\frac{{\alpha\choose 2}+{n-\alpha\choose 2}}{\alpha(n-\alpha)}}\right)^{M},

(6.8)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}[Z_{\mathbb{G},\mathrm{bal}}(\beta,0)]

\displaystyle=c_{1}\cdot\sum_{\alpha\in\mathfrak{B}}\binom{n}{\alpha}\left({\frac{\alpha(n-\alpha)}{\exp(\beta)\binom{n}{2}}}\right)^{m}\sum_{M=0}^{m}\binom{m}{M}\exp(2\beta M))\left({\frac{{\alpha\choose 2}+{n-\alpha\choose 2}}{\alpha(n-\alpha)}}\right)^{M},

(6.9)

where
c_{1}=c_{1}(d,\beta)=\Theta(1)
.

Further, by the binomial theorem

\displaystyle\left({\frac{\alpha(n-\alpha)}{\exp(\beta)\binom{n}{2}}}\right)^{m}\sum_{M=0}^{m}\binom{m}{M}\exp(2\beta M))\left({\frac{{\alpha\choose 2}+{n-\alpha\choose 2}}{\alpha(n-\alpha)}}\right)^{M}

\displaystyle=\left({\frac{\alpha(n-\alpha)}{\binom{n}{2}}\exp(-\beta)+\frac{\binom{\alpha}{2}+\binom{n-\alpha}{2}}{\binom{n}{2}}\exp(\beta)}\right)^{m}

\displaystyle=\Theta(1)\left({2\frac{\alpha}{n}\left({1-\frac{\alpha}{n}}\right)\exp(-\beta)+\left({1-2\frac{\alpha}{n}+2\left({\frac{\alpha}{n}}\right)^{2}}\right)\exp(\beta)}\right)^{m}.

(6.10)

Approximating
\binom{n}{\alpha}
by Stirling’s formula and setting
z=\alpha/n
, we obtain from (
6.8
)–(
6.10
) the estimates

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}[Z_{\mathbb{G}}(\beta,0)]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathfrak{B}}\exp(nh(z)+c_{2})\left({2z\left({1-z}\right)\exp(-\beta)+\left({1-2z+2z^{2}}\right)\exp(\beta)}\right)^{m}

\displaystyle+\sum_{\alpha\notin\mathfrak{B}}\exp(nh(z)+O\left({\log(n)}\right))\left({2z\left({1-z}\right)\exp(-\beta)+\left({1-2z+2z^{2}}\right)\exp(\beta)}\right)^{m},

(6.11)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}[Z_{\mathbb{G},\mathrm{bal}}(\beta,0)]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathfrak{B}}\exp(nh(z)+c_{2})\left({2z\left({1-z}\right)\exp(-\beta)+\left({1-2z+2z^{2}}\right)\exp(\beta)}\right)^{m},

(6.12)

where
c_{2}=c_{2}(n,d,\beta)=O(\log n)
.
We proceed to investigate the exponential parts of the above summands.
Let

\displaystyle f(z)

\displaystyle=h(z)+\frac{m}{n}\log(2z(1-z)\exp(-\beta)+(1-2z+2z^{2})\exp(\beta)).

(6.13)

The derivatives of
f(z)
work out to be

\displaystyle f^{\prime}(z)

\displaystyle=\log(1-z)-\log z+\frac{2m}{n}\cdot\frac{(2z-1)(\exp(\beta)-\exp(-\beta))}{2z(1-z)\exp(-\beta)+(1-2z+2z^{2})\exp(\beta)},

(6.14)

\displaystyle f^{\prime\prime}(z)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{1-z}-\frac{1}{z}+\frac{4m}{n}\cdot\frac{\exp(\beta)-\exp(-\beta)}{2z(1-z)\exp(-\beta)+(1-2z+2z^{2})\exp(\beta)}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\ -\frac{4m}{n}\cdot\left({\frac{(2z-1)(\exp(\beta)-\exp(-\beta))}{2z(1-z)\exp(-\beta)+(1-2z+2z^{2})\exp(\beta)}}\right)^{2}.

(6.15)

Hence,
f^{\prime}(1/2)=0
and
f^{\prime\prime}(z)<0
for all
z\in(0,1)
if
d\operatorname{th}\beta<1
.
Consequently, (
6.12
) yields

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}[Z_{\mathbb{G},\mathrm{bal}}(\beta,0)]

\displaystyle\geq\exp(nf(1/2)+c_{2})=\exp\left({n\log 2+\frac{m}{n}\log\operatorname{ch}\beta+O(\log n)}\right),

(6.16)

while for
\alpha\not\in\mathfrak{B}
we find

\displaystyle\exp(nf(z))

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left({nf(1/2)-n\Omega((z-1/2)^{2})+O(\log n)}\right)=o\left({{\mathbb{E}}[Z_{\mathbb{G},\mathrm{bal}}(\beta,0)]/n}\right);

(6.17)

to obtain the last inequality we used the choice of
\omega=\sqrt{n}\log n
.
Finally, the assertion follows from (
6.11
), (
6.12
), (
6.16
) and (
6.17
).
∎

Lemma 6.3
.

Let

\displaystyle g(z)

\displaystyle=h(z)+\frac{m}{n}\log\left({z(1-z)+{\exp(2\beta)\over 2}(z^{2}+(1-z)^{2})}\right),

\displaystyle\lambda

\displaystyle=\sqrt{2\over\pi n}\,\left({\frac{2n}{\exp(\beta)(n-1)}}\right)^{m}\exp\left({-{m^{2}\over n^{2}}\operatorname{th}^{2}\beta-{m\over n}\cdot{2\exp(2\beta)\over 1+\exp(2\beta)}}\right).

Then

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\left[{Z_{\mathbb{G},\mathrm{bal}}(\beta,0)}\right]\sim\lambda\sum_{\alpha\in\mathfrak{B}}^{n}\exp(ng(\alpha/n)).

(6.18)

Proof.

Combining (
6.6
) with Lemma
6.1
, we obtain

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\left[{Z_{\mathbb{G},\mathrm{bal}}(\beta,0)}\right]

\displaystyle\sim\sum_{\alpha\in\mathfrak{B}}\binom{n}{\alpha}\left({\frac{\alpha(n-\alpha)}{\exp(\beta)\binom{n}{2}}}\right)^{m}\sum_{M=0}^{m}\binom{m}{M}\left({\frac{{\alpha\choose 2}+{n-\alpha\choose 2}}{\alpha(n-\alpha)}}\right)^{M}\exp\left({2\beta M-{2M^{2}\over n^{2}}-{2(m-M)^{2}\over n^{2}}+{m^{2}\over n^{2}}}\right).

(6.19)

For a given
\alpha
the function
M\mapsto\binom{m}{M}\left({\frac{{\alpha\choose 2}+{n-\alpha\choose 2}}{\alpha(n-\alpha)}}\right)^{M}\exp\left({2\beta M-{2M^{2}\over n^{2}}-{2(m-M)^{2}\over n^{2}}+{m^{2}\over n^{2}}}\right)
attains its maximum value at

\displaystyle M_{*}(\alpha)=m\left({1+\frac{\exp(-2\beta)\alpha(n-\alpha)}{\binom{\alpha}{2}+\binom{n-\alpha}{2}}}\right)^{-1}.

In particular, values
M\sim M_{*}(\alpha)
dominate the inner sum (on
M
) in (
6.19
).
Consequently, since for
\alpha\in\mathfrak{B}
we obtain
M\sim M_{*}(\alpha)\sim{m\over 1+\exp(-2\beta)}
, by the binomial theorem the expression (
6.19
) simplifies to

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\left[{Z_{\mathbb{G},\mathrm{bal}}(\beta,0)}\right]

\displaystyle\sim\left({\frac{2n}{\exp(\beta)(n-1)}}\right)^{m}\exp\left({-{m^{2}\over n^{2}}\operatorname{th}^{2}\beta}\right)\sum_{\alpha\in\mathfrak{B}}{n\choose\alpha}\left({{\alpha\over n}\left({1-{\alpha\over n}}\right)}\right)^{m}\left({1+\exp(2\beta){{\alpha\choose 2}+{n-\alpha\choose 2}\over\alpha(n-\alpha)}}\right)^{m}.

As a next step we apply Stirling’s formula to the binomial coefficient
\binom{n}{\alpha}
for
\alpha\in\mathfrak{B}
, obtaining

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\left[{Z_{\mathbb{G},\mathrm{bal}}(\beta,0)}\right]

\displaystyle\sim\sqrt{2\over\pi n}\left({\frac{2n}{\exp(\beta)(n-1)}}\right)^{m}\exp\left({-{m^{2}\over n^{2}}\operatorname{th}^{2}\beta}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\sum_{\alpha\in\mathfrak{B}}\exp\left({nh\left({\alpha\over n}\right)+m\left[{\log{\alpha\over n}+\log\left({1-{\alpha\over n}}\right)+\log\left({1+\exp(2\beta){{\alpha^{2}}+(n-\alpha)^{2}\over 2\alpha(n-\alpha)}-{2\exp(2\beta)\over n}}\right)}\right]}\right).

(6.20)

Expanding the final log-term in (
6.20
) as

\displaystyle\log\left({1+\exp(2\beta)\cdot{{\alpha^{2}}+(n-\alpha)^{2}\over 2\alpha(n-\alpha)}-{2\exp(2\beta)\over n}}\right)

\displaystyle=\log\left({1+\exp(2\beta)\cdot{{\alpha^{2}}+(n-\alpha)^{2}\over 2\alpha(n-\alpha)}}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad-{2\exp(2\beta)\over n}\left({1+\exp(2\beta)\cdot{{\alpha^{2}}+(n-\alpha)^{2}\over 2\alpha(n-\alpha)}}\right)^{-1}+O(1/n^{2})

finally completes the derivation of (
6.18
).
∎

We proceed to evaluate the sum
\sum_{\alpha\in\mathfrak{B}}^{n}\exp(ng(\alpha/n))
via the Laplace method.

Lemma 6.4
.

We have

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha\in\mathfrak{B}}^{n}\exp(ng(\alpha/n))

\displaystyle\sim\sqrt{\frac{\pi n}{2(1-d\operatorname{th}\beta)}}\exp\left({ng(1/2)}\right).

Proof.

Comparing
g(z)
with the function
f(z)
from (
6.13
), we discover that
g(z)=f(z)+\frac{m}{n}(\beta-\log 2)
.
Therefore, (
6.14
)–(
6.15
) demonstrate that
g(z)
is concave and attains its unique maximum at
z=1/2
.
As a consequence, we can approximate the sum
\sum_{\alpha\in\mathfrak{B}}^{n}\exp(ng(\alpha/n))
by an integral over
B=[\min\mathfrak{B},\max\mathfrak{B}]
, which gives

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha\in\mathfrak{B}}\exp(ng(\alpha/n))

\displaystyle\sim\int_{B}\exp(ng(\alpha/n)){\mathrm{d}}\alpha\sim\exp\left({ng(1/2)}\right)\int_{B}\exp\left({{g^{\prime\prime}(1/2)\over 2n}\alpha^{2}}\right){\mathrm{d}}\alpha

\displaystyle\sim\sqrt{-\frac{n}{g^{\prime\prime}(1/2)}}\exp\left({ng(1/2)}\right)\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\exp\left({-\frac{z^{2}}{2}}\right){\mathrm{d}}z=\sqrt{-\frac{2\pi n}{g^{\prime\prime}(1/2)}}\exp\left({ng(1/2)}\right).

(6.21)

Since
g^{\prime\prime}(1/2)=f^{\prime\prime}(1/2)=4(d\operatorname{th}\beta-1)
by (
6.15
), the assertion follows from (
6.21
).
∎

Proof of Proposition
2.19
.

The fact that
{\mathbb{E}}[Z_{\mathbb{G}}(\beta,0)]\sim{\mathbb{E}}[Z_{\mathbb{G},\mathrm{bal}}(\beta,0)]
follows from Lemma
6.2
.
Moreover, since

\displaystyle g(1/2)=\log 2+\frac{m}{n}\log\frac{1+\exp(2\beta)}{4},

the asymptotic formula for
{\mathbb{E}}[Z_{\mathbb{G},\mathrm{bal}}(\beta,0)]
follows from Lemmas
6.3
and
6.4
and a bit of algebra.
∎

6.2.
Proof of Proposition
2.20

We prove Proposition
2.20
by way of an auxiliary random graph model that we call the
planted Ising model
.
Let
\mathfrak{B}=\left\{{\sigma\in\{\pm 1\}^{V_{n}}:|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma(v_{i})|\leq 2\sqrt{n}\log n}\right\}
be the set of all balanced configurations, i.e., configurations that are counted by
Z_{\mathbb{G},\mathrm{bal}}(\beta)
as defined in (
2.26
).
For
\sigma\in\mathfrak{B}
we define a random graph
\mathbb{G}^{*}(\sigma)=\mathbb{G}^{*}_{n,m}(\sigma,\beta)
by letting

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}\left[{\mathbb{G}^{*}(\sigma)=G}\right]

\displaystyle\propto\exp\left({\beta\sum_{e\in E(G)}\sigma(e)}\right){\mathbb{P}}\left[{\mathbb{G}=G}\right].

(6.22)

To be precise, the
\propto
-symbol in (
6.22
) hides the normalising factor

\displaystyle Z^{*}(\sigma,\beta)

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\left[{\exp\left({\beta\sum_{e\in E(\mathbb{G})}\sigma(e)}\right)}\right]

\displaystyle=\binom{\binom{n}{2}}{m}^{-1}\sum_{m_{+}=0}^{m}\binom{\binom{|\sigma^{-1}(1)|}{2}+\binom{|\sigma^{-1}(-1)|}{2}}{m_{+}}\binom{|\sigma^{-1}(1)|\cdot|\sigma^{-1}(-1)|}{m-m_{+}}\exp(\beta(2m_{+}-m))

(6.23)

Additionally, we define a random balanced configuration
\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*}\in\mathfrak{B}
by letting

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}\left[{\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*}=\sigma}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{1}\left\{{\sigma\in\mathfrak{B}}\right\}\frac{{\mathbb{E}}\left[{\exp\left({\beta\sum_{e\in E(\mathbb{G})}\sigma(e)}\right)}\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}\left[{Z_{\mathbb{G},\mathrm{bal}}(\beta)}\right]}.

(6.24)

Finally, our main object of interest in this section is the random graph
\mathbb{G}^{*}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*})
obtained by first choosing
\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*}
according to (
6.24
) and then choosing a random graph from the resulting planted distribution (
6.22
).
The following lemma explains our interest in this object.

Lemma 6.5
.

For any random variable
Y
we have

\displaystyle\frac{{\mathbb{E}}\left[{Z_{\mathbb{G},\mathrm{bal}}(\beta)Y(\mathbb{G})}\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}[Z_{\mathbb{G},\mathrm{bal}}(\beta)]}

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}[Y(\mathbb{G}^{*}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*}))].

Proof.

By the definitions (
6.24
) and (
6.22
) of
\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*}
and
\mathbb{G}^{*}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*})
we have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}[Y(\mathbb{G}^{*}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*}))]{\mathbb{E}}[Z_{\mathbb{G},\mathrm{bal}}(\beta)]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathfrak{B}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[{\exp\left({\beta\sum_{e\in E(\mathbb{G})}\sigma(e)}\right)}\right]{\mathbb{E}}[Y(\mathbb{G}^{*}(\sigma))]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathfrak{B}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[{\exp\left({\beta\sum_{e\in E(\mathbb{G})}\sigma(e)}\right)Y(\mathbb{G})}\right]={\mathbb{E}}[Z_{\mathbb{G},\mathrm{bal}}(\beta)Y(\mathbb{G})],

as claimed.
∎

As a next step we are going to investigate the energy contribution of the planted configuration
\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*}
in the planted graph
\mathbb{G}^{*}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*})
.

Lemma 6.6
.

With probability
1-\exp(-\Omega(\sqrt{n}))
we have

\displaystyle\left|{\frac{m\exp(2\beta)}{2+2\exp(2\beta)}-\sum_{vw\in E(\mathbb{G}^{*}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*}))}\mathbb{1}\{\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*}(v)=\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*}(w)=1\}}\right|

\displaystyle=O\left({n^{2/3}}\right),

\displaystyle\left|{\frac{m\exp(2\beta)}{2+2\exp(2\beta)}-\sum_{vw\in E(\mathbb{G}^{*}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*}))}\mathbb{1}\{\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*}(v)=\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*}(w)=-1\}}\right|

\displaystyle=O\left({n^{2/3}}\right).

Proof.

Let
\sigma\in\mathfrak{B}
.
Using Stirling’s formula, we approximate the expression (
6.23
) as

\displaystyle Z^{*}(\sigma,\beta)

\displaystyle=\binom{\binom{n}{2}}{m}^{-1}\sum_{m_{+}+m_{-}\leq m}{m\choose m_{+},m_{-}}{\binom{|\sigma^{-1}(1)|}{2}\choose m_{+}}{\binom{|\sigma^{-1}(-1)|}{2}\choose m_{-}}\binom{|\sigma^{-1}(1)|\cdot|\sigma^{-1}(-1)|}{m-m_{+}-m_{-}}\exp(\beta(2(m_{+}+m_{-})-m))

\displaystyle=\frac{\Theta(n^{-1/2})}{\exp(\beta m)}\sum_{m_{+}+m_{-}\leq m}\binom{m}{m_{+},m_{-}}\left({\frac{|\sigma^{-1}(1)|}{n}}\right)^{2m_{+}}\left({\frac{|\sigma^{-1}(-1)|}{n}}\right)^{2m_{-}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad\quad\cdot\left({\frac{2|\sigma^{-1}(1)|\cdot|\sigma^{-1}(-1)|}{n^{2}}}\right)^{m-m_{+}-m_{-}}\exp(2\beta(m_{+}+m_{-}))

\displaystyle=\exp(O(\omega))2^{-m}\exp(-\beta m)\sum_{m_{+}+m_{-}\leq m}\binom{m}{m_{+},m_{-}}\exp((2\beta-\log 2)(m_{+}+m_{-})).

(6.25)

Further, let

\displaystyle f(y_{+},y_{-})

\displaystyle=-y_{+}\log y_{+}-y_{-}\log y_{-}-(1-y_{+}-y_{-})\log(1-y_{+}-y_{-})+(2\beta-\log 2)(y_{+}+y_{-}).

This function is strictly concave and its derivatives work out to be

\displaystyle\frac{\partial f}{\partial y_{+}}

\displaystyle=-\log y_{+}+\log(1-y_{+}-y_{-})+2\beta-\log 2,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial f}{\partial y_{-}}

\displaystyle=-\log y_{-}+\log(1-y_{+}-y_{-})+2\beta-\log 2.

Hence,
f
attains its unique maximum at the point

\displaystyle y_{+}^{*}

\displaystyle=y_{-}^{*}=\frac{\exp(2\beta)}{2+2\exp(2\beta)}.

(6.26)

Letting
\mathcal{M}_{0}=\{(m_{+},m_{-}):|m_{+}-m\exp(2\beta)/(2+2\exp(2\beta))|<n^{2/3},~|m_{-}-m\exp(2\beta)/(2+2\exp(2\beta))|<n^{2/3}\}
and applying Stirling’s formula once more, we to obtain

\displaystyle\exp(O(\omega))2^{-m}\exp(-\beta m)\sum_{m_{+},m_{-}\notin\mathcal{M}_{0}}\binom{m}{m_{+},m_{-}}\exp((2\beta-\log 2)(m_{+}+m_{-}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\exp(O(\omega))2^{-m}\exp(-\beta m)\sum_{m_{+},m_{-}\notin\mathcal{M}_{0}}\exp(mf(m_{+}/m,m_{-}/m))

\displaystyle\leq\exp(-\Theta(n^{2/3}))2^{-m}\exp(-\beta m)\sum_{m_{+},m_{-}\notin\mathcal{M}_{0}}\exp(mf(y_{+}^{*},y_{-}^{*})).

(6.27)

From (
6.25
)–(
6.27
) we obtain the following expression.

\displaystyle Z^{*}(\sigma,\beta)

\displaystyle=(1+\exp(-\Theta(n^{2/3}))\cdot\binom{\binom{n}{2}}{m}^{-1}\sum_{m_{+},m_{-}\in\mathcal{M}_{0}}{m\choose m_{+},m_{-}}{\binom{|\sigma^{-1}(1)|}{2}\choose m_{+}}{\binom{|\sigma^{-1}(-1)|}{2}\choose m_{-}}\binom{|\sigma^{-1}(1)|\cdot|\sigma^{-1}(-1)|}{m-m_{+}-m_{-}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\cdot\exp(2\beta(m_{+}+m_{-})-\beta m).

(6.28)

Finally, the assertion follows from (
6.22
), (
6.23
) and (
6.28
).
∎

Lemma 6.7
.

For any
\ell\geq 3
we have
{\mathbb{E}}[\boldsymbol{C}_{\ell}(\mathbb{G}^{*}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*}))]\sim\frac{d^{\ell}}{2\ell}(1+\operatorname{th}^{\ell}\beta).

Proof.

Let
u_{0},\ldots,u_{\ell-1}
be any sequence of
\ell
distinct vertices.
We need to compute the probability these vertices form a cycle.
To this end, let

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{m}_{1,1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{vw\in E(\mathbb{G}^{*}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*}))\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*}}\mathbb{1}\{\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*}(v)=\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*}(w)=1\},

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{m}_{-1,-1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{vw\in E(\mathbb{G}^{*}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*}))\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*}}\mathbb{1}\{\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*}(v)=\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*}(w)=-1\},

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{m}_{1,-1}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{m}_{-1,1}=m-\boldsymbol{m}_{1,1}-\boldsymbol{m}_{-1,-1}.

Given
\boldsymbol{m}_{1,1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{m}_{-1,-1}
and
\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*}
we consider the
2\times 2
-matrix
\boldsymbol{Q}=(\boldsymbol{Q}_{st})_{s,t\in\{\pm 1\}}
with entries

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}_{1,1}

\displaystyle=\frac{\boldsymbol{m}_{1,1}}{\binom{|\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*\,-1}(1)|}{2}},

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}_{-1,-1}

\displaystyle=\frac{\boldsymbol{m}_{-1,-1}}{\binom{|\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*\,-1}(-1)|}{2}},

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}_{1,-1}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{Q}_{-1,1}=\frac{\boldsymbol{m}_{1,-1}}{|\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*\,-1}(1)\times\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*\,-1}(-1)|}.

Moreover, let

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{C}(u_{0},\ldots,u_{\ell-1})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{1}\left\{{u_{0},\ldots,u_{\ell-1}\mbox{ form a cycle in }\mathbb{G}^{*}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*})}\right\}.

Then

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\left[{\boldsymbol{C}(u_{0},\ldots,u_{\ell-1})\mid\boldsymbol{Q},\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*}}\right]

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}\boldsymbol{Q}_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*}(u_{i}),\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*}(u_{i+1\!\!\!\!\!\mod\ell})}.

Hence, letting
Q=(Q_{s,t})_{s,t\in\{\pm 1\}}
be the matrix with entries

\displaystyle Q_{1,1}

\displaystyle=Q_{-1,-1}=\frac{2d\exp(2\beta)}{1+\exp(2\beta)},

\displaystyle Q_{1,-1}

\displaystyle=Q_{-1,1}=\frac{2d}{1+\exp(2\beta)}

and applying Lemma
6.6
, we obtain

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\left[{\boldsymbol{C}(u_{0},\ldots,u_{\ell-1})}\right]

\displaystyle\sim(2n)^{-\ell}\tr(Q^{\ell}).

(6.29)

Since
Q
has eigenvalues
2d
and
2d(\exp(2\beta)-1)/(\exp(2\beta)+1)=2d\operatorname{th}\beta
, (
6.29
) implies that

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\left[{\boldsymbol{C}(u_{0},\ldots,u_{\ell-1})}\right]

\displaystyle\sim\left({\frac{d}{n}}\right)^{\ell}(1+\operatorname{th}^{\ell}\beta).

(6.30)

Finally, since every
\ell
-cycle induces
2\ell
sequences
u_{0},\ldots,u_{\ell}
that form a cycle (due to the two possible orientations of the cycle and the
\ell
choices of the starting point), the assertion follows from (
6.30
).
∎

Corollary 6.8
.

For any
\ell\geq 3
and any
k_{3},\ldots,k_{\ell}\geq 0
we have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\left[{\prod_{j=3}^{\ell}\prod_{i=1}^{k_{j}}\left({\boldsymbol{C}_{j}(\mathbb{G}^{*}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*}))-i}\right)}\right]\sim\prod_{j=3}^{\ell}\left[{\frac{d^{j}}{2j}(1+\operatorname{th}^{j}\beta)}\right]^{k_{j}}.

Proof.

The proof is based on the computation of the joint factorial moments of
\boldsymbol{C}_{j}(\mathbb{G}^{*}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*}))
.
Specifically, towards the proof of Lemma
6.7
we computed the first moment of
\boldsymbol{C}_{j}(\mathbb{G}^{*}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*}))
by calculating the probability that a specific sequence of vertices forms a cycle.
In order to calculate the joint factorial moments of
(\boldsymbol{C}_{j}(\mathbb{G}^{*}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*})))_{3\leq j\leq\ell}
, we generalise this argument by computing the probability that several sequences of vertices form cycles of given lengths simultaneously.
Since the probability that
\mathbb{G}^{*}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*})
contains a pair of bounded-length overlapping cycles is of order
O(1/n)
, we just need to take into account pairwise disjoint vertex sequences, in which case the argument is a routine extension of the proof of Lemma
6.7
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
2.20
.

Let
L\geq 3
, let
c_{3},\ldots,c_{L}\geq 0
be integers and let

\displaystyle Y(G)=\mathbb{1}\left\{{\forall 3\leq\ell\leq L:\boldsymbol{C}_{\ell}(G)=c_{\ell}}\right\}.

Then Corollary
6.8
implies that

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\left[{Y(\mathbb{G}^{*}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*}))}\right]

\displaystyle\sim\prod_{\ell=3}^{L}{\mathbb{P}}\left[{{\rm Po}\left({(1+\chi_{\ell})\vartheta_{\ell}}\right)=c_{\ell}}\right].

Hence, Lemma
6.5
shows that

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\left[{Z_{\mathbb{G},\mathrm{bal}}(\beta)Y(\mathbb{G})}\right]

\displaystyle\sim{\mathbb{E}}[Z_{\mathbb{G},\mathrm{bal}}(\beta)]\prod_{\ell=3}^{L}{\mathbb{P}}\left[{{\rm Po}\left({(1+\chi_{\ell})\vartheta_{\ell}}\right)=c_{\ell}}\right].

(6.31)

Finally, as is well known the number of cycles of lengths
3\leq\ell\leq L
in
\mathbb{G}
are asymptotically jointly Poisson with means
d^{\ell}/(2\ell)
.
Therefore, (
6.31
) implies that

\displaystyle\frac{{\mathbb{E}}\left[{Z_{\mathbb{G},\mathrm{bal}}(\beta)Y(\mathbb{G})\mid\forall 3\leq\ell\leq L:\boldsymbol{C}_{\ell}(\mathbb{G})=c_{i}}\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}[Z_{\mathbb{G},\mathrm{bal}}(\beta)]}

\displaystyle\sim\prod_{\ell=3}^{L}\frac{{\mathbb{P}}\left[{{\rm Po}\left({(1+\chi_{\ell})\vartheta_{\ell}}\right)=c_{\ell}}\right]}{{\mathbb{P}}\left[{{\rm Po}(\vartheta_{\ell})=c_{\ell}}\right]}=\prod_{\ell=3}^{L}(1+\chi_{\ell})^{c_{\ell}}\exp(-\vartheta_{\ell}\chi_{\ell}),

as desired.
∎

6.3.
Proof of Proposition
2.21

As before we let
\omega=\omega(n)=\sqrt{n}\log n
and
\mathfrak{B}=\mathfrak{B}_{n}=\left\{{\alpha\in\mathbb{Z}:|\alpha-n/2|\leq\omega}\right\}
.
Moreover, for
0\leq\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\leq n
we let
\mathfrak{R}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})=\left\{{\rho\in\{0,\ldots,n\}:\rho\leq\min\{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\}}\right\}
.
Furthermore, let

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}

\displaystyle=\{\{\sigma,\tau\}:\sigma,\tau\in\{\pm 1\}^{2}\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{1}

\displaystyle=\left\{{s\in\mathcal{S}:|s|=1}\right\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{-1}

\displaystyle=\left\{{\{(1,1),(-1,-1)\},\{(1,-1),(-1,1)\}}\right\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{0}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{S}\setminus(\mathcal{S}_{1}\cup\mathcal{S}_{-1}).

and let

\displaystyle\Psi=\left\{{(\psi_{s})_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\in\{0,\ldots,m\}^{\mathcal{S}}:\sum_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\psi_{s}=m}\right\},

Additionally, for
\psi=(\psi_{s})_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\in\Psi
let

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}(\psi)

\displaystyle=\sum_{s\in\mathcal{S}_{1}}\psi_{s}-\sum_{s\in\mathcal{S}_{-1}}\psi_{s}.

Finally, given
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\rho
let

\displaystyle\rho_{++}=\rho_{\{(1,1)\}}

\displaystyle=\rho,

\displaystyle\rho_{+-}=\rho_{\{(1,-1)\}}

\displaystyle=\alpha_{1}-\rho,

\displaystyle\rho_{-+}=\rho_{\{(-1,1)\}}

\displaystyle=\alpha_{2}-\rho,

\displaystyle\rho_{--}=\rho_{\{(-1,-1)\}}

\displaystyle=n-\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2}+\rho.

Lemma 6.9
.

We have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}[Z_{\mathbb{G},\mathrm{bal}}(\beta,0)^{2}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\in\mathfrak{B}\\
\rho\in\mathfrak{R}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})\\
\psi\in\Psi\end{subarray}}\exp\left({2\beta{\mathcal{E}}(\psi)}\right){n\choose\alpha_{1}}{\alpha_{1}\choose\rho}{n-\alpha_{1}\choose\alpha_{2}-\rho}\prod_{s\in\mathcal{S}_{1}}{{\rho_{s}\choose 2}\choose\psi_{s}}\prod_{\{s,t\}\in\mathcal{S}_{0}\cup\mathcal{S}_{-1}}{\rho_{s}\rho_{t}\choose\psi_{\left\{{s,t}\right\}}}{{n\choose 2}\choose m}^{-1}.

Proof.

By the linearity of expectation,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}[Z_{\mathbb{G},\mathrm{bal}}(\beta,0)^{2}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\sigma,\tau}\mathbb{1}\left\{{\left|{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma(v_{i})}\right|,\left|{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\tau(v_{i})}\right|\leq 2\omega}\right\}{\mathbb{E}}\exp\left({\beta\sum_{vw\in E(\mathbb{G})}\sigma\left({v}\right)\sigma\left({w}\right)+\tau\left({v}\right)\tau\left({w}\right)}\right).

(6.32)

As in the proof of Lemma
6.1
, we use the invariance of
\mathbb{G}
under vertex permutations to rearrange the above sum.
Specifically, given
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\in\mathfrak{B}
and
\rho\in\mathfrak{R}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})
let
\sigma^{(\alpha_{1})}
,
\tau^{(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\rho)}
be the configurations

\displaystyle\sigma^{(\alpha_{1})}(v_{1})=\cdots\sigma^{(\alpha_{1})}(v_{\alpha_{1}})

\displaystyle=1,

\displaystyle\sigma^{(\alpha_{1})}(v_{\alpha_{1}+1})=\cdots\sigma^{(\alpha_{1})}(v_{n})

\displaystyle=-1,

\displaystyle\tau^{(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\rho)}(v_{1})=\cdots=\tau^{(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\rho)}(v_{\rho})

\displaystyle=1,

\displaystyle\tau^{(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\rho)}(v_{\rho+1})=\cdots=\tau^{(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\rho)}(v_{\alpha_{1}})

\displaystyle=-1,

\displaystyle\tau^{(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\rho)}(v_{\alpha_{1}+1})=\cdots=\tau^{(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\rho)}(v_{\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}-\rho})

\displaystyle=1,

\displaystyle\tau^{(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\rho)}(v_{\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}-\rho+1})=\cdots=\tau^{(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\rho)}(v_{n})

\displaystyle=-1.

Thus,
\alpha_{1}
and
\alpha_{2}
account for the total number of
+1
entries in
\sigma^{(\alpha_{1})}
and
\tau^{(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\rho)}
, respectively, while
\rho
accounts for the number of vertices set to
+1
under both configurations.
Then we can rewrite (
6.32
) as

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}[Z_{\mathbb{G},\mathrm{bal}}(\beta,0)^{2}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\in\mathfrak{B}\\
\rho\in\mathfrak{R}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})\end{subarray}}\binom{n}{\alpha_{1}}\binom{\alpha_{1}}{\rho}\binom{n-\alpha_{1}}{\alpha_{2}-\rho}{\mathbb{E}}\exp\left({\beta\sum_{vw\in E(\mathbb{G})}\sigma^{(\alpha_{1})}(v)\sigma^{(\alpha_{1})}\left({w}\right)+\tau^{(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\rho)}\left({v}\right)\tau^{(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\rho)}\left({w}\right)}\right).

(6.33)

To evaluate the expectation from (
6.33
), consider
\psi\in\Psi
.
If for any
s=(s_{1},s_{2}),t=(t_{1},t_{2})\in\{\pm 1\}^{2}
the graph
\mathbb{G}
contains precisely
\psi_{\{s,t\}}
edges
e=vw
such that

\displaystyle\sigma^{(\alpha_{1})}(v)

\displaystyle=s_{1},

\displaystyle\tau^{(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\rho)}(v)

\displaystyle=s_{2},

\displaystyle\sigma^{(\alpha_{1})}(w)

\displaystyle=t_{1},

\displaystyle\tau^{(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\rho)}(w)

\displaystyle=t_{2},

then
